## 1.2. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant GlaxoSmithKline Trading Services Limited submitted on 19 May 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Lynavoy (linerixibat), through the centralised procedure falling within the Article 3(1) and point 4 of Annex of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 10 November 2022.

The applicant applied for the following indication: Lynavoy is indicated for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC).

## 1.3. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application.

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, and nonclinical and clinical data based on applicant's own tests and studies and bibliographic literature substituting/supporting certain test(s) or study(ies).

## 1.4. Information on paediatrics

Pursuant to Article 7 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included an EMA decision(s) [P/0337/2020] on the granting of a (product-specific) waiver.

## 1.5. Information on orphan market exclusivity

Lynavoy was designated as an orphan medicinal product EU/3/21/2515 on 12.11.2021 in the following condition: Treatment of primary biliary cholangitis.

Following the CHMP positive opinion on this marketing authorisation, the Committee for Orphan Medicinal Products (COMP) reviewed the designation of Lynavoy as an orphan medicinal product in the approved indication. More information on the COMP's review can be found in the orphan maintenance assessment report published under the 'Assessment history' tab on the Agency's website: Lynavoy | European Medicines Agency (EMA).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 1.5.1. Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the applicant did submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products from the start of the procedure (Iqirvo, Lyvdelzi).

## 1.6. Applicant's request(s) for consideration

## 1.6.1. Accelerated assessment request

The applicant requested accelerated assessment in accordance to Article 14 (9) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The CHMP did not agree to the applicant's request for an accelerated assessment as the product was, based on the data provided at the stage of the accelerated assessment, not considered to be of major public health interest. This was based on the following:

It cannot be disputed that linerixibat may potentially be able to address relevant aspects of the disease burden caused by the presence of pruritus in the patients with PBC. However, the presented efficacy data at face value' are currently questionable with regard to their clinical relevance (differences to placebo appear small), comprehensiveness and potentially biased analyses.

Therefore, it cannot be concluded based on the provided data that the unmet medical need can be fulfilled and whether the product will therefore be of major interest from the point of view of public health.

## 1.6.2. Conditional marketing authorisation (CMA)

NA

## 1.6.3. Marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances

NA

## 1.6.4. New active substance status

The applicant requested the active substance linerixibat contained in the above medicinal product to be considered as a new active substance, as the applicant claims that it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

## 1.6.4.1. CHMP recommendation on new active substance status

Based on the review of available data on the active substance, the CHMP considers that linerixibat is to be qualified as a new active substance in itself, as it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union. See the Appendix 1 on NAS.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

Primary biliary cholangitis (PBC) is an autoimmune hepatobiliary disorder characterized by destruction of small intrahepatic bile ducts leading to chronic and progressive cholestasis, and retention of bile acids and other toxic metabolites. If left untreated this can lead to cirrhosis and liver failure. It is a rare disease with an estimated prevalence of 22.3 per 100,000 people in EU countries [Gazda, 2021]; in North America it ranges from 31.8 per 100,000 in Canada [Yoshida, 2018] to 39.2 per 100,000 in the US [Lu, 2018]. PBC predominantly affects more females than males [Lu, 2018]. Progression is typically followed through alkaline phosphatase (ALP) and bilirubin as biomarkers [Lindor, 2019].

Cholestatic pruritus is a common symptom of PBC and is estimated to affect 55% to 89% of patients with PBC at some point during the course of their disease [Hegade, 2019a; Mayo, 2023; Veer, 2023; Gungabissoon, 2024]. Observational studies report the proportion of PBC patients with moderate to severe or clinically significant pruritus to be approximately 25% to 53% [Hegade, 2019a; Mayo, 2023; Veer, 2023; Gungabissoon, 2024].

The causal pathway of pruritus in PBC has not been fully elucidated. Potential mediators of the pathophysiology include bile acids, lysophosphatidic acid (LPA), endogenous opioids and interleukin-31 (IL-31) [Lindor, 2019; Kremer, 2024]. Bile acids have been implicated by multiple lines of evidence: they accumulate in the blood of PBC patients, with markedly higher levels in those with pruritus compared with those without [Hegade, 2019b]. Furthermore, treatments that reduce pruritus such as nasobiliary drainage or plasma-filtering procedures have also been shown to reduce serum bile acid levels. MRGPRX4 is a putative itch receptor activated by bile acids in humans; plasma bile acids are correlated with itch in cholestatic patients at a level that can activate this receptor [Yu, 2019].

LPA is a lipid-signalling molecule that is elevated in many cholestatic patients with itch. LPA injected into mice causes itch in a dose-dependent manner. Consistent with these data, activity of autotaxin, the enzyme that produces LPA, correlates with itch intensity in PBC patients with pruritus [Lindor, 2019]. Endogenous opioids are also increased in many PBC patients with pruritus (and some without pruritus). Opioids such as morphine and heroin commonly cause the side effect of pruritus, and cholestatic itch has been ameliorated by opiate antagonists [Lindor, 2019]. IL-31, a known mediator of pruritus in atopic dermatitis, has recently been shown in PBC patients to correlate with levels of pruritus [Kremer, 2024].

A treatment which reduces plasma bile acids, LPA, and IL-31 may therefore be expected to ameliorate cholestatic pruritus in patients with PBC.

Treatment options for cholestatic pruritus in patients with PBC are very limited. Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA) is considered first-line-pharmacotherapy for PBC, but it has no effect on itch [Lindor, 2019; EASL, 2017]. The peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor (PPAR) agonists, elafibranor and seladelpar, have recently been shown to improve ALP when used as second-line therapy (in combination with UDCA) in adults who have had an inadequate response to UDCA, or as monotherapy in patients unable to tolerate UDCA. Patients requiring second-line therapy account for approximately 30% of patients with PBC [Carbone, 2018]. The older PPAR agonists, fibrates, are recommended for second line treatment of PBC and have reported some benefit for itch [Lindor, 2019; EASL, 2017]. The effects of newer PPAR agonists on pruritus were inconsistent with only seladelpar, and not elafibranor, showing significant improvement (at 6 months) in pruritus based on a pruritus numerical rating scale in a subset of patients with moderate to severe pruritus at baseline [Kowdley 2023; Hirschfield, 2024]. Even if the PPAR agonists could fully address itch in the second-line population, the significant proportion of patients receiving UDCA alone would remain without adequate control of their itch. No therapies specifically for cholestatic pruritus in the broad PBC patient population are licensed EUwide. Although suggested as first-line treatment in global treatment guidelines on the basis of limited evidence, cholestyramine, a bile acid binding resin, is only approved for this indication in some European countries [EMA Reflection Paper PBC and PSC, 2023]. Its label includes warnings and precautions due to potential inhibition of absorption of fat-soluble vitamins, increased risks of bleeding, and the need for other drugs to be administered at least 1 hour before or 4 to 6 hours after cholestyramine [Questran USPI, 2024; Questran SmPC, 2023]. The drug is often reported as poorly tolerated due to palatability issues and is associated with rapid and considerable rates of discontinuation [Duncan, 1984; Zuin, 1991]. As a result, cholestyramine may be prescribed for cholestatic pruritus in patients with PBC but is often discontinued due to inadequate efficacy at the lower, tolerated, approved doses. In a recent assessment in the US, only 9% (15/170) of PBC patients with any degree of pruritus and 13% (8/63) with clinically significant pruritus were receiving cholestyramine or another bile acid sequestrant [Mayo, 2023]. Furthermore, the published evidence base for cholestyramine effects on pruritus in PBC patients is extremely limited (2 randomized clinical trials of only 2 weeks [N=8] and 2 months [N=30] duration [Duncan, 1984; Zuin, 1991] and a recent meta-analysis based on the Duncan study with 7 PBC patients found that it did not significantly improve pruritus compared with placebo [Medina-Morales, 2023]. Thus, it is agreed that there is an unmet medical need for new effective and well tolerated treatment options in the affected population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.2. Aspects of development

## Overview of previous CHMP Scientific Advice

Across multiple SA procedures (2019-2020 and subsequent EMA SA), CHMP considered the overall clinical development for linerixibat in PBC-associated pruritus acceptable in principle, while requesting focused refinements. The size of the safety database was judged potentially sufficient for MAA given disease rarity, though uncertainties remained and long-term data were emphasised. The clinical pharmacology package was viewed as limited, but no additional clinical DDI studies were requested. Dose selection of 40 mg twice daily was regarded as reasonably justified; a single, properly designed pivotal Phase III study could suffice for MAA in this rare condition and the target population (moderate-to-severe pruritus after failure of first-/second-line therapy) was endorsed. Efficacy endpoints evolved from a Week 24-point estimate to the average change over 24 weeks in itch NRS; CHMP endorsed strengthening key secondary endpoints and broader symptom assessment (e.g. PGI-S/PGI-C, PBC-40 itch domain, 5-D itch), while noting GLIMMER data suggested no ALP or UDCA effects warranting further evaluation. PRO validation plans were endorsed; ≥4 on worst itch NRS as the moderate-to-severe threshold was supported; the responder definition required more stringent criteria (e.g. very low/no itch). Safety recommendations included continued specific monitoring for GI haemorrhage and colon cancer (e.g. FOBT), and no TQT study was required. For durability, CHMP requested robust maintenance-of-effect assessment, leading to LLSAT amendments (≥12 months continuous treatment before assessment; 8-week withdrawal; analyses at 24 and 52 weeks). Pandemic-related decentralised/hybrid conduct was conditionally acceptable with clear GCP safeguards (investigator responsibilities, site/video/home visit delineation, MRN qualification); metasite populations should be analysed exploratorily. The COMP noted that orphan significant-benefit justification should rely primarily on data versus

concomitant cholestyramine, with preference studies only supportive.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3. Description of the product

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a potent and selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT. It has been developed by GSK as a twice daily oral tablet for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in patients with PBC.

Linerixibat has been designed to have poor cellular permeability, high aqueous solubility, and chemical stability within the GI contents to facilitate interaction with its target at the brush border of enterocytes and to be minimally absorbed from the GI tract with minimal systemic exposure [Wu, 2013]. It is therefore expected to have little or no systemic toxicity or liability for drug interactions.

This marketing application is for linerixibat as an oral treatment for cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary biliary cholangitis.

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as immediate release tablets containing 40 mg linerixibat as active substance.

Other ingredients are:

Tablet core: microcrystalline cellulose (E 460), croscarmellose sodium (E 468) and magnesium stearate (E 470b).

Tablet coating: hypromellose (E 464), titanium dioxide (E 171), macrogol (E 1521), iron oxide black (E 172) and iron oxide red (E 172)

The product is available in opaque, white, high-density polyethylene (HDPE) bottles with polypropylene child-resistant closures and polyethylene induction heat seal liner, containing a HDPE canister with silica gel desiccant as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The chemical name of linerixibat is 3-((((3R,5R)-3-butyl-3-ethyl-7-methoxy-1,1-dioxido-5-phenyl2,3,4,5-tetrahydrobenzo[f][1,4]thiazepin-8-yl)methyl)amino)pentanedioic acid corresponding to the molecular formula C28H38N2O7S. It has a relative molecular mass of 546.68 and the following structure:

Figure 1: active substance structure

<!-- image -->

The chemical structure of linerixibat was elucidated by a combination of IR spectroscopy, mass spectrometry, NMR spectroscopy, UV-vis spectroscopy and single crystal x-ray crystallography by which absolute stereochemical conformation was confirmed. The solid-state properties of the active substance were measured by determining melting point (DSC), dissociation constant (pKa), partition coefficient (Log P) and hygroscopicity.

The active substance is a white to almost white non-hygroscopic crystalline solid, slightly soluble to very slightly soluble in various buffers across the physiological pH range. Several process-relevant crystalline forms were identified including forms 1, 2 and 3. Several solvates were also identified. Crystalline anhydrous form 1 is the thermodynamically most stable form at room temperature. The other forms have been determined to convert to form 1 in competitive slurry experiments although the tablets contain a mixture of forms 1 and 3 (see pharmaceutical development section for further discussion).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Linerixibat exhibits stereoisomerism due to the presence of two chiral centres. One originates in a starting material whereas the other is generated selectively during the process. Enantiomeric and diastereomeric purity are controlled routinely by HPLC.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation, and process controls

Evidence of GMP compliance has been provided in the QP declaration.

Linerixibat is synthesized in four main synthesis steps followed by recrystallisation using well defined starting materials with acceptable specifications. The starting materials were defined in line with scientific advice from CHMP during development.

The second chiral centre is introduced by a diastereoselective reduction under substrate control using the existing chiral centre and a chiral sulfoxide introduced by diastereoselective oxidation under catalyst control and further purified by crystallisation as the (S)-camphorsulfonic acid salt.

Adequate in-process controls are applied during the synthesis. The specifications and control methods for intermediate products, starting materials and reagents have been presented.

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on chemistry of new active substances.

Potential and actual impurities were well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised. The manufacturing process does not utilise discrete sources of nitrosamines or nitrosating agents, however inorganic reagents used throughout the synthesis could contain trace levels of nitrite leading to nitrosamine impurities in the active substance. This is effectively mitigated by the use of a nitrosating agent scavenger in Stage 4 of the process.

The commercial manufacturing process for the active substance was developed in parallel with the clinical development program. Evolution of the synthetic route to improve the quality of the active substance was presented in sufficient detail and changes were justified.

The applicant used Quality by Design (QbD) principles to develop the manufacturing process including the use of risk assessment and prior knowledge to define critical quality attributes (CQAs) and critical process parameters. Attributes with a potential to impact safety and efficacy were defined as CQAs which include identity, linerixibat content, impurities, and residual solvents. Following initial screening and scoping experiments, multivariate experimentation (design of experiments, DoE) was used on lab scale to investigate parameter interactions and identify appropriate ranges for process parameters which are defined in design spaces for each of the five process steps. For each step, the design space comprises ranges for raw materials and process parameters such as time and temperature. All ranges have been adequately justified by the data provided. Robustness studies examined process boundaries and subsequent confirmatory studies confirmed the appropriateness of the design spaces. The potential impact of scale on the design spaces were determined using a science and risk-based approach in accordance with the principles of ICH Q8, Q9, Q10 including mechanistic models and different scale experiments with no scale-dependent factors identified. The design spaces are considered justified for use at commercial scale.

The active substance is packaged in double anti-static low-density polyethylene (LDPE) bags closed with plastic ties which complies with Commission Regulation (EU) 10/2011, as amended. The bags are placed in an outer rigid container, which provides light protection and is suitable for handling, storage, and transportation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.2.3. Specification

The active substance specification includes tests for description, identity, assay, drug-related impurities, chiral impurities content, residual solvents, water content, residue on ignition, and particle size distribution.

The active substance specifications are based on the active substance CQAs. Impurities present at higher than the qualification threshold according to ICH Q3A were qualified by toxicological and clinical studies and appropriate specifications have been set.

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and non-compendial methods appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards has been presented.

Batch analysis data 7 production scale batches of the active substance using the proposed commercial route (C6) were provided, along with additional data from 3 batches manufactured using an earlier synthetic process (C4) which are considered representative. The results are within the specifications in place at the time and consistent from batch to batch.

## 3.2.4. Stability

Stability data from 3 production scale batches each of active substance manufactured using processes C4 and C6 respectively from the proposed manufacturer stored in the intended commercial package for up to 24 months under long term conditions (30 ºC / 65% RH), for up to 24 months under intermediate conditions (30 ºC / 75% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. 24 months' data is also available refrigerated at ambient humidity. Samples were tested according to the stability indicating parameters from table 1. No significant changes were observed for assay, impurities, chiral impurities or water content, and all results complied with specification. Additionally, no changes were observed to the polymorphic form.

In addition, data were presented following short-term storage of one batch each from routes C4 and C6, under stressed conditions (50 °C/ambient humidity, 40 °C/75% RH outside the packaging, freeze/thaw temperature cycling). Photostability testing following the ICH guideline Q1B was also performed on these batches. An increase in impurities was observed at 40 °C/75% RH and in samples exposed to light. No significant changes were observed to these parameters under the other conditions. No changes were seen to the other measured parameters under any conditions.

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed supplier is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 36 months without temperature restrictions when stored protected from light in the proposed container.

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

The finished product is an immediate release film-coated tablet for twice daily oral administration. The tablets are purple, biconvex, with 'GS 3JG' debossed on one side and plain on the other side.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmaceutical development

Development of the product applied QbD principles including definition of the Quality Target Product Profile (QTPP) outlined in Table 4.

Table 4: Lynavoy QTPP

| Dosage Form and Strength                 | A 40 mg film-coated immediate release tablet to support twice daily dosing in primary biliary cholangitis (PBC) patients with cholestatic pruritus. The tablet should be of an acceptable size for patient convenience to support patient compliance in chronic dosing, as well as to allow handling by elderly patients. The product should be coloured (not white) due to polypharmacy in the patient population                                                                                                                                                        |
|------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Drug Product Quality Criteria            | The product components (active and inactive ingredients) must have the requisite functional characteristics. The dosage form must meet compendial and any other relevant quality standards, including drug product CQAs (Critical Quality Attributes) at manufacture and over the proposed shelf-life. The drug product manufacturing process needs to be robust, reproducible, and suitable for the use of linerixibat drug substance.                                                                                                                                   |
| Drug Delivery and Release Considerations | Linerixibat is highly soluble within the physiological pH range of 1.2 – 6.8, However, it is classified as a BCS Class 4 due to a minimum solubility of 0.11 mg/mL at pH 3.7. Linerixibat Tablets, 40 mg is locally acting in the lower ileum, and the drug substance has high solubility and low permeability in the gut, meaning that linerixibat is completely soluble and available when it reaches the site of action (ileal bile acid transporter, IBAT) in the lower ileum. Hence there is no concern for linerixibat bioavailability and no need for enhancement. |
| Container Closure System                 | The drug product needs to be packaged in a container closure system that provides adequate protection from moisture vapour, protection through distribution and use, and a secure (i.e. child resistant) method of storage. Tablets will be packaged in HDPE bottles. A trade (1 month; 60 count) and a sample pack (14 days; 28 count) are required.                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Stability Criteria                       | For the commercial tablet, the components of the drug product (active and inactive ingredients) and packaging materials must be physically and chemically compatible with the requisite functional characteristics to ensure appropriate stability of the drug product over the proposed shelf life, to not constrain commercialisation in worldwide markets. Not less than 2 years when stored at 30°C/65% RH or 30°C/75% RH (Climatic Zone IV conditions). The product must remain stable throughout the in-use period (up to 30 days).                                 |

The applicant assessed the impact of the input material attributes (active substance and excipients) on the finished product quality in terms of product performance, stability, manufacturability, and container closure system. The CQAs of the drug product were identified as description, identity, drugrelated impurities, a nitrosamine impurity content, linerixibat content, uniformity of dosage units, and dissolution.

The active substance is slightly soluble to very slightly soluble in various buffers across the physiological pH range and is considered a BCS class IV substance. It is highly soluble at relevant gastrointestinal (GI) pHs and exhibits minimal absorption from the GI tract. This ensures that all the released active substance is available in the GI tract and reaches the site of action in the lower ileum without the need for a targeted release formulation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A dissolution method was developed. In the initial submission, the data supporting the proposed specification limit was considered insufficient to ascertain whether any significant deviations were observed, calling into question the proposed limit and resulting in a major objection. In response, the applicant provided data including individual unit % dissolved for each tested unit at each measured timepoint, the number of units tested per batch and timepoint. The CHMP considered the method and proposed specification acceptable, and the major objection was resolved.

Linerixibat is produced as crystalline form 1 but undergoes a partial conversion to form 3, mainly during the compression step. The applicant provided data showing that the presence form 3 has no impact on manufacturability, stability, or dissolution, therefore, polymorphic form is not considered an active substance CQA.

The excipients selected for use are microcrystalline cellulose, croscarmellose sodium, magnesium stearate and tablet coating. The rationale for the choice, including the safety of the excipients, is acceptable. All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients, and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation. The list of excipients is included in section 6.1 of the SmPC.

Based on scientific and prior knowledge and risk assessment, the only excipient attribute identified with a high potential to impact product quality is the nitrite level in microcrystalline cellulose (MCC). The active substance has two secondary amine structures with the potential to form Nitrosamine Drug Substance Related Impurities (NDSRIs). The finished product was tested for potential NDSRIs, and an acceptable control strategy was implemented.

Development of the manufacturing process followed QbD principles. The control strategy for the finished product was developed based on risk assessment and the process understanding gained during development to achieve the drug product CQAs. The control strategy includes the attributes of the input materials parameters of the manufacturing process including facility and equipment operating conditions, attributes of in-process materials and in-process controls and finished product specifications and the associated methods. The information provided and applied control strategy are deemed appropriate.

The primary packaging is opaque, white, high-density polyethylene (HDPE) bottles with polypropylene child-resistant closures and polyethylene induction heat seal liner, containing a HDPE canister with silica gel desiccant. The materials comply with Ph. Eur. and EC requirements. The choice of the container closure system has been validated by stability data and is adequate for the intended use of the product.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

For all sites involved in the manufacture, control and batch release of the finished product sufficient evidence of GMP compliance has been provided.

The manufacturing process shown consists of four main steps: blending, compression, film-coating and packaging. The process is considered to be a standard manufacturing process.

A satisfactory process validation scheme was provided. Defined CQAs, CPPs, and the control strategy to support process validation demonstrate and document that the process, together with the defined control strategy, is capable of manufacturing Linerixibat tablets in a reproducible manner. The inprocess controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process and pharmaceutical form.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.3. Product specification

The finished product release and shelf-life specifications include appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form including description, identification, uniformity of dosage units, linerixbat content, impurities, dissolution, and microbiological quality.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product was assessed following a riskbased approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. The risk assessment demonstrated that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE for an oral dosage form. The absence of elemental impurity controls in the finished product specification is justified.

A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product was performed considering all suspected and actual root causes in line with the \"Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/409815/2020) and the \"Assessment report- Procedure under Article 5(3) of Regulation EC (No) 726/2004- Nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products\" (EMA/369136/2020). The control strategy ensures no nitrosamine impurity will be present at or above 10% of its AI limit and no further control measures are deemed necessary.

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards has been presented.

Batch analysis results are provided for 6 production scale batches confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

The finished product is released on the market based on the above release specifications, through traditional final product release testing.

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

Stability data from 3 production scale batches of finished product stored for up to 36 months under long term conditions (30 ºC / 75% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. The batches are representative of those proposed for marketing and were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing.

Samples were tested according to the release shelf-life specifications (Table 4). The analytical procedures used are stability indicating. No significant changes were observed to any of the measured parameters, except for a small increase in one impurity. Considering the impurity is neither specified, not mutagenic, this is not considered an issue.

Studies on bulk finished product demonstrated its stability up to 24 months at 25 ºC / 60% RH. An inuse stability study was conducted on a batch stored under long term conditions for 24 months. Samples were found to be stable when subjected to the simulated patient use regime the finished product based on the SmPC administration instructions for 60 days at 30 ºC / 75% RH which covers all pack sizes applied for.

Samples were found to be photostable when tested according to ICH Q1B and were found to be stable when subjected to repeated freeze/thaw or high temperature (50 ºC) conditions.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 4 years without specific storage conditions as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.5. Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner. The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use. The major objection on the adequacy of the dissolution specification was resolved by provision of additional data by the applicant.

The applicant has applied QbD principles in the development of the active substance and finished product and their manufacturing process. Design spaces have been proposed for all 5 steps in the manufacture of the active substance and have been acceptably justified and verified.

The quality of this product is considered to be acceptable when used in accordance with the conditions defined in the SmPC. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way.

## 3.5. Recommendations for future quality development

Not applicable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

The ileal bile acid transporter (IBAT) is expressed in the distal ileum, where it controls reabsorption of bile acids from the intestine for recirculation to the liver. Linerixibat is a reversible inhibitor of the IBAT. It decreases the reabsorption of bile acids in the ileum leading to their increased faecal elimination.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Linerixibat plasma concentrations were measured by bioanalytical methods based on either protein precipitation (PPT) or solid phase extraction (SPE); followed by high performance liquid chromatographic (HPLC) separation coupled with tandem mass spectrometric (MS/MS) detection. Concentrations of linerixibat present in biological samples were determined from calibration curves constructed from analysis of samples spiked with known concentrations of linerixibat. The suitability of each analytical method was confirmed by determining the accuracy, precision, linearity, range and limit of quantification. In addition, quality control samples were analyzed in parallel to establish the suitability of the method at the time of analysis.

In studies where [ 14 C]-linerixibat was used, determination of radioactive concentrations was carried out by either direct LSC sometimes following combustion of the sample or AMS following LC separation. For analysis of radioactivity concentrations in tissues, quantitative whole-body autoradiography was used. The profiling and identification of metabolites of linerixibat was performed using LC-MSn.

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

Linerixibat inhibited uptake of [ 3 H]-taurocholate with an IC50 of 44 nM (~22 ng/mL) in in-vitro studies employing human embryonic kidney (HEK293) cells expressing the human IBAT protein. Potency of linerixibat as indicated by IC50 values determined in these in-vitro studies was at dog and monkey IBAT approximately twice as high (both 23 nM) compared to human IBAT and at mouse and rat IBAT approximately 20 times as high (2.1 nM and 1.9 nM, respectively) compared to human IBAT. Linerixibat is a very weak NTCP inhibitor (normally expressed in hepatocytes), with an IC50 value 637 µM.

Thus, linerixibat effectively inhibited IBAT-mediated transport of bile acids in a cell based assay. Inhibition of the IBAT has therefore been adequately demonstrated.

The species used in general toxicity repeated dose toxicity studies were mice, rats and dogs. Based on the in-vitro primary pharmacology studies, these species are considered relevant species.

Linerixibat is expected to exert its action at the IBAT expressed at the luminal/apical surface of the enterocytes in the terminal ileum. The recommended human clinical dose is 40 mg BID. The single dose amount of 40 mg in a hypothetical volume of 250 mL would equal 160 mg/L, which is more than 6,500 times higher than the in-vitro determined IC50 of 44 nM (~22 ng/mL) at the human IBAT

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

protein. Even taking the artificial environment of the in-vitro study, further dilution during the enteral passage and possibly a binding of linerixibat to material in the gut into account, the in-vitro determined potency of linerixibat at the human IBAT is expected to be high enough in order to enable linerixibat to exert its pharmacological action in vivo.

In vivo pharmacodynamic studies were conducted in mice, rats, dogs and monkey using the oral route of administration in order to demonstrate that pharmacological inhibition of BA reabsorption with an IBAT inhibitor leads to increased excretion of BA into the faeces.

Linerixibat administered orally significantly and dose-dependently increased in normal male C57BL mice the 24-hour excretion of total faecal bile acids and faecal weight normalized concentration of bile acid. The 50% effective dose (ED50) value for the latter parameter was 0.02 mg/kg bw. The results of this study are in line with an in-vivo inhibition of the IBAT and a resulting inhibition of the intestinal bile acids (BA) and an increased faecal elimination of BA. The linerixibat-induced increase in fecal elimination of BA in mice may be considered Proof of Concept.

In Zucker diabetic rats administered orally twice daily linerixibat doses of 0.001 to 10 mg/kg/dose for 2, 3 or 5 weeks, linerixibat significantly and partially dose-dependently increased excretion of faecal bile acids. In the 5-week study at several time points and linerixibat dose levels non-dose-related reductions of plasma BA were seen.

A single oral dose of linerixibat (10 mg/kg) to healthy male beagle dogs increased faecal bile acid excretion &gt;4-fold compared to vehicle group.

When rhesus monkeys were treated orally twice daily with linerixibat (1, 3, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/dose) in a dose escalation manner in food, linerixibat dose-dependently increased faecal bile acids.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

The activity of linerixibat was tested in an enhanced X-screen Profiling Panel (eXP) including 37 pharmacological targets and in a Cell Health Assay (measures of cellular toxicity). Linerixibat tested at 1 µM did not show agonist or antagonist activity at any of the tested targets, defined as a half maximal effective concentration of less than 1 µM.

In an enzyme and radioligand binding assay with 17 enzyme assays and 72 physiological receptors, ion channels and transporters potential interactions of linerixibat were screened at a concentration of 10 µM. Linerixibat did not significantly stimulate or inhibit (defined as ≥50%) the activity of the 17 enzymes tested and did not bind (defined as ≥50%) to any of the 72 receptors, ion channels and transporter assays tested.

Finally, linerixibat was examined in an aortic ring contraction response tissue assay. Up to the highest concentration tested of 10 µM, linerixibat had no significant effect (defined as ≥50%) on serotonin (5HT2A) induced aortic ring contraction.

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

The Applicant conducted in-vitro and in-vivo safety pharmacology studies.

In a GLP compliant neurobehavioural single oral dose safety study in male rats at the dose levels of 500 and 2000 mg/kg bw only alteration of faeces consistency is reported, which is most likely due to the pharmacological activity of the compound. This was also the case in the oral respiratory safety study. The NOAEL for neurobehavioural effects can therefore be considered 2000 mg/kg. In other submitted rat studies with oral administration of 2000 mg/kg Cmax levels of 469 and 766 ng/mL have been reported. Depending on the clinical Cmax taken as a basis (0.922 or 1.980 ng/mL) safety factors of 237 to 831 result. In a GLP compliant respiratory single oral dose safety study in male rats the no effect oral dose was 500 mg/kg, and at 2000 mg/kg bw a mild, reversible decrease in pulmonary ventilation (minute volume) of up to 29% was seen that was due to similar decreases in respiratory rate and tidal volume, and a mild, reversible increase in total pulmonary resistance from approximately 1 to 4 hours postdose. In a toxicokinetic study in rats submitted, at the oral NOAEL of 500 mg/kg, a Cmax of 280 ng/mL had been determined. Thus, depending on the clinical Cmax taken as a basis (0.922 or 1.980 ng/mL) safety factors of 304 or 141 result. In contrast to the oral study, an IV dose of 10 mg/kg linerixibat induced an increase in mean total pulmonary ventilation (minute volume) (up to 58%) due to a mean increase in tidal volume (up to 39%), which was primarily due to an increase in one of the four animals. A dose-response effect and a NOAEL could not be established as 10 mg/kg was the only dose tested. However, IV administration of linerixibat did not reproduce the respiratory findings seen in the oral study (decreased pulmonary ventilation and increased pulmonary resistance). No concerns for respiratory effects at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) are raised. In a GLP compliant in-vitro hERG assay up to the highest soluble concentration of 138.71 µg/mL (253.73 μM) no effect on the hERG channel tail current was seen. It is considered acceptable that lower linerixibat concentration has not been tested. According to SmPC section 5.2 following multiple doses of linerixibat 40 mg twice daily in PBC patients with cholestatic pruritus, the mean Cmax (95 % CI) was 922 pg/mL. Without taking plasma protein binding into account, the concentration tested in the hERG assay was about 150,000 times higher than the clinical Cmax. A small increase in contractility observed in a rabbit heart wedge assay at 3 μM is not raising concerns. In a rabbit wedge assay, the effects of linerixibat on electrocardiographic parameters and contractility in the isolated, arterially perfused rabbit left ventricular wedge preparation were evaluated. Linerixibat tested up to 100 μM, exerted negligible effects on QT interval, transmural dispersion of repolarization and QRS interval. There was a concentration-independent, 17.8% increase at 3 μM in the isometric contractile force generation, but this positive inotropic effect diminished with increasing concentrations, and contractility returned to baseline at 100 μM. This suggests that linerixibat may elicit a small positive inotropic effect in the 3-30 μM concentration range. However, the magnitude of the effect was small and was not considered concerning. Also taking into account the low clinical systemic exposure, it is unlikely that linerixibat would induce clinically significant QT prolongation, conduction-dependent arrhythmia or Torsade de Pointes in humans. In a non-GLP compliant oral cardiovascular safety study in rats at 1000 mg/kg (but not at 10 or 100 mg/kg) increased in mean arterial blood pressure (+15%), heart rate (+16%), and cardial workload (+31%) and a decrease in the PR interval of up to -4.6 msec was observed. In a subsequent non-GLP compliant cardiovascular safety study in rats with IV doses up to 10 mg/kg bw, in which a significantly higher exposure would be expected (a Cmax of 2617 ng/mL is reported in male rats at a single IV dose of 10 mg/kg in a pharmacokinetic study), no such effects were observed. In a GLP compliant cardiovascular safety study in dogs with oral administration of 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg bw/day, at the highest dose of 1000 mg/kg a mild, reversible increase in mean arterial pressure (up to 10 mmHg) was observed. No PK data is available from this dog study. At the no-observedeffect-dose of this study of 100 mg/kg the Cmax in male dogs ranged in other dog studies submitted from 102 ng/mL to 334 ng/mL, which is 110 to 360 times higher than the predicted human plasma Cmax (0.922 ng/mL) in patients at the MRHD mentioned in SmPC section 5.2 or 52 to 169 times higher than the Cmax after a single dose of 40 mg in patients (1.98 ng/ml for patients with moderate hepatic impairment). No concerns are raised regarding the cardiovascular safety of linerixibat in clinical use.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.1.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No non-clinical studies investigating pharmacodynamic drug interactions have been conducted with linerixibat. A pharmacokinetic interaction study in rats and clinical co-administration studies with UDCA and OCA have been conducted. Based on the available non-clinical and clinical data and on the high specificity of linerixibat for IBAT this is considered acceptable.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

## Single dose administration

Single dose pharmacokinetics after oral and intravenous administration has been investigated in mice, rats, dogs, rabbits and monkeys in a series of non GLP studies. In rabbits, absorption was studied after oral administration only. Investigations were performed at fasting and non-fasting conditions with different vehicle formulations. In most studies only males were investigated. In one study in rats (2011N112040) and one study in dogs (2011N111915) blood samples were taken from the portal vein instead from the systemic circulation.

Following single IV administration of linerixibat, systemic clearance was high across nonclinical species. The volume of distribution was low to moderate. Consequently, the half-life of linerixibat was short (0.2 - 0.6 hr). Systemic exposure increased in an IV-dose proportional manner. Single-dose IV human data (from the clinical radiolabeled study 205895) are consistent with the data from nonclinical species in terms of rapid clearance and small volume of distribution resulting in short elimination t½. Following a single oral radiolabeled dose to humans, linerixibat exhibited absorption-limited flip-flop kinetics: longer oral versus intravenous half-life (6-7 hrs vs. 0.8 hrs).

After oral administration linerixibat exposure increased with dose across nonclinical species. Linerixibat oral bioavailability was apparently unaffected by food in rats and dogs and below 1% in rats, dogs and monkeys. Rat and dog studies following oral administration with portal and systemic blood sampling demonstrated that the low oral bioavailability resulted primarily from the low fraction absorbed, consistent with its low permeability and secondarily from the high hepatic extraction of any absorbed drug.

## Repeated dose administration:

Repeated dose pharmaco- and toxicokinetics have been investigated after intravenous and oral administration. Most studies are GLP-compliant. One study in mice (2020N432134), three studies in rats (RD2010/00322, RD2010/00301and 2016N279429), and one study in pregnant rabbits (2014N206910) are not GLP-compliant.

Studies with repeated dose intravenous administration are limited to one study in rats. Studies with repeated dose oral administration have been performed in mice (up to 26 weeks), rats (up to 26 weeks), dogs (up to 39 weeks) and monkeys (up to 4 days) and in pregnant rats and rabbits. Linerixibat was administrated once daily by oral gavage in rats and dogs, twice daily in (pregnant) rabbits and in food treats in monkeys. Linerixibat was given as solution or suspension in an aqueous solution containing sodium triphosphate, hydroxypropyl methylcellulose and Tween 80 apart from the studies in monkeys.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In monkeys neither exact dose administered could be determined nor relevant exposure could be detected. Therefore, these studies were excluded from further evaluation.

One study in rats (RD2010/00492) was excluded from the tabulated results presented in Module 2.6 of the current application and from further considerations, due to an apparently significant exposure of control animals to linerixiabat.

Overall, oral linerixibat exposure generally increased with dose across species in nonclinical toxicology studies in a dose-proportional or greater than dose-proportional manner. No consistent sex-related differences in systemic exposure of linerixibat were observed in animals. Time-dependent increases in linerixibat exposure were evident in the rat 13- and 26-week studies. In contrast, in dogs a trend of decreased exposure over time was observed (13- and 2-week studies), but this trend was not reproduced in the 39-week study despite the same dose range. In pregnant rats, systemic exposure to linerixibat increased approximately dose proportionally or less than dose proportionally, depending on the study, and was only quantifiable at ≥200 mg/kg/day. In pregnant rabbits, exposure often increased greater than dose proportionally, with evidence of accumulation upon repeated twice-daily dosing.

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

Distribution of linerixibat was investigated in-vivo and in-vitro. In-vitro investigations included binding of linerixibat to plasmatic or microsomal proteins, partioning into blood cells and P-glykoprotein (P-gp) associated transmission over cell monolayers in stable transfected human Multi-Drug Resistance 1 - Madin Darby Canine Kidney (hMDR1-MDCK) cells. In-vivo studies are limited to a whole-body distribution study in rats and reproductive toxicity studies investigating foetal or postnatal exposure during maternal administration.

Overall, the in-vitro studies indicate a low protein binding capacity of 71.0- 80.6 % in the final study with 14 C-linerixibat. In preliminary studies similar results were obtained. Blood-to-plasma portioning investigations showed a low binding ratio (0.635 to 0.840).

Following oral administration of 14 C-linerixibat (10 mg/kg) to intact male rats, radioactive drug-related material was rapidly excreted, almost entirely via faeces (99% of the dose). Minimal oral absorption of radioactive drug-related material was apparent in BDC rats, determined by the combined recovery of radioactivity in bile and urine (3% of the dose). Beyond the contents of the gastrointestinal tract, distribution of drug-related material was minimal. Measurable radioactivity was only quantifiable in the small intestine and liver. Together with limited measurable radioactivity in blood and plasma, collectively the data obtained in this study strongly suggest minimal oral absorption following oral administration of linerixibat to rats.

Distribution of linerixibat into the milk of lactating animals was not directly investigated. However, conclusions can be drawn from the GLP pre- and postnatal development study [2019N410463] indicating lactational exposure to linerixibat via milk.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

The metabolism of linerixibat was investigated in-vitro and in-vivo. In-vitro investigations involved metabolic profile, stability, and glutathione adduct formation. In-vivo metabolism was investigated in mice, rats, dogs and humans, and chiral conversion in mice, dogs and humans.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In two in-vitro studies with cryopreserved hepatocytes [2011N113371 and 2011N112743] in total 4 human metabolites (M1 to M4) could be identified. The human metabolites (M1 to M4) were formed in hepatocytes from rats and dogs too. In mouse hepatocytes, two of the human metabolites were observed. An additional metabolite was observed in mouse hepatocytes that was unique to that species. All metabolites detected were formed through oxidation of the alkyl chains. In activated rat and hamster liver S9 fractions some additional metabolites (M5 to M13) could be detected, which were originated by dehydrogenation/ hydroxylation of the side chains [2022N510107]. General stability of linerixibat investigated in hepatocytes of mouse, rat dog monkey and human origin [2010N107931], showed some degradation resulting in a calculated in-vivo clearance with rat&gt;mouse&gt;dog&gt;monkey&gt;human, capacity. In plasmatic samples of rats (2010N109738) and gastrointestinal samples of rats and humans no degradation of linerixibat could be detected (2011N113026, 2011N115034 and 2011N113324). There was no evidence of glutathione adduct

formation (2011N112648).

In-vivo studies (2018N385212 and 2015N256071) with single dose administration of 14 C-linerixibat at 30 mg/kg in mice, 10 mg/kg in rats and 5 mg/kg in dogs showed that, the plasmatic radioactivity in mice and rats was too low for further investigations. In dog plasma, unchanged linerixibat (9 to 13% of plasma radioactivity) and mono-oxygenated metabolites predominated (M3, M4, and M5; 17 to 30%, 11 to 17%, and 7 to 11% of the plasma radioactivity, respectively) could be detected. Other uncharacterized minor circulating components, some of which were associated with radiolabeled impurities of test material were also detected. In pooled samples of rats and dogs following 13 weeks of repeated dose oral administration of linerixibat at 1000 mg/kg/day (rat) and 500 mg/kg/day (dog), unchanged linerixibat and 3 metabolites formed by hydroxylation (M1, M2 and M3) were observed in pooled dog plasma (0-8 h).

In humans (2020N432927) with 14 C-linerixibat unchanged linerixibat was the main circulating compound following i.v. administration (85.6% of radioactivity). A radiochemical impurity of the initial test solution (compound E) was also detected. Following oral administration, unchanged linerixibat and radiochemical dose impurity accounted for 5.8% and 3.8% of the total circulating radioactivity, respectively. A notable uncharacterized component (moiety G), previously observed in nonclinical 14 Clinerixibat studies, accounted for 18.2% of the sample radioactivity. Minor components included oxidative metabolites M5 and M6 accounting for 4.1% and 3.4% of the radioactivity, respectively. An uncharacterized peak in the solvent front (P1 Oral), consistent with a column breakthrough artefact, accounted for 13.3% of the radioactivity. All other components were below 7% of total radioactivity. In another clinical study (2020N432927) human faecal metabolites M8, M9 and M10 were detected after oral administration of linerixibat.

A study on chiral conversion of linerixibat to other diastereomers in plasma of mice and humans after oral administration showed no relevant conversion of linerixbat (2020N442223).

The applicant concludes from these studies that linerixibat metabolism was minimal in humans following both oral and intravenous administration of 14 C-linerixibat. Instead, elimination of intravenous dose in human occurred by excretion of unchanged parent drug via the biliary/fecal and urinary routes.

Metabolism of oral 14 C-linerixibat in mice and rats was similarly low, although it was relatively higher in dogs.

The oxidative metabolites observed in vitro and in vivo were fundamentally consistent between nonclinical species and humans. No major human metabolites were observed, and there was no evidence of reactive metabolite formation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Excretion was investigated in mouse, intact and bile duct cannulated rat, dog and human in a series of non GLP studies. Radioactive and non-radioactive methods were used. Investigations are limited to males only. The results of the main studies performed with 14 C-linerixibat are presented in Table 5 below. Mice, rats and dogs received the indicated doses by oral administration. Humans received a single dose via i.v. administration. Urine, faeces and bile were collected and the percentage of applied radioactivity determined.
