The low concentrations of linerixibat measured in the portal vein [2011N112040, 2011N111915 rats and dogs respectively] and the nearly quantitative recovery in the faeces [2018N372243; 2018N370009; 2018N371104; 2019N424264] suggest that linerixibat was not absorbed well from the gastrointestinal tract of fed male SD rats and was resistant to degradation in the rat gastrointestinal tract. The low recovery in bile and urine samples from rats treated orally is consistent with the poor absorption of linerixibat. In addition, no evidence of a glucuronide metabolite was observed in bile and urine samples from rats treated orally with linerixibat.

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

Potential drug interactions were investigated in several in-vitro test systems and in one in-vivo study in male rats.

Two studies (2010N109399 and 2017N34575). investigated potential inhibitory effects of linerixibat of human cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes. In the preliminary study (2010N109399) direct and time dependent inhibitory effects were investigated with pooled substrates and pooled human microsomes. In this study the potential IC50s of CYP 1A2, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and CYP3A4 were determined. No clear result could be obtained for CYP2C9. For all other CYP the potential IC50 was above 33µM. Time dependent inhibition was investigated for CYP3A4 only. However, no time dependent effect could be shown. In the final study (2017N34575) the direct and metabolism-dependent inhibition of the human CYPs CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1 and CYP3A4 by linerixibat, was investigated in pooled human liver microsomes individually. No significant direct inhibitory effect of linerixibat could be shown up to the highest concentration (100 µM) tested. Indications on time dependent/metabolism inhibitory effects were detected for CYP3A4 only. A followup experiment to determine the kinetic parameters of inactivation, half-maximal inactivation (KI) and maximal rate constant of inactivation (kinact), for CYP3A4, using midazolam as a substrate was conducted. Linerixibat caused a progressive loss of enzyme activity as the pre-incubation time with concentration of linerixibat required to achieving aKI of 1305 µM, resulting in inactivation efficiency (kinact/KI) of 0.00012 min-1µM-1 for CYP3A4. The average percent binding of linerixibat with human liver microsomes following a 5-hr incubation period at 37˚C using equilibrium dialysis method was NADPH increased. Linerixibat was an apparent inactivator of CYP3A4, with a kinact of 0.16 min -1 and a determined to be 54% and fumic was 0.46.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In a studies investigating inductive effects on CYP expression (2019N400807) no effects of linerixibat on mRNA levels of CYP1A2, 2B6 and 3A4 could be detected. A screening study investigating a brought panel of different pharmacodynamic targets did not predict PXR activation (2011N112848).

A study investigating the potential metabolic breakdown of linerixibat by 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1, 3A4 and 3A5 (2017N336849), showed that linerixibat is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4.

A study with cells expressing human P-gp showed no inhibitory effect on P-gp mediated transport up to a concentration of 300 µM (2014N191987). Linerixiabt inhibited the human BCRP mediated transport of cimetedine at the highest concentration tested (300 µM) to 27% of control (2011N120784). In a study (2016N307817) designed to evaluate the ability of linerixibat to act as an inhibitor of the following transporters: P-gp, BCRP, OAT1 and OAT3, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, OCT2, and MATE1 and MATE2-K, linerixibat inhibited the OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated transport of each probe substrate with calculated IC50 values of 2.69 and 0.265 µM, respectively. OAT3 was inhibited by 47.9%, at the highest concentration tested (100 µM). No inhibitory effects were detected for P-gp and BCRP (up to 300 µM) and of OAT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K (up to 100 µM).

This shows, that linerixibat is not a substrate of P-gp and OATP2B1, but of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3.

Binding studies with colesvelam (2011N113254) and cholesyramin (2020N448460) showed a significant binding of linerixibat to both substances in PBS as well as fasted state simulated intestinal fluid (FaSIF). For colesvalem the fraction of unbound linerixibat was 0.05 and 0.22 in PBS and FaSIF respectively. For cholestyramin the fraction of unbound linerixibat was 0.13 to 0.048 in PBS and 0.10 to 0.058 in FaSIF.

One in vivo study in male rats investigated the influence of linerixibat on the absorption of bile acidlike drugs [e.g., obeticholic acid (OCA) and ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA)] (2017N319427). OCA was chosen as model substance. Following repeated oral administration of OCA with linerixibat for 7 days, AUC0-t values of OCA decreased on average 30% and Cmax values decreased 23% compared to values when OCA was given alone. The plasma concentrations of the taurine conjugate of OCA decreased 50% to 83% over the 24-hr sampling time course upon co-administration with linerixibat. The glycine conjugate was not detected in plasma samples from either regimen at appreciable levels.

## Effects of other medicinal products on the pharmacokinetics of linerixibat / victim drug interaction potential:

The applicant concludes in the view of the results summarized above, and given the minimal absorption, flip-flop absorption limited PK in humans, and minimal metabolism that has been observed in humans, the risk of other drugs to affect the pharmacokinetics of linerixibat are predicted to be low.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

CYP3A4 was the predominant mediator of in vitro oxidative metabolism; however, given the minimal metabolism in humans (and animals), effects of other Drugs on CYP3A4 are considered a not clinically relevant mechanism.

Linerixibat was a substrate of hepatic OATP1B1 and OATP1B3(2017N333902).; however, due to minimal intestinal absorption and flip-flop absorption-rate limited pharmacokinetics in humans, potential effects of other drugs on hepatic OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 linerixibat transport are considered not to be clinically relevant.

Notably, linerixibat was not a substrate for human P-gp (2011N120417) or BCRP (2011N120784), indicating that oral co administration of linerixibat with P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors would not increase linerixibat absorption. Consistent with minimal absorption, linerixibat is not a substrate for the intestinal OATP isoform, OATP2B1(2017N333902).

In-vitro studies demonstrated that 78-95% of linerixibat was bound by colesevelam or cholestyramine in phosphate buffered saline and FaSIF.

\"Effects of linerixibat on the pharmacokinetics of other drugs/ Perpetrator drug interaction potential

The applicant concludes in the view of the results summarized above and given, the minimal systemic exposure, the risk for clinically significant effects of linerixiby on the pharmacokinetics of other drugs to be low.

Linerixibat is an in-vitro inhibitor of CYP3A4, but extrapolation using human PK did not meet the criteria for a clinically-relevant inhibitor. In-vitro studies showed that linerixibat is neither an inhibitor nor an inducer of other major CYP enzymes.

Linerixibat inhibited OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OAT3 in-vitro, but extrapolation using human PK did not meet the criteria for a clinically-relevant inhibitor. Otherwise, linerixibat was not an inhibitor of other major transporters. As an IBAT inhibitor, linerixibat may limit absorption of bile acid-like drugs. Clinical studies demonstrated linerixibat may be taken with UDCA without requiring dose adjustment. In contrast, co-administration of linerixibat and obeticholic acid decreased plasma concentrations of obeticholic acid and its conjugates (taurine and glycine), which may reduce the efficacy of obeticholic acid.

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

N.A.

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

The acute toxicity of linerixibat has been characterized in an oral dose escalation study in the dog that was conducted to support the definitive repeat dose toxicity study and in dose ranging IV studies in rats and dogs. In rats at 30 mg/kg (IV, slow bolus), one rat had redness around the injection site, and another rat had staining of the urogenital (yellow) or anal (brown/orange) areas. In dogs, single oral (gavage) escalating doses of linerixibat up to 1000 mg/kg with at least 4 days between doses were associated with faecal alterations (soft, loose/watery and/or mucoid) at doses ≥10 mg/kg, and emesis at ≥500 mg/kg, although the faecal alteration appeared not dose dependent. Red material in the faeces was observed at 30 and 60 mg/kg. Single escalating IV doses up to 15 mg/kg (slow bolus) or 60 mg/kg (4-hr infusion) in dogs with at least 3 days between doses were associated with faecal alterations at least once after each dose and emesis on the day after receiving the 4-hr infusions at 15 and 30 mg/kg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

The general toxicity of linerixibat was studied in repeated dose toxicity studies in mice, rats and dogs of up to 4, 26 and 39 weeks, respectively, with maximum doses of 2000, 1000, and 500 mg/kg/day, respectively.

## Mouse

In CD-1 and RasH2 mice, linerixibat was tested for up to 4 weeks via oral gavage up to 2000 mg/kg in two non-GLP compliant and one GLP-compliant studies. Epithelial degeneration/necrosis, inflammation, and/or exudate in the nasal turbinate and/or nasopharynx were observed at ≥100 mg/kg, with apparent clinical signs noted at ≥500 mg/kg, including decreased general activity, piloerection, hunched posture, squinted eye(s), sternal recumbency, ataxia, laboured/irregular and/or audible respiration, swollen abdomen, squinted eyes, cool to the touch, excessive lacrimation, liquid/mucoid and/or yellow feces and /or thin appearance. The severity and incidence of these findings were dose dependent. Group early termination due to moribund conditions was performed at 1000 mg/kg in the 4-week non-GLP study in CD-1 and RasH2 mice. The clinical observations, microscopic findings, and moribund conditions were considered due to gavage-related reflux and irritant response in nasal cavity to linerixibat. Similar effects are described in the 104-week carcinogenicity study in rats, but not in the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats. These findings are not considered clinically relevant as there is no clinical correlate for this method of gavage dosing. Given no apparent clinical signs at 100 mg/kg, the NOAEL was determined at 100 mg/kg for the GLP-compliant study in RasH2 mice. Taking the AUC stated by the Applicant for humans at the intended clinical dose (22.1 ng*h/mL) as a basis an exposure margin of approximately 7-fold for males and 1.4-fold for females results.

## Rat

In the Sprague Dawley rat 14-day oral toxicity study, minimal mucosal basophilia/hypertrophy with or without increased inflammatory cell infiltrate was observed microscopically in the large intestine at all doses (lowest dose 1 mg/kg/day).

One 2-week GLP toxicity study and one GLP toxicokinetic study (in which apart from toxicokinetics only clinical signs and bodyweight were recorded) in SD rats dosed with linerixibat at up to 2000 mg/kg/day and one non-GLP toxicity study in WH rats at up to 1000 mg/kg/day via oral administration were conducted. The common findings across these three studies included soft feces (at ≥100 mg/kg/day in SD rats and 1000 mg/kg/day in WH rats), brown/orange staining of the fur around the anal area (at ≥100 mg/kg/day in SD rats and 1000 mg/kg/day in WH rats), minimal mucosal basophilia/hypertrophy in the cecum (at ≥1 mg/kg/day in SD rats and ≥10 mg/kg/day in WH rats) or colon (at ≥1 mg/kg/day in SD rats and 1000 mg/kg/day in WH rats). In one 2-week GLP-compliant study in SD rats, minimal inflammatory cell infiltrates in the cecum were observed, and the incidence of cecal minimal inflammatory cell infiltrates was inversely correlated with dose in both males and females, with highest incidences at 1 mg/kg/day and no incidences at 2000 mg/kg/day. The inverse correlation with dose was not observed in other longer studies in WH rats. Other findings revealed by the studies in SD rats included a low incidence of minimal inflammatory cell infiltrate in the colon, transient decrease in group mean body weight gain and food consumption in males at 2000 mg/kg/day, and minor changes in clinical pathology, urine protein and urine glucose parameters. All the linerixibat related changes were considered non-adverse due to the minimum in severity of the findings, no microscopic correlates, and no negative impact on the general daily function of the rats. The NOAEL of the study in SD rats was determined at 2000 mg/kg/day corresponding to AUCs of 2756 ng*h/mL in male and 1749 ng*h/mL in female animals at the end of the study. Taking the AUC stated by the Applicant for humans at the intended clinical dose (22.1 ng*h/mL) as a basis an exposure margin of approximately 125-fold for males and 79-fold for females results.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

One 13-week and one 26-week GLP-compliant toxicity studies in in WH rats dosed with linerixibat at 10, 200, and 1000 mg/kg/day via oral administration with a 4-week and 6-week recovery period, respectively, were conducted. In both studies, clinical signs were almost exclusively limited to the high dose group, primarily being transient watery/mucoid/non-formed faeces, brown haircoat of the inguinal, hind quarter, perineal, and/or scrotal areas. Minimal to slight basophilia/hypertrophy in the cecum, colon, and rectum were observed at ≥10 mg/kg/day in the 13-week study and at ≥200 mg/kg/day in the 26-week study with no evidence of degeneration/necrosis, hyperplasia or increased inflammation. In the 26-week study, a clear dose-dependent increase in the incidence of minimal to slight hypertrophy of the zona glomerulosa in the adrenal cortex was noted at ≥200 mg/kg/day, with 2 males affected at 200 mg/kg/day and 23/24 animals affected at 1000 mg/kg/day. The microscopic findings in the adrenal cortex were not associated with electrolyte imbalance based on the clinical pathology. At all doses, minor decreases in mean glucose (up to 0.7-fold compared to control), triglyceride (up to 0.39-fold compared to control), and mean total serum bile acids (0.32-fold compared to control for males and 0.23-fold compared to control for females) were seen. Given that all the linerixibat-related effects were of low severity and/or lack of associated clinical signs, absence of clinical pathology or microscopic correlates, for both studies the NOAELs were determined at 1000 mg/kg/day. Taking the AUC stated by the Applicant for humans at the intended clinical dose (22.1 ng*h/mL) as a basis a gender-averaged exposure margin of approximately 12-fold for the 13-week study and 64-fold for the 26-week study results.

## Dog

In dogs, linerixibat was orally administered at up to 1000 mg/kg/day in one 7-day non-GLP and one 14-day GLP study and at up to 500 mg/kg/day in one 13-week- and 39-week-GLP study.

In the 7-day non-GLP and/or the 14-day GLP-compliant toxicity study, linerixibat-related findings were increased faecal bile acids (2.1-to 14-fold relative to pre-dose values), increased incidence of loose and/or mucoid faeces at ≥1 mg/kg/day and discoloured faeces at ≥100 mg/kg/day, slight to extreme salivation and emesis at ≥200 mg/kg/day, focal, minimal to mild haemorrhage in the colonic and/or rectal mucosa at ≥20mg/kg/day, minimal diffuse hypertrophy of the goblet cells lining the crypts of the colon and irregular surface in the gallbladder mucosa (only in the male in the 7-day study) at 1000 mg/kg/day. In the 14-day GLP study, mild increase in serum GLDH (2.9-fold relative to pre-dose value) was noted.

In the 13-week dog study, transient reversible non-formed faeces, excessive salivation, and vomitus (males only) at 500 mg/kg/day were the only findings. In addition to the abnormal faeces, in the 39week dog study decreased cholesterol (0.68 to 0.88-fold of pre-dose values) and decreased triglyceride (0.37 to 0.82-fold of pre-dose values) were seen at ≥5 mg/kg/day in the 39-week dog study. While tested animals generally exhibited abnormal faeces and minor changes in the clinical chemistry parameters such serum GLDH, cholesterol and triglyceride, there were no changes in body weight, food consumption, hydration status and during microscopic examination. For the GLPcompliant dog studies the NOAELs were determined at the high doses. Taking the AUC stated by the Applicant for humans at the intended clinical dose (22.1 ng*h/mL) as a basis a gender-averaged exposure multiples 212-fold for the 14-day dog study, 10-fold for the 13-week dog study, and 62-fold for the 39-week dog study result.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

Genotoxicity testing of linerixibat (GSK2330672) was carried out in vitro (gene mutation test in bacteria, mammalian cell mutation test) and in vivo (rat, micronucleus test) in accordance with ICH S2(R1) guidance and GLP requirements. Linerixibat tested negative for an increase in reverse mutations in an AMES test (2010N108047) with and without metabolic activation up to the recommended top concentration of 5000 µg/plate for this assay under the conditions of the study. A mammalian cell mutation test (2010N105749) with mouse lymphoma L5178Y cells was tested negative for inducing chromosomal aberrations and an increase in mutant frequency with and without metabolic activation up to 520 μg/ml the highest dose tested due to solubility and stability of linerixibat in DMSO.

Linerixibat tested negative for an increase in micronucleated PCEs in a micronucleus assay in rats (2010N106039) up to the recommended top concentration of 2000 mg/kg for this assay under the conditions of the study. No exposure data was determined in the study but by comparison to TK data obtained in study RD2010/00322 sufficiently high margins to the clinical dose/exposures (40 mg/bid) may be expected.

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

Carcinogenicity testing of linerixibat (GSK2330672) was carried out in a 6-months oral carcinogenicity study in hemizygous (RasH2-H) mice (2021N476126) and a 2-year oral carcinogenicity study in rats (2020N454153) in accordance with the ICH S1 guidance and GLP requirements. The study designs and the dose selection derived from preliminary oral DRF and toxicity studies in male and female RasH2 WT and male CD1 mice (2020N432134, 2019N422089) and oral toxicity studies in rats up to 26 w of treatment.

Independent of the mouse strain in both DRF studies dose-dependent minimal to severe clinical signs of toxicity were determined in the respiratory and olfactory tract in all dose groups. Epithelial degeneration and necrosis, inflammation, luminal exudate and oedema were assessed as localized, irritant response to reflux following the gavage procedure and responsible for animals unscheduled euthanized in moribund conditions at doses ≥ 500 mg/kg. Minimal to slight findings in the liver in male and female RasH2 WT mice (decreased vacuolation hepatocytes in centrilobular areas) were without histopathologic correlate. In the pivotal 26-week carcinogenicity study no treatment related effects on survival and mortality were observed. In both genders, sporadically decreases in body weight and food consumption occurred without a dose-relationship. As observed in the previous DRF studies nonneoplastic alterations (♀ ≥ 50 mg/kg, ♂ 160 mg/kg) were restricted to minimal to slight degeneration of the epithelium and exudate in the nasal turbinate and nasopharynx without correlating observations. These findings were considered as localized irritant response to reflux and were consistent with observations observed in the DRF studies but to a minor extent. Minimal to slight findings in the liver (decreased vacuolation) in high dose animals as described above for the DRF studies were without histopathologic correlate and regarded as adaptive physiologic response (Thoolen 2010, Harada 1999). No (other) linerixibat related findings in clinical (histo-)pathology, respectively changes in body weight, and food consumption were observed. All other (non-)neoplastic findings were not dose related, were of low incidence/severity and expected to occur (spontaneously) as background findings in the RasH2 mice strain (Tsuchiya 2002, Takaoka 2003, Kanno 2003, Morton 2002, Paranjpe 2013).

In the pivotal carcinogenicity study in rats no linerixibat related effects on mortality and survival were observed expect for an increase in the high dose group in males (53 %, control 28 %). The causes of death were most frequent gastric reflux assessed as localized, irritant response. Neoplastic causes of death and early euthanasia (pituitary pars distalis adenoma, pars distalis carcinoma, uterine endometrial adenocarcinoma, mammary gland fibroadenoma) were non-significant in both genders. In the remaining animals of both genders minimal to marked findings in the nasal cavity namely exudat, necrosis, erosion/ulcer, epithelial degeneration/regeneration, and (chronic) inflammation due to gastric reflux were observed. These changes are consistent with gastric reflux as previously reported in the literature (Damsch 2011). The findings were considered to represent a localized, irritant response rather than a systemic effect. This observation was in line with that observed in mice (see above). No (meaningful) influence on the occurrence of palpable masses, body weights, food consummation, and ophthalmic examinations were detected. Non-neoplastic alterations in the large intestinal tracts were minimally increased mucosal basophilia/hypertrophy observed in all dose groups of both genders. Non dose related minimal to mild (occasional marked) increases in liver findings (clear cell foci of alteration) were observed in females of all dose groups without increases in the incidence of neoplastic alterations in this organ. The observation was consistent in all toxicology studies conducted in rat independent of study duration (≤ 6-month). No progression in severity was observed and dependent on the duration of the recovery period these findings were (partly) reversible. The observation most likely represents GI irritation related to physicochemical properties of linerixibat and changes in bile acid flux through the GI tract, a well-known effect of ileal bile acid transporter inhibitors (Al-Dury 2018). In mid and high-dose animals significant minimal to mild increases in hypertrophy of the zona glomerulosa in the adrenals were observed without degenerative changes in the adrenal gland. The finding is consistent with observations in the 6-month chronic toxicity study with reversibility shown after recovery. No linerixibat related increased neoplastic alterations were found except increased pituitary pars intermedia adenomas in mid-does males. This was not considered significant due to the tumor in rats is not rare with a background incidence &gt; 1 %. Further, no dose-dependency was observed, and the finding was detected only in one gender. All other (non-)neoplastic findings had similar incidences in controls were spontaneous in origin and commonly expected and observed in rats of this strain and age.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Neither signs of changes in hormonal balance (changes in endocrine and reproductive organs) nor chronic inflammation or immune modulatory effect were observed in pre-clinical toxicity studies. If observed, changes in clinical chemistry parameters were mild in magnitude and reversible after recovery. Further, linerixibat was negative for genotoxicity in vitro.

In TK analysis in general, in all species (rodents, human) the systemic exposure of linerixibat was minimal (near LoQ) after oral administration with a bioavailability below 1 %. The minimal tissue distribution after oral administration was restricted to the GI tract (small intestine) and the liver. Due to the minimal absorption TK parameters (tmax, Cmax, AUC) were highly variable and only nonconsistent conclusions regarding the systemic exposure, dose proportionality, sex differences, and changes in exposure over time could be predicted in the same species. Therefore, the data should be interpretated with caution. Due to non-consistent sex differences, the exposure calculations were gender averaged. No differences in toxicokinetic parameters were observed compared to the chronic toxicity studies conducted in rodents. Gender combined safety margins based on exposure (Cmax/AUC) were 30/5-fold for mice and 120/29-fold for rats to the maximal recommended clinical dose of 40 mg/bid (Cmax 922 pg/ml, AUC 22.12 ng h/ml).

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

In the fertility and early embryonic development study there was no effect on general toxicity, gonadal function, mating behaviour, implantation, and general fertility of male and female rats.

Embryofoetal development was not affected in rats. Abortions were noted in rabbits in the high dose group likely due to maternal toxicity and not clinically relevant. In addition, there were no adverse effects on prenatal and postnatal development including sexual maturation and reproductive capacity noted in the offspring of dams exposed to linerixibat during gestation and lactation. Plasma concentrations after maternal dosing of linerixibat in pups on PND10 ranged from &lt;1.00 ng/mL up to 15.3 ng/mL at 1000 mg/kg/day, indicating lactational exposure to linerixibat via milk.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

These findings are adequately described in the SmPC and PL.

## 4.4.6. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

Exposure multiples to the clinical human exposure at the end of the general toxicity studies of the longest duration in rats (26 weeks) and dogs (39 weeks) are 65- and 62-fold based on AUC, respectively, and based on Cmax 941- and 920-fold, respectively. These safety margins are considered relatively large and do not raise concerns regarding the intended clinical use of linerixibat.

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

The local tolerance in the gastrointestinal tract after oral administration of linerixibat was evaluated in the repeat dose toxicity studies. No stand-alone studies evaluating local tolerance were conducted, which is agreed.

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

## Assessment of Phototocicity

A radiolabelled excretion mass balance and tissue distribution study (2018N370009, quantitative whole-body autoradiography) in male pigmented Long-Evans rats revealed no distribution of linerixibat to melanin containing tissues. No radioactivity was detectable in the skin or eyes after an oral single dose of 10 mg/kg (14C-linerixibat). Maximum absorptions of linerixibat in the UV-Vis spectrum (3.2 S.1.3. General properties) were measured at approximately 208 nm, 238 nm, and 272 nm (shoulder) with corresponding molar extinction coefficients (MEC) of about 40600, 16000, and 3220 l mol -1 cm -1 and thus, according to the current ICH S10 guideline no photosafety testing is requested. Therefore, with respect to all available data a potential for phototoxicity of linerixibat is considered low.

## Assessment of potential genotoxic Impurities

If the quantity of a respective genotoxic impurity that appeared in drug starting materials, in synthesis, as intermediates, or as synthetic precursors, exceeded the ICH Q3A reporting threshold (about 80 impurities) the genotoxic potential was assessed in an in-silico assessment with the complementary Quantitative Structure-Activity Relationship ((Q)SAR) methodologies DEREK™. Most of the ones found negative with both methods are kept below the identification limit of 0.10% and are of no concern. A few were found positive for one of both methods and are kept well below the TTC (tertbutyl hydroperoxide), below the PDE (cobalt), or checked with the Ames bacterial mutagenicity test and/or mouse lymphoma L5178Y TK+/- test by new studies or literature (GW568450, GSK3543032, chloroacetonitrile, benzoyl chloride, and thionyl chloride and found negative. Four non-mutagenic impurities are specified as not greater than the qualification limit of 0.15% (GSK2413434, GSK4045118, GSK4505892 and GSK3521491), and two non-mutagenic chiral impurities are specified as not greater 0.3% (GSK2592213 and GSK3664544). These last two impurities were present in sufficient amounts in the repeat-dose toxicity studies for qualification conform ICHQ3A(R2).

## Assessment of nitrosamine Impurities

The mono-nitrosamine impurities and the di-nitrosamine, sharing the position of the two N-nitroso groups with the two mono-nitrosamines, were assessed for their mutagenic potential in accordance with the ICH M7 (R1) guidance and the Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products which included in silico methods, enhanced AMES tests and in vivo mutagenicity studies (transgenic rodent (TGR) mutation assay, Pig-A assay). In addition, in vitro metabolism was analysed with induced rat and hamster liver S9 fractions for detection of formation of N-dealkylation- and hydrolysis-metabolites.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The nitrosamines were first assessed for mutagenicity in silico in two QSAR models; Derek Nexus as expert rule-based model and Leadscope as statistical-based model. Reports were submitted for assessment for both models. In Derek, two nitrosamine impurities were plausible for in vitro and in vivo mutagenicity, while one nitrosamine impurity was negative. In Leadscope all nitrosamines were ruled Indeterminate, which indicates there was either not enough evidence in the model or the results were conflicting regarding mutagenicity. However, since the outcome in Derek for two nitrosamines was plausible, mutagenicity cannot be excluded based on the in-silico outcomes.

Similarity assessment (based on global structural similarity of the Lhasa Carcinogenicity Database using the default similarity score of 80%), did not provide solid evidence for an acceptable intake level. However, structurally similar compounds indicated that the alpha-carbon tertiary butyl substitution of the nitrosamine group may reduce rodent carcinogenicity.

In vitro metabolism of linerixibat and the three nitrosamines was studied with induced rat and hamster liver S9 fractions and simulated gastric fluid, simulated intestinal fluid, and mouse plasma. No Ndealkylation- or hydrolysis metabolites were detectable predictive of no alpha-hydroxylation adjacent to the N-nitroso-function, indicating a low potential of metabolic activation.

In vitro, all three metabolites were tested negative for mutations in an GLP conform enhanced AMES test with a full complement of bacterial strains [(TA100, TA1535, TA1537, TA98 and WP2 uvrA (pKM101) + S9 (10/30) % rat/hamster, 60 min pre-incubation method, - S9 plate incorporation method].

An in vivo GLP-conform TGR assay was performed to evaluate the potential of the two mononitrosamine impurities to induce gene mutation in the lacZ transgene within the liver, glandular stomach, small intestine (ileum and jejunum) and large intestine (colon) from treated Muta™Mice. For the one mono-nitroamine impurity also a Pig-A analysis with blood samples was conducted. For the other mon-nitrosamine impurity blood samples were also collected but not analyzed.

One was negative for the induction of mutant frequencies at the lacZ gene in all tested tissues, and for the induction of Pig-a gene mutations in either mature erythrocytes (RBC), or immature reticulocytes (RET) in peripheral blood, at oral doses up to 500 mg/kg/d.

One was negative to induce gene mutation in the lacZ transgene (oral dose up to 200 mg/kg/d) except for a small statistically significant (P≤0.05) linear trend observed in the stomach. One animal in the 200 mg/kg/d dose group was slightly outside the historical vehicle control. It was considered by the study director that these increases were of no biological relevance.

The di-nitrosamine was not tested in vivo.

Given the negative results of all three N-nitrosamines in the enhanced Ames test, no indication of alpha hydroxylation and the negative TGR assays of the two mono-nitrosamines, it is concluded by the CHMP, that all three nitrosamines can be regarded as non-mutagenic impurities.

During the ongoing procedure feedback was also received from the Nitrosamine Operational Expert Group (NSOEG) of the Non-clinical Working Party:

\"For the mono-nitrosamine impurities it can be agreed that they are non-mutagenic and limits according to ICH Q3A/B are acceptable.\"

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Regarding the third nitrosamine, di-nitrosamine impurity, of note, in a previous Scientific Advice (EMA/SA/0000131569), it was suggested that if one of the mono-nitrsamine and the di-nitrosamine impurities are tested in an in vivo TGR assay and show negative results, these impurities are sufficiently de-risked to consider it class 5 and control it according to ICH Q3A/B. In this case a test with the di-nitrosamine impurity only would suffice, since this impurity contains two nitroso groups, one of which is shared with one of the mono-nitrosamine impurties. Since the remaining structure of the molecules is the same in both impurities the negative result for di-nitrosamine impurity could be extrapolated to mono-nitrosamine impurity. Instead, the Applicant conducted the in vivo test with the mono-nitrosamine impurity. An in vivo test only with only the di-nitrosamine impurity would have been more convenient and in line with 3Rs as it contains both nitroso-groups shared with the other two nitrosamines. However, at the time of the advice, the in vivo study with the other mono-nitrosamine impurity was already ongoing.

Nevertheless, given the negative in vivo data with both the mono-nitrosamine impurities, with support of the negative in vitro metabolism data and negative EAT (albeit with limitations), and taking into account chemical aspects regarding potency (the nitroso-group next to the tert-butyl group will most likely not be activated, and based on the negative in vivo result of the most potent nitroso-group in the mono-nitrosamine impurity, it can be anticipated that the two nitroso-groups in the di-nitrosamine impurity are not expected to increase the potency of this nitrosamine), it can be agreed that also the di-nitrosamine impurity is not mutagenic and that ICH Q3A/B qualification limits are acceptable.\"

Taken together, it can be concluded that all three nitrosamines are not mutagenic and can be controlled at Q3 A/B levels.

## 4.4.9. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The applicant provided a complete Phase I assessment as well as valid PBT-Screening. The refined PECsurface water value of 8.12 ng/L does not exceed the action limit of 10 ng/L for the requirement of a Phase II assessment.

PBT-Screening provided a logKow below 4.5 for all relevant pH-values. Further PBT-Assessment is not necessary.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

Patients with cholestatic pruritus have significantly elevated levels of serum bile acids (BA) when compared to cholestatic patients without pruritus (Kremer, 2010). There are several publications referred to by the Applicant that indicate potential mechanisms by which linerixibat could reduce serum BAs and resulting decrease in pruritus. These include a reduction of activity and expression of autotaxin, the enzyme that produces the known pruritogen lysophosphatidic acid, or a reduced activation of the BA receptor FXR and subsequent reduction in the release of fibroblast growth factor 19 (FGF19) from intestinal epithelial cells into the plasma (Xu, 2023; Schramm, 2022; Nevens, 2016; Hegade, 2017; Hegade, 2019; Levy, 2023). Unfortunately, there was no data in the non- clinical dossier on these potential mechanisms involved in the reduction of BA-related pruritus.

In several primary pharmacology animal models, linerixibat also significantly increased biomarkers associated with type 2 diabetes including active and total GLP-1, reduced plasma TG, decreased plasma insulin and reduced gastric inhibitory polypeptide (GIP). For this indication, these parameters are taken into account as secondary pharmacodynamic effects.

Non-clinical data showed inhibition of IBAT and increased excretion of bile acids. Although it is known that serum BA levels are linked to itch in patients with cholestatic pruritus, no link between excreted bile acids and pruritus was established in the non-clinical dossier. Thus, both the mechanism of action and proof of concept of pruritus have been limitedly demonstrated in the non-clinical dossier. Whether this increased faecal BA excretion eventually leads to an improvement of cholestatic pruritus in patients with primary biliary cholangitis is therefore to be demonstrated clinically.

Considering the very low systemic clinical exposure (1980 pg/ml in patients with moderate hepatic impairment), linerixibat has been tested sufficiently regarding secondary pharmacodynamics and risks for offtarget pharmacological activity are not anticipated based on these assays.

Overall, the safety pharmacology studies do not indicate that linerixibat would cause clinically relevant neurobehavioural, respiratory or cardiovascular effects. It should be noted that very high variation in exposure is found among individual animals and among experiments. From a toxicology perspective the relevance of the obtained exposures may impact the discussed safety margins.

## harmacokinetics

## Analytical methods

Analytical methods for Linerixibat in mouse, rat, rabbit, dog, and monkey plasma were validated by HPLCMS/MS, meeting ICH M10 criteria for accuracy, precision, linearity, and stability.

## Absorption

## Single dose administration:

Single dose administration studies in mice, rats, and monkeys are limited to male animals. In rabbits and dogs males and females were investigated. Investigations were generally performed with small study groups. Therefore, gender differences could not be investigated in this set of experiments. In rats and dogs (studies no.: 2011N112040 and 2011N111915) blood was sampled from the circulation and the portal vein. In the circulation the plasma concentration of linerixibat was significant lower (not detectable in rats or very low in dogs) than in the portal vein, indicating some uptake and elimination within the hepato-biliary system.

The Applicant's conclusions that the volume of distribution was high and that the systemic clearance was low to moderate across non-clinical species following single-dose intravenous administration are not fully agreed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In rats and dogs plasma clearance was high, exceeding hepatic blood flow in the respective species (55 and 30 ml/min/kg respectively) (Davies 1993). In monkeys mean plasma clearance was moderate with approximately 70% of the hepatic blood flow. The mean volume of distribution was approximately 3 times higher in rats and dogs as the extracellular fluid volume (0.4 and 0.3 l/kg rats and dogs respectively), suggesting possible tissue distribution into tissues in rats and dogs. In monkeys the volume of distribution equals approximately the extracellular fluid volume. Relevant tissue distribution is therefore not anticipated in monkeys. This issue does not impact the benefit risk evaluation and does not trigger another concern, especially in the light of the low plasma concentration after oral administration and the insignificant distribution shown in the distribution studies. Overall, Human data (from the radiolabeled study) are consistent with nonclinical species showing a minimally absorbed compound (fraction absorbed = 0.167%), with very low oral bioavailability (0.05%).

## Repeated dose administration

Exposure to linerixibat was highly variable between individuals of a study group and does not allow firm conclusions on dose proportionality of systemic exposure, sex or time dependence. This finding is as far as investigated in agreement with the results of the single dose administration studies and believed to be a result of the low intestinal absorption. No relationship to formulation or feeding status is apparent. The toxicokinetic results of a toxicity study in rats (GlaxoSmithKline Document Number RD2010/00492/00 Study ID R42649. GSK2330672B: 14-Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study in Rats Followed by at least a 2-Week Recovery Period (GlaxoSmithKline Study No. R42649, report Date 11 March 2011) was not presented in the tabulated summary, because of the permanent presence of linerixibat in the vehicle control groups. According to the study reports of studies 2010N109877 and RD2010/00492/00 the contamination was further investigated, and it was concluded that contamination of the samples occurs ex-vivo likely during the toxicokinetic blood sample collection procedure. Apparently, the problem was solved using another room for the ex-vivo handling of blood samples. An additional study (2010N108008) investigating contamination of control groups in rats was submitted in the toxicology section but was not further discussed or taken into account. Furthermore, contamination of control groups is also described for dogs (RD2010/00491, 2014N221497 and 2019N405134). In the study reports it is concluded that these findings do not affect the outcome of the studies. Overall an in depth evaluation of these findings as postulated by the Guideline on the evaluation of control samples in nonclinical safety studies: checking for contamination with the test substance (CPMP/SWP/1094/04) is not provided since such findings should also be stated in the Written Summaries and highlighted in the Nonclinical Overview where discussion of their relevance and impact on the validity of the study, on the interpretation of the results and on the human risk assessment is expected. Although detailed information on which studies are affected is missing from the summaries and a detailed discussion is missing from the nonclinical overview overall, appropriate measures were obviously taken at the study implementation level. Further actions are therefore not required.

## Distribution

Binding of linerixibat to plasmatic or microsomal proteins, partioning into blood cells and P-glykoprotein (P-gp) associated transmission over cell monolayer was investigated in-vitro. The studies indicate a low protein binding capacity (71.0- 80.6 % in the final study with 14 C-linerixibat). In preliminary studies similar results were obtained. Blood-to-plasma partioning investigations showed a low binding ratio (0.635 to 0.840). Permeability studies with P-gp in stable transfected human Multi-Drug Resistance 1 - Madin Darby Canine Kidney (hMDR1-MDCK) indicate a low permeability. In-vivo investigations with 14 C-Linerixibat in male rats showed that linerixibat is not largely distributed in the body. Linerixibat was mostly confined within the gastrointestinal tract. In bile duct cannulated animals some radioactivity could be found in liver and small intestine.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In-vivo studies were performed in males only and distribution into female reproductive organs could not be assessed. In consideration of the low bioavailability, the absence of clear sex differences in the absorption studies and the lack of distribution into any tissue except (small amounts) in the liver this is considered acceptable.

Distribution of linerixibat into the milk of lactating animals was not investigated. However, plasmatic concentration of suckling pups was investigated and did not indicate relevant exposure.

## Metabolism

Metabolic breakdown of linerixibat was investigated in-vitro and in-vivo in several models.

The applicant concludes from these studies, that linerixibat metabolism was minimal in humans following both oral and intravenous administration of 14 C-linerixibat. Instead, elimination of an i.v. dose in humans occurred by excretion of unchanged parent drug via the biliary/fecal and urinary routes.

Metabolism of oral 14 C-linerixibat in mice and rats was similarly low, although it was relatively higher in dogs.

The oxidative metabolites observed in-vitro and in-vivo were fundamentally consistent between nonclinical species and humans. No major human- metabolites were observed, and there was no evidence of reactive metabolite formation.

The applicant´s view was initially not completely agreed to as significant shares of moiety G were found in human plasma However finally, after assessment of the applicant´s response to a request the applicant´s view is shared that moiety G is most likely a radiochemical impurity or a metabolite thereof and not a metabolite of linerixibat.

Furthermore, the detection of circulating metabolites in animals is limited to dogs. In pooled plasma samples of dogs, unchanged linerixibat (9 to 13% of plasma radioactivity) and mono-oxygenated metabolites predominated (M3, M4, and M5; 17 to 30%, 11 to 17%, and 7 to 11% of the plasma radioactivity, respectively). In human studies with radioactive linerixibat the main circulating metabolites are M5 and M6 and the moiety G. M5 is clearly a metabolite in dogs and moiety G, as concluded above, does not require no further investigations. However, the formation of M6 could not be shown in rats and dogs. Overall, the applicant´s view is shared, that M6 is a minor human metabolite requiring no further independent investigations.

Initially, one concern was raised regarding human metabolites M8, M9, and M10. The applicant clarified that theses metabolites are unique human faecal metabolites, which were not detected in plasmatic samples of the species investigated. The origin of theses metabolites remains unclear. However microbiological degradation is highly unlikely. The share of these metabolites is very small (each below 1% of total gastrointestinal exposure to linerixibat). Therefore, further investigations are not considered necessary.

## Excretion

The applicant´s view is agreed to. In mice, rats and dogs absorption of linerixibat is low after oral administration. A study in rats showed a slight increased linerixibat concentration in the portal vein in comparison to the jugular vein indicating the ability of hepatobiliary excretion of linerixibat. Similar, investigations in bile duct cannulated rats and dogs showed small shares of radioactivity recovered from bile (3% and 6.66% for rats and dogs respectively). Consequently, linerixibat is the main compound recovered from faeces in nonclinical species. The amounts are between 92% (dog) and 99% (rat) in intact animals. Urinary excretion was below 1% in all species investigated using intact animals.

The investigation in humans after i.v. administration of linerixibat shows similar results. Linerixibat is primarily excreted by faecal elimination (69%) and only a small amount (17%) is urinarily excreted, indicating a very similar excretion pattern between humans and nonclinical species.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

The applicant has not submitted clinical module 5.3.2. Therefore, in-vitro drug interaction studies are limited to the non-clinical investigations presented above. The applicant's conclusions were initially questioned from a non-clinical perspective. Upon request, the applicant submitted an additional study report [2025N567499] evaluating linerixibat's potential for clinically relevant drug interactions within a plasma concentration range of 0.118 to 0.158 ng/mL (0.000216-0.000289 µM). The report is not entirely satisfactory, as the investigated concentration range is very limited. However, the issue is not pursued further from the non-clinical point of view. Clinically relevant questions concerning drug interactions will be discussed in the clinical section of the current report. Linerixibat was an apparent inactivator of CYP3A4, with a maximal rate constant of inactivation (kinact) of 0.16 min -1 and a concentration of linerixibat required to achieving half-maximal inactivation (KI) of 1305 µM, resulting in inactivation efficiency (kinact/KI) of 0.00012 min -1 µM -1 for CYP3A4. The average percent binding of linerixibat with human liver microsomes following a 5-hr incubation period at 37˚C using equilibrium dialysis method was determined to be 54% and fu,mic was 0.46 (in report 2025N567499 slightly

Linerixibat inhibited OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OAT3 in vitro. For OAT3 no IC50 could be calculated and the applicant´s position is shared that in this case further investigations are not necessary.

In-vitro studies investigating BSEP are not provided. However, in one study potential effects of linerixibat on bile salts was investigated with obetocholic acid as model substance (2017N319427), which show a pharmacodynamic interaction.

different values were used). Species differences

Overall, rats are the most relevant nonclinical species for linerixibat safety assessment due to their pharmacokinetic profile being more representative of humans. Monkeys showed very low systemic exposure, limiting their utility, while dogs exhibited extensive metabolism and inconsistent exposure. Across nonclinical species, linerixibat exhibited very low oral bioavailability (&lt;1% in rats, and dogs), driven primarily by poor gastrointestinal absorption and extensive first-pass hepatic extraction. Human data are consistent with nonclinical species showing a minimally absorbed compound (fraction absorbed = 0.167%), with very low oral bioavailability (0.05%). Due to the very low exposure levels and high variability observed across species, interpretation of the pharmacokinetic data should be approached with caution.

## Toxicology

## Single dose toxicity

It is agreed with the Applicant that single oral doses ≤1000 mg/kg in dogs and IV doses ≤60 mg/kg in rats and dogs were associated with mild gastrointestinal observations in dogs, and no other significant signs of toxicity were observed.

## Repeat dose toxicity

In the mouse 4-week studies epithelial degeneration/necrosis, inflammation, and/or exudate in the nasal turbinate and/or nasopharynx were observed at ≥100 mg/kg, with apparent clinical signs noted at ≥500 mg/kg, including decreased general activity, piloerection, hunched posture, squinted eye(s), sternal recumbency, ataxia, labored/irregular and/or audible respiration, swollen abdomen, squinted eyes, cool to the touch, excessive lacrimation, liquid/mucoid and/or yellow feces and /or thin appearance. The clinical observations, microscopic findings, and moribund conditions were considered due to gavage-related reflux and irritant response in nasal cavity to linerixibat. Similar effects are described in the 104-week carcinogenicity study in rats, but not in the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats. These findings are not considered clinically relevant as there is no clinical correlate for this method of gavage dosing.

In the 13-week Wistar Han rat study minimal to slight mucosal basophilia/hypertrophy was observed in the large intestine at doses ≥10 mg/kg/day and in the 26-week study at doses ≥200 mg/kg/day with no evidence of degeneration/necrosis, hyperplasia or increased inflammation. These microscopic findings were considered non-adverse mainly because they were generally minimal to slight in severity and were completely reversible after a 6-week recovery period. They did not show progression in severity when linerixibat was given to rats for 2 years. No such findings were seen in the dog. In the dog 14-day study, focal, minimal to mild haemorrhage was observed in the mucosa of the colon and/or rectum of males given ≥100 mg/kg/day and females given 20 or 1000 mg/kg/day. The mucosal haemorrhages were considered non-adverse mainly because they were very small focal lesions and occurred in the absence of microscopic evidence of vascular injury. In the dog 13- and 39-week studies, there were no microscopic changes at doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. Altered faeces were seen in general toxicity studies in all species investigated (mouse, rat and dog). In the 13- and 26-week rat and 13- and 39-week dog studies, abnormal faeces (nonformed, liquid) were observed at a dose of 1000 mg/kg/day in rats and at 500 mg/kg/day and ≥50 mg/kg/day, respectively, in dogs. In dogs in the 14-Day and 13-week-studies a dose-dependent emesis was seen, which was not seen in the 26-week dog study employing the same doses as in the 13-week study. In the dog, a mild transient reduction in body weight was seen in the 39-week study. The Applicant´s conclusion that the intestinal effects observed in toxicology studies with linerixibat are consistent with irritation and may be related to the physicochemical properties of linerixibat itself or due to changes in bile acid flux through the GI tract is considered plausible. These observations were considered non-adverse and the NOAELs in the rat 26-week and dog 39-week oral studies were 1000 and 500 mg/kg/day, respectively, the highest doses administered. Based on the toxicokinetic data of all the repeat dose toxicity studies, there existed high variability (e.g. 23fold differences) in the systemic exposure of linerixibat among individuals after oral administration, resulting in overlapping exposures between lower and higher dose groups. This is likely due to the low absorption and high variability in absorption of this compound in animals. Based on clinical data, similar large variability in systemic exposure and low absorption occurred to humans. The potential of the compound to induce systemic toxicity at high systemic exposure (high absorption) was studied in a non-GLP-compliant repeat-dose intravenous (IV) study in rats (R42625). Rats receiving linerixibat at up to 60 mg/kg/day for 7 days via IV administration (with an exposure margin of ≥500-fold compared to the clinical AUC of 22.1 ng*h/mL as stated by the Applicant) only demonstrated increased incidence of non-adverse mixed-cell mucosal inflammatory infiltrate in the cecum without overt systemic toxicity. Based on the non-clinical toxicology data and the mechanism of action, linerixibat did not seem to cause systemic toxicity. Given 1) the generally low absorption and the subsequent low systemic exposure, 2) the results of IV toxicity study in rats, and 3) the mechanism of action of the compound (i.e. intended to \"locally\" bind to ileum bile acid transporters), it is considered that systemic toxicity is probably not a concern for humans (e.g. reproductive toxicity, safety pharmacology) and that linerixibat is unlikely to be a systemic carcinogen. Local toxicity in the gastrointestinal tract is considered very important for conclusions on human safety, and the use of exposure margins to determine the clinical relevance of local effects is problematic. Since there is no data on local concentration of linerixibat in the gut, the doses used and the NOAELs in the non-clinical toxicity studies are considered more relevant than exposure margins during toxicological interpretation of local effects. The human AUC of 22.1 ng*h/ml used for the exposure multiple calculations is based on the mean concentration at 1-3 hrs post-dose at Week 8 of the clinical study 212620 multiplied by 24 hours. This mean concentration is 922.2 pg/ml with an SD of 2068 pg/ml (n=52), which is therefore highly inaccurate, probably caused by linerixibat's extremely low oral bioavailability. This means that the calculated exposure multiples are very inaccurate. However, because the doses of the exposure multiples of the NOAELs in the general toxicity studies of the longest duration in rats (26 weeks) and dogs (39 weeks) are 625- and 312-fold higher resp. then the human dose, and the exposure multiples are 65- and 62-fold resp., and therefore sufficiently high, it is acceptable that these roughly estimated safety margins can be considered large enough to expect safe clinical use in humans.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Genotoxicity

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) was tested for genotoxicity in vitro (gene mutation test in bacteria, mutation test in mammalian cells) and in vivo (rats, micronucleus test) in accordance with ICH S2(R1) guidelines and GLP requirements and was negative in all cases. The risk of genotoxicity from the administration of Linerixibat is considered low and adequately addressed.

## Carcinogenicity

Linerixibat was tested negative in a 6-months oral carcinogenicity study in hemizygous (RasH2-H) mice (2021N476126) and a 2-year oral carcinogenicity study in rats (2020N454153) in accordance with the ICH S1 guidance and GLP requirements. No Linerixibat related proliferative, or neoplastic findings were detected in the target organs (gastrointestinal tract, liver) in rodents. All findings were minimal to slight, showed no evidence of progression over time or increasing study duration, were (partly, dependent on duration of recovery period) reversible and consistent with well-known GI effects of ileal bile acid transporter inhibitors (Al-Dury 2018) and adaptive physiologic response in the liver (Thoolen 2010. Harada 1999). Therefore, the risk of carcinogenicity from oral linerixibat administration is considered low and sufficiently addressed.

## Reproductive and developmental toxicity

In the fertility and early embryonic development study there was no effect on general toxicity, gonadal function, mating behaviour, implantation, and general fertility of male and female rats. Embryofoetal development was not affected in rats. Abortions were noted in rabbits in the high dose group likely due to maternal toxicity and are considered not clinically relevant.

In addition, there were no adverse effects on prenatal and postnatal development including sexual maturation and reproductive capacity noted in the offspring of dams exposed to linerixibat during gestation and lactation. Plasma concentrations after maternal dosing of linerixibat in pups indicating lactational exposure to linerixibat via milk. These findings are adequately described in the SmPC and PL.

As the product is not intended for paediatric use, it is agreed that no juvenile toxicity studies are needed.

Due to redundancy in information some revisions concerning carcinogenicity, genotoxicity and reproductive and developmental toxicity in Section 5.3 \"Preclinical safety data\" of the product information were implemented.

## Immunotoxicity

The mechanism of action, non-clinical and clinical data so far did not indicate effects on white blood cell parameters or platelet counts. The potential risk of immunotoxicity was considered limited. The lack of standalone toxicity study is agreed.

## Local tolerance

The local tolerance in the gastrointestinal tract after oral administration of linerixibat was evaluated in the repeat dose toxicity studies. No stand-alone studies evaluating local tolerance were conducted, which is agreed.

## Phototoxicity

No phototoxicity testing was conducted given that linerixibat does not absorb light in the 290 to 700 nm wavelength range and no photosafety concern was identified. This is agreed.

The ERA provided is considered complete and acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

Linerixibat is a potent and selective reversible inhibitor of the IBAT, thereby inhibiting reabsorption of BA in the distal ileum.

In vivo the inhibition of the IBAT has been demonstrated in non-clinical animal studies. Increase in faecal elimination of bile acids e.g. in rats is in line with the intestinal inhibition of the IBAT and a subsequent inhibition of bile acid reabsorption. Whether this leads to a reduction in pruritis was not evaluated in nonclinical models.

Secondary pharmacology screening studies did not cause concerns regarding off-target activity.

Safety pharmacology studies did not raise concerns regarding effects of linerixibat on CNS, respiratory and cardiovascular functions during clinical use.

The pharmacokinetics and toxicokinetics from the pivotal pre-clinical animal species is similar to the kinetics of linerixibat in humans. The rat and dog are acceptable species as pivotal toxicity species.

Based on systemic exposure the data from general toxicology studies, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity, developmental and reproductive toxicity studies do not raise concerns regarding the intended clinical use of linerixibat.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>
