## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

Linerixibat PK was evaluated at several oral doses throughout clinical development. The clinical pharmacokinetics of linerixibat has been investigated in 11 clinical studies, as well as dose-response modelling that integrated data across studies. The 11 clinical studies comprised: eight Phase 1 studies, including single and repeat-dose studies, oral bioavailability/mass balance, food effect assessments, drug interaction studies, and studies including special populations (Japanese participants and hepatic impairment); two Phase 2 studies conducted in adult participants with cholestatic pruritus in primary biliary cholangitis for 2 weeks and 12 weeks; and one Phase 3 study in adult participants with cholestatic pruritus in primary biliary cholangitis.

## 5.2.1. Methods

The bioanalytical methods used for quantification of linerixibat in human plasma and urine and its metabolites and co-medication such as UDCA and its conjugates GUDCA and TUDCA were validated in terms of selectivity, linearity, sensitivity, precision, and accuracy over the appropriate concentration range in quantifying these analytes in samples derived from studies conducted during the clinical development program. The original method for linerixibat (GK3HPP) had a range of 1 to 1000 ng/mL, the more sensitive method (GK3LHPP) a range of 10 to 10,000 pg/mL. Only the analytical method GK3LHPP with the LLOQ of 10 pg/mL resulted in sufficient quantifiable plasma samples and the determination of PK parameters in clinical studies. However, analytical method GK3LHPP showed carryover effects during method validation and during study subject sample analyses.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a potent and selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT. It has been developed by GSK for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in patients with PBC.

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

Non-compartmental data analysis was used to determine the PK parameters. Linerixibat is minimally absorbed, therefore, no in dept PK analysis, including population PK, was possible.

Exposure-response modelling was not possible, since measurable plasma concentrations of linerixibat were not measurable or very low to (very low oral bioavailability). Therefore, the administered linerixibat dose was used to investigate the relationship with efficacy and safety. For efficacy, the dose relationship with worst itch (WI-NSR) and total serum bile acid (TSBA) concentrations were investigated. For safety, the dose relationship with diarrhoea was investigated. With increasing dose, the risk of diarrhoea increased.

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

Absorption of linerixibat is very limited and therefore linerixibat plasma concentrations were low (pg range). After a single oral dose of 40 mg, the Cmax ranges from 17.3-55.8 pg/mL under fasted conditions. After repeated oral dosing with 40 mg twice daily (independent of food intake), the Cmax ranges from 103-343 pg/mL and the AUC0-12h ranges from 626-1877 pg × h/mL. The inter-individual variability was high (up to 419%).

Based on the short t½ following IV administration (0.828 h) when compared to longer t½ following oral administration (6.76 h), it can be concluded that linerixibat exhibited absorption rate-limited flip-flop kinetics.

The absolute bioavailability of linerixibat is very low (F = 0.05% with a range of 0.02-0.14%). This is due to the low absorption of linerixibat and the high first pass metabolism. The low absorption is in-line with the low permeability observed in vitro for linerixibat.

Cmax and AUC were lower when linerixibat was administered under fed compared to fasted conditions. However, a food effect on Cmax and AUC0-t is difficult to interpret given the high number of plasma PK samples that were BLQ. The effect of other meal types on the exposure to linerixibat is unknown, but expected to be between a high-fat, high-caloric meal and fasted. The activity of linerixibat is local in the ileum and therefore fasted or fed conditions is not expected to have a clinical effect on the efficacy of linerixibat.

No bioequivalence studies were performed. During development, several formulations of linerixibat were used including oral solutions (0.06 and 1.5 mg/g and 1.5 or 2 mg/mL) and tablets containing 10

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

mg, 40 mg and 45 mg. The 40 mg is to-be-marketed formulation. The absorption is low and the formulation is not expected to affect the absorption.

## 5.2.2.4. Distribution

The plasma protein binding of linerixibat ranged from 64.6 to 77.9% using equilibrium dialysis and was not concentration dependent over a concentration range of 30-1000 ng/mL. The blood to plasma ratio ranged from 0.609 to 1.01, indicating that linerixibat does not accumulate in red blood cells.

The volume of distribution of linerixibat is 16.3 L following an IV microdose. Linerixibat is likely distributed into extracellular fluid, but not extensively. As the systemic exposure is very low, the amounts of linerixibat in extracellular fluid is expected to be low.

## 5.2.2.5. Metabolism

Linerixibat is metabolised by CYP3A4 and not by CYP1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1, and 3A5. Following IV administration 2 metabolites were observed in plasma, both present for &lt;2% of the circulating radioactivity. Following oral dosing, no metabolites were observed in plasma samples up to 6 hours and urine samples up to 168 hours. Only 3 metabolites were observed in faeces samples up to 168 h (all ≤1% of the recovered radioactivity).

## 5.2.2.6. Transporters

Linerixibat is a substrate of OATP1B1 and 1B3, but not a substrate of P-glycoprotein, BCRP, and OATP2B1.

## 5.2.2.7. Elimination

After IV-administration of 100 μg linerixibat, 16.5% of the radioactivity was excreted in urine and 69.2% in faeces. Following oral dosing, approximately 97% was excreted in faeces and &lt;0.1% was excreted in urine. Overall, the IV and oral elimination indicate that absorbed linerixibat is mainly excreted via faeces and to a limited extent via urine.

The elimination half-life of linerixibat is 0.83 h following IV administration and 6.8 h following oral administration. The systemic clearance of linerixibat is high (61.8 L/h).

## 5.2.2.8. Dose proportionality and time dependency

Due to the very low absorption (and thus exposure) no dose-proportionality can be determined, but the exposure appears to increase with increasing oral dose.

Following repeated dosing of 40 mg twice daily a modest accumulation for the Cmax of 2.1- to 3.7-fold was observed and for the AUC0-12 of 3.1- to 4.8-fold was observed after 14 days. No PK data is available earlier than 14 days. It is therefore unknown when steady state plasma concentrations are reached. However, based on the elimination half-life (6.8 h), this is most likely reached within 14 days following twice daily dosing.

## 5.2.2.9. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

Linerixibat PK was investigated in 3 clinical studies in patients with primary biliary cholangitis. However, in study BAT117213 a not sensitive analytical method was used and therefore linerixibat could not be detected in sufficient samples. In the other two clinical samples, sparse samples were taken.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Mean plasma concentrations from samples taken 1-3 hours post-dose was 901.69 pg/mL to 1667.39 pg/mL across the sampling visits of Week 8 to 24. The linerixibat plasma concentrations in patients in study 201000 were similar with those observed in healthy subjects. In study 212620 plasma concentrations were higher than those observed for healthy subjects. In both study 201000 and 212620 the plasma concentrations were similar in week 8, week 12 and week 16.

The therapeutic window was 20 mg once daily to 90 mg once daily or 40 mg twice daily with the upper limited based on increase in diarrhoea.

## 5.2.2.10. Special populations

The PK of linerixibat in special populations was investigated in a dedicated clinical study in hepatic impairment (study 214899) and Japanese subjects (study 205808). No other dedicated clinical studies were conducted in special populations.

Based on patient data, mild renal impairment does not seem to affect the exposure to linerixibat.

The plasma exposure to linerixibat is ~12-fold higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function (10-fold for Cmax and 13 fold for AUC). Also, the urinary excretion is ~10-fold higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function. The higher exposure to linerixibat in subjects with hepatic impairment is likely due to an increase in oral absorption, as increased intestinal permeability has been reported with liver disease.

Due to the used analytical method (LLOQ = 1 ng/mL) no linerixibat plasma concentrations &gt;LLOQ were observed in the clinical study in healthy Japanese subjects. However, patients from Western countries, China and Japan were included in studies 201000 and 212620 and the plasma concentration observed between 1-3 h were compared between the different ethnic groups. No differences were observed in the PK between the Japanese, Chinese and other patients.

No effect of age or gender was observed based on the exposure in the clinical studies. No information was provided on the effect of body weight on the PK of linerixibat. Given the local activity, no effect of body weight on the effect of linerixibat is expected.

## 5.2.2.11. Pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic interaction studies

## DDIs as victim

Linerixibat is a substrate of the enzyme CYP3A4 and the transporters OATP1B1 and 1B3. No clinical DDI studies were conducted with linerixibat as victim of DDIs. Linerixibat undergoes minimal absorption and systemic metabolism after oral administration. Therefore, it is not expected that linerixibat is a victim of DDIs. However, medicinal products that could increase the permeability of the intestine could lead to a higher exposure.

## DDIs as perpetrator

Linerixibat was not an in vitro inhibitor of CYPs or other transporters at clinically relevant concentrations. Linerixibat was not an inducer via AhR, CAR or PXR at clinically relevant concentrations.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In vitro studies indicated that linerixibat binds to colesevelam which may result in deceased absorption of bile acid binding resins. In clinical trials, administration of bile acid binding resins (required to be at least 4 h before or after linerixibat) over 24 weeks did not affect total serum bile acid (TSBA) concentrations. Accordingly, it is recommended in the SmPC that linerixibat is taken at least 4 h before or after taking a bile acid binding resin, or at as great an interval as possible.

The effect of linerixibat on the absorption of obeticholic acid (including metabolites/conjugates) and ursodeoxycholic acid was investigated in clinical studies. Co-administration with linerixibat (40 mg twice daily) resulted in decreased absorption of total-obeticholic acid and obeticholic acid conjugates. Therefore, obeticholic acid is not recommended to be administered with linerixibat.

Linerixibat had no significant effect on alkaline phosphatase when co-administered with ursodeoxycholic acid. However, plasma concentrations of ursodeoxycholic acid, glyco-ursodeoxycholic acid and tauro-ursodeoxycholic acid Cmax and AUC were relevantly changed (overall decreased due to a decrease in concentrations in conjugates). Linerixibat may be co-administered with ursodeoxycholic acid. However, the clinical relevance of the changes in the profile of ursodeoxycholic acid and its conjugates remains unclear. The corresponding information, including a recommendation to monitor alkaline phosphatase concentrations, has been added to the SmPC.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a potent and selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT and interrupts the enterohepatic recirculation process and shunts unabsorbed bile acids for excretion in the faeces.

As a result of the reduced absorption of bile acids entering ileal enterocytes, intestinal FXR is not activated resulting in a decrease in FGF-19. FGF-19 is responsible for repressing bile acid synthesis in the liver. Linerixibat mediated FGF-19 reduction leads to a subsequent increase in cholesterol metabolism to bile acids as measured via an increase in serum C4, a surrogate marker for hepatic CYP7A1 activity. In addition, the increased hepatic FXR also increases LDL receptor leading to a subsequent decrease in serum total cholesterol and LDL.

With respect to cholestatic pruritus linerixibat decreases the autotaxin enzyme that produces another putative pruritogen, lysophosphatidic acid, and IL-31, a cytokine known to mediate pruritus in PBC.

Thus, from the mechanism of action linerixibat, some effect on pruritus via decreasing TSBAs, LPA (via measurement of autotaxin as a surrogate), and IL-31 might be expected.

The product has been specifically designed to have poor cellular permeability, complete dissolution at relevant GI pHs, chemical stability within the GI contents to facilitate interaction with its target at the brush border of enterocytes and to be minimally absorbed from the GI tract with minimal systemic exposure [Wu, 2013].

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

## Primary Pharmacology

Several PD endpoint values measured in the Phase 2 and Phase 3 clinical studies may be seen as concordant with the pharmacological mechanism of action. Collectively, these provide insight into the utility of the biomarkers to support the anticipated therapeutic effect based on overall population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Observed decreases in pruritus pathogenesis.

- TSBA: after a single dose of linerixibat, TSBA concentrations decreased indicating rapid inhibition of IBAT. Significantly reduced concentrations were sustained throughout the treatment period and returned towards baseline upon cessation of linerixibat dosing.
- Autotaxin: linerixibat (40 mg BID) significantly decreased serum concentrations of autotaxin (a surrogate of LPA) compared to placebo throughout the treatment period and returned to baseline upon cessation of linerixibat dosing.
- IL-31: linerixibat (40 mg BID) significantly decreased serum concentrations of IL-31 compared to placebo throughout the treatment period. IL-31 is a cytokine recently proposed to mediate pruritus in PBC.

Observed changes in biomarkers of target engagement.

- Due to linerixibat inhibition of bile acid absorption in the ileum, linerixibat decreased serum FGF-19 compared to baseline throughout the treatment period and returned towards baseline upon cessation of linerixibat dosing. Absorption of bile acids stimulate FXR activation, and synthesis of FGF-19 in ileal enterocytes, so a decrease in FGF-19 reflects reduced bile acid absorption.
- Consistent with the mechanism of action, linerixibat significantly increased serum C4 to steady state compared to baseline throughout the treatment period and returned towards baseline upon cessation of linerixibat dosing. Results indicate increased bile acid synthesis due to inhibition of bile acid absorption in the ileum.
- Numerical increases in HDL-cholesterol and decreases in LDL-cholesterol and total cholesterol were observed in GLIMMER along with similar trends in GLISTEN indicative of increased de novo synthesis of bile acids from their cholesterol precursor due to the reduced bile acid pool in the body.

In addition to these observed PD trends, model-based analyses were submitted to support the therapeutic effect of linerixibat. Consistent with the linerixibat mechanism of action, the K-PD model for TSBA predicted rapid and dose-dependent decreases in TSBA. At the to-be-marketed dose of 40 mg BID, a plateau in effect was reached by approximately 3 to 5 days of dosing. Predicted TSBA exposures (AUC[0-24]) significantly correlated with improvement in Weekly Itch Score from baseline in a timematched repeated measures regression analysis over the 24- and 12-week treatment periods of GLISTEN and GLIMMER, respectively.

## Secondary Pharmacology

No specific information concerning secondary pharmacology was provided in the provided clinical documents; information is limited on animal data only (for more details refer to non-clinical part of this AR).

Linerixibat did not show significant activity against a panel of over 100 additional receptors, ion channels, transporters, and enzymes as assessed by a series of binding, functional and enzyme activity assays (for more details please refer to the non-clinical part of this AR.)

Safety pharmacology studies did not raise concerns regarding effects of linerixibat on CNS, respiratory and cardiovascular functions during clinical use in animals.

From a clinical perspective relevant, secondary pharmacology effects in animals were observed with respect to the cardiac, gastrointestinal, CNS and cardiac system. But considering applied dose of 40 mg BID as reflected in the safety outcome of the main clinical trials (GLIMMER and GLISTEN) mainly the gastrointestinal effects may be of clinical relevance. However, it needs to be considered that the described effects and adverse events are not correlated with the blood PK but rather caused by local intestinal effects due to the mechanism of action.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In this context, it appears reassuring that the difference is extremely large between the applied 40 mg BID dose (TD: 80mg/day) in patients and the NOAEL dose of 100 mg/kg/day) evaluated in animals.

## 5.2.3.3. Pharmacodynamic interactions with other medicinal products or substances

The results from study BAT117213 do clearly show an interaction of linerixibat with the re-absorption of conjugated UCDA compounds. The Applicant refers to the scientific literature which allegedly demonstrates that efficacy of UDCA with regard to the conjugate species is dependent on reaching certain plasma levels (Dilger 2012).

This investigation tried to elucidate the patterns of biliary enrichment of different bile acid species by investigating the percentage of total biliary bile acids based on molar concentrations in cystic and duodenal bile. One of the results of this analysis revealed that after UDCA treatment, GUDCA predominated in each subject group with a fraction of more than one third of total bile acids, while TUDCA amounted to less than 5%. The study also concluded that there was a significant (albeit rather weak (r=0.43) correlation of UDCA in plasma and biliary enrichment, but a strong correlation between the sum of molar concentrations of UDCA and its conjugates given as fraction of total bile acids in plasma (r=0.73). The main conclusion with regard to the correlation of plasma concentrations and biliary enrichment (and thus presumed clinical effects) was that the relative fraction of UDCA together with its conjugates in plasma just before a dose (at Ctrough) was by far the most reliable predictor of biliary enrichment of UDCA.

The results of the study clearly show that the PK of UDCA and its metabolites are relevantly affected by linerixibat treatment. If any, the conclusion from the Dilger 2012 study with regard to the results of the PK analysis of study BAT117213 could be drawn, would be that the total UDCA species at Ctrough would be the appropriate analyte to base any claim of unaltered PD profile of UDCA upon.

The analysis of the course of ALP over time does indeed not show a clear tendency for consistent changes, and it rather appears to be a reflection of the variability of ALP course over time. It also remains questionable whether the observation period in this study of 2 weeks would be regarded to be sufficient to demonstrate relevant changes. Therefore, the ALP analysis from study BAT117213 is regarded to be of limited value.

The results from the GLIMMER (2b) study appear to be somewhat more reassuring regarding UDCA PD: The changes in ALP - while reflecting mostly variability and being based on partly a small sample size - rather point into the direction of overall improvement over the trial duration. At least there is no obvious deterioration of the disease activity markers observed.

Regarding UDCA (and conjugates) concentrations (hence the PK interaction) the following can be concluded: There was no clear concentration-time pattern and no consistent pattern of UDCA concentration differences across treatment groups. Box plots of UDCA concentrations were highly variable by visit when comparing within a treatment group. Box plots of GUDCA and TUDCA concentrations were also variable across all linerixibat treatment groups compared with placebo. Overall, the reduction of conjugated UDCA species could not clearly be confirmed in this study. However, this might reflect the missing standardisation of UDCA treatments (as compared to study BAT 117213) rather than \"real\" effects. A full reassurance of missing PK interaction cannot be deduced.

The GLISTEN study (phase 3) did not evaluate the course of the ALP/bilirubin markers as part of a potential interaction but rather refers to the demonstration of safety. Nevertheless, the results show some changes of the markers (not only ALP) that do not fully confirm that the course of the disease is not negatively influenced. However, from the data presented, it will remain unclear whether this relates to a PK/PD interaction with UDCA or is an \"intrinsic\" property of the compound. The reader is therefore referred to the safety evaluation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The potential interaction with BABRs was \"theoretically\" addressed with imposing a 4-hour period between intake of linerixibat and BABRs. Although included as stratification factor into the GLISTEN trial, only 10 patients in the active and placebo groups finally received such a concomitant medication. The evaluation of PK and PD was limited due to small sample (only 3 patients included in PK analysis), but the evaluation of PD markers (although also based on low numbers) did not reveal any hints for interaction when the BABR medication was taken 4 hours apart from linerixibat. A recommendation to follow 4-hour break between intake of linerixibat and BABRs is included in the PI.

The potential interaction with metformin is not really addressed by the Applicant. However, the dedicated study 200185 that includes a statistical evaluation is considered suitable to exclude a relevant interaction. This is not relevantly questioned by the results of the study 201351 which has a parallel group design, and low numbers of patients in each of the groups. Moreover, the maximum increase in AUC appears to be not greater than 10%, and 25% for Cmax, likely lacking clinical relevance. The non-presentation of the data in the overview and/or Summary documents is therefore considered acceptable.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

Typical exposure-response analyses were not possible for linerixibat due to the very low oral bioavailability (0.05%) and intermittently measurable, highly variable systemic concentrations (despite a very sensitive bioanalytical assay with an LLQ of 10 pg/mL). Therefore, a K-PD approach was implemented to characterize the dose-response relationship over time for TSBA concentrations and WI-NRS itch scores. In addition, a proportional odds model was developed to predict the probability of a score of 1-7 (\"no discomfort at all\" to \"very severe discomfort\") for GSRS Question 11 \"Have you been bothered by diarrhoea during the past week?\" in relation to linerixibat dose. The K-PD modelling dataset included pooled data across 7 linerixibat studies for any participants who received at least one dose of study drug and had at least one modelling endpoint measurement. For GLISTEN, only Part A data was included in the pooled dataset:

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

The applicant explained that the proposed dose of linerixibat (40 mg BID) has been selected in order to maximize itch benefit and balance tolerability based on the collective data across the Phase 1-3 studies as well as K-PD modelling and simulation. The K-PD models provided a quantitative framework to assess the balance of efficacy and tolerability.

With respect to the efficacy PD marker, the applicant reports that in GLIMMER the dose-dependent decreases in itch scores in the 40 mg BID, 90 mg BID and 180 mg QD dose cohorts showed improvement in Monthly Itch Score relative to placebo over 12 weeks of treatment. The 40 mg BID cohort itch response over 12 weeks showed less variability than other dose cohorts and started early as shown by Weekly Itch Score reduction as early as the first week. Model predictions supported the conclusion that a 90 mg QD dose was probably slightly less effective than the 40 mg BID cohort on average, supporting selection of a BID dose regimen rather than QD.

With respect to safety, the provided data showed that tolerability of the 40 mg BID dose was better than the 90mg QD, 90 mg BID and 180 QD doses. There were dose-dependent trends in the frequency of reporting the system organ class of GI disorders including diarrhoea, abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting in this trial. Model predictions of itch responders and increased bother from diarrhoea normalized to placebo responses indicated that the 40 mg BID dose maximizes benefit while balancing tolerability.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a potent and selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT and interrupts the enterohepatic recirculation process and shunts unabsorbed bile acids for excretion in the faeces.

## Pharmacokinetics

Linerixibat has been specifically designed to have poor cellular permeability. Measurable plasma concentrations were sparse at all doses, even with the more sensitive bioanalytical method (LLQ of 10 pg/mL However, linerixibat plasma concentrations are neither required nor relevant to its efficacy.

## ADME

Metabolism is neglectable and no major human metabolites were detected in plasma or urine (study 205895). However, the metabolites in plasma were only investigated in samples taken 1, 2, 3 and 6 h post-dose which is too short to determine the metabolite profile (tmax is ~2.5 h and t½ is ~6.5 h). Furthermore, the metabolite profile was also investigated in studies 201351 and BAT117213, but the less sensitive analytical method was used and therefore no conclusions can be drawn from this study. In study 201351 (PK of linerixibat in Type 2 diabetes patients), no linerixibat and metabolites were detected in plasma except for 3 samples from 2 subjects. In urine, only linerixibat was observed and was &lt;0.02% of the dose. The analytical method used had a LLOQ of 1 ng/mL which was too high to observe linerixibat in plasma. In the mass balance study also no metabolites were detected, but sampling time was not correct to detect metabolites.

Based on the current data, it is unlikely that metabolites are present for a significant extent in human plasma due to the low absorption. However, it cannot be excluded that linerixibat is metabolised to a significant extent following absorption.

## Patient population

In the two clinical studies in patients (which used the most sensitive analytical method), sparse samples were taken. Most sampling times in study 212620 were between 1 and 3 hours post dose and samples showed a large variation in linerixibat plasma concentrations. Comparison of the concentrations observed 1-3 h post-dose in patients treated with 40 mg twice daily with the Cmax observed in healthy volunteers at steady state. However, the tmax in the healthy population after 2 weeks was 7.5 hours (range 0.5-12 hours) which is much later than the samples taken at 1-3 h in patients. This is a limitation. Furthermore, as the variability in the plasma concentrations of linerixibat was high in all studies performed, no clear assessment of the difference between exposure in patients and healthy subjects can be made. It is therefore unknown if the PK is similar in healthy volunteers and patients with primary biliary cholangitis.

## Special populations

Hepatic impairment The increase in exposure up to 13-fold in patients with hepatic impairment does not lead to additional safety issues in humans and was &gt;100-fold lower than the NOAEL in the non-clinical studies. Therefore, no adjustment is needed in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Renal impairment

Linerixibat has not been studied in patients with severe renal impairment or end stage renal disease (ESRD) including those on haemodialysis. Renal impairment is not expected to impact linerixibat exposure, because linerixibat is minimally absorbed and has negligible recovery in urine (&lt; 0.04 % of the oral dose).

## Drug-drug interactions

## Victim

Medicinal products that could increase the permeability of the intestine could lead to a higher exposure, including subjects with hepatic impairment. The increased fraction absorbed is most likely rapidly metabolised in the liver. Furthermore, the exposure is most likely still &gt;60-fold lower than the NOAEL observed in the non-clinical studies. It is therefore agreed that linerixibat can be given with medicinal products that increase the permeability of the intestine.

## Pharmacodynamics

## Mechanism of action

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a potent and selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT. Due to this inhibition the enterohepatic recirculation of bile acids is reduced. As a result the TSBA's are decreased.

With respect to cholestatic pruritus linerixibat decreases TSBA, lysophosphatidic acid and IL-31. the decrease in TSBAs, LPA (via measurement of autotaxin as a surrogate), and IL-31 is suggestive for an effect on pruritis. In principle, the mechanism of action can be seen as plausible and other products (e.g. maralixibat and odevixibat) are already approved for symptomatic treatment of hyperbilirubinaemic pruritus in other target populations (ALGS; PFIC).

As a result of the reduced absorption of bile acids entering ileal enterocytes, intestinal FXR is not activated resulting in a decrease in FGF-19. This reduction leads to an increase in cholesterol metabolism to bile acids as measured via an increase in serum C4. In addition, the increased hepatic

FXR also increases LDL receptor leading to a subsequent decrease in serum total cholesterol and LDL. Solubility and impact on local interactions

The data and discussions on interactions/factors that may influence the performance of the new product in the gastrointestinal tract are limited. As linerixibat is only minimally absorbed and locally effective in the gastrointestinal tract, its retention time in the lumen is comparatively longer, making it more sensitive to various factors. In addition, linerixibat has pH-dependent solubility (U-shaped pH solubility, low solubility (ICH M9) in pH range 3.5 - 4.0 for 40 mg dose). Factors/interactions which are likely to impact the performance of the product in the gastrointestinal tract are e.g. drug-drug interactions, products which change the performance/solubility of the product in the GI tract (e.g. Acid Reducing Agents).

From a biopharmaceutics perspective, the excipients enable rapid disintegration, thus making the drug available for solubilisation. The impact of excipients (including lubricant and film coat level) on disintegration time and dissolution was investigated during development and was shown not to adversely affect drug product performance. Considering that the dissolution plot of linerixibat tablets in aqueous media demonstrates that dissolution reaches 80% over 60 minutes even at pH 3.7 where linerixibat demonstrates the lowest solubility, it is agreed in principle that it is likely that there is sufficient time in a high solubility environment (either in the stomach at lower pH, or in the intestine at higher pH) for drug dissolution to occur during GI transit prior to reaching the target site in the ileum. Thus, drug concentrations at the target site may be relatively insensitive to minor changes in dissolution rate and minor changes in non-functional excipients. In conclusion, the CHMP agreed that the impact of the excipients appears minor in this case.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Primary pharmacology

linerixibat decreases pruritogens as serum bile acids, IL-31 and LPA. these observations are (qualitatively) in accordance with the findings observed for other IBAT inhibitors (e.g. maralixibat and odevixibat) currently approved for symptomatic treatment for itching in Alagille syndrome and PFIC.

A convincing dose-response relationship could however not be retrieved from the data.

## Secondary pharmacology

Linerixibat did not show significant activity against a panel of over 100 additional receptors, ion channels, transporters, and enzymes as assessed by a series of binding, functional and enzyme activity assays (for more details please refer to the non-clinical part of this AR.)

Safety pharmacology studies did not raise concerns regarding effects of linerixibat on CNS, respiratory and cardiovascular functions during clinical use in animals.

From a clinical perspective relevant, secondary pharmacology effects in animals were observed with respect to the cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, and CNS systems. But considering applied dose of 40 mg BID as reflected in the safety outcome of the main clinical trials (GLIMMER and GLISTEN) mainly the gastrointestinal effects may be of clinical relevance. However, it needs to be considered that the described effects and adverse events are not correlated with the blood PK but rather caused by local intestinal effects due to the mechanism of action.

In this context, it appears reassuring that the difference is extremely large between the applied 40 mg BID dose (total daily dose, 80 mg/day) in patients and the NOAEL dose of 100 mg/kg/day) evaluated in animals.

## Interactions (PK and PD):

The Applicant has conducted a battery of in-vitro investigations to elucidate the interaction potential of the compound, and to evaluate whether in-vivo studies exploring potential drug-drug interactions (DDIs) will be necessary. For the details of the results of these studies, it is referred to the preclinical part of this AR.

The results of these investigations showed that there is no relevant influence of linerixibat on the CYPs 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1, and 3A5. However, a time-dependent interaction potential was seen for CYP3A4. Similarly, there was no potential for interaction detected for PXR, as well as for the following transporters: P-gp, BCRP, OAT1 and OAT3, OCT2, and MATE1 and MATE2-K. However, Linerixibat inhibited the OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated transport of the probe substrates used.

Linerixibat was not detected to be a substrate of PgP, BCRP, and OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OATP2B1.

No in-vitro investigation was presented for the potential interaction with BSEP. At request, the Applicant has presented argumentation assuming that the observed effects (decrease of TSBAs, increase of C4, no changes on potential compensatory mechanisms) would not speak in favour of BSEP inhibition. These arguments are considered relevant. In conjunction with the toxicology findings, and the clinical safety data - not indicating biliary/cholestatic origin of liver-related findings/adverse events - the argumentation of the Applicant is finally accepted and the issue considered resolved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Applicant also explored the binding of linerixibat to bile acid binding resins (BARBs, such as cholestyramine, or colesevelam) and a high binding rate was detected. The Applicant took the consequence to recommend in the SmPC (and implement in clinical studies) a 4-hour gap between linerixibat and BARB intake.

In order to explore whether in-vivo studies would be necessary for the potential for DDIs based on CYP3A4, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, the guidance-based static R value extrapolation method was used. The calculations showed that Linerixibat did not meet the criteria for clinical investigations. This decision was also based on the fact that linerixibat is absorbed at a very low fraction, and itself undergoes negligible metabolism.

The Applicant further explored the potential to interact with common medication used in PBC which might potentially be influenced by the inhibition of intestinal bile acid transport. At the time of the conduct of the study programme, this was not only UDCA (a \"natural\" bile acid but usually only available with a very low fraction in human bile), but also OCA (being a synthetic bile acid).

Two interaction studies were conducted with OCA and showed a clear reduction of OCA most obvious for the conjugated forms of OCA and less so for the parent compound. The interaction could not be mitigated by imposing a 4 hr separation window between linerixibat and OCA intake. Since, however, the compound OCA has been taken from the market in 2024, the relevance of the studies is considered to be rather low. It can, however, be taken as an example of the fact that linerixibat is able to reduce the absorption of synthetic bile acids, mainly based on the conjugated forms. The Applicant has, at request, deleted the paragraph on OCA interaction which is acceptable considering the withdrawal of OCA from the European Market.

The potential to influence the PK of the standard medication for PBC, UDCA, was explored in the further clinical studies related to the phase 2a, 2b, and phase 3 of the development.

Study BAT117213, the phase 2a study was also designed as an interaction study with high standardisation of drug intake in an appropriate cross-over design.

The results of the study showed that UDCA plasma levels, quite similar to OCA, were relevantly reduced for the conjugates (mainly TUDCA and GUDCA).

The Applicant has also evaluated UDCA PK within study 201000 (GLIMMER) in a subgroup of 2x10 patients. There was no clear pattern of direction of change in concentration values at each sampling visit compared with Baseline for any treatment group. The PK concentrations by visit for GUDCA and TUDCA showed, however, reduced main values compared with baseline for most but not all timepoints, partly confirming the results of study BAT117213. However, the magnitude of the changes appears relevantly lower than in study BAT117213. The results would support the conclusion that no relevant DDI is present, but the results are regarded to be less reliable than those from study BAT117213, since the UDCA intake was not standardised.

The Applicant has also evaluated the interaction potential with UDCA based on the evaluation of the course of the biomarker ALP as a measure for disease activity.

The results from study BAT117213 do indeed not indicate any relevant systematic alteration of ALP, but rather reflect natural variability.

ALP was a marker for the potential interaction with UDCA was also used in the phase 2 and phase 3 studies (Study 201000, GLIMMER and 212620, GLISTEN).

In study 201000 the analysis of change from Baseline in serum ALP concentrations at Week 16 showed a numerical difference from placebo similar to or in favour of linerixibat for all active doses, and it was concluded that this supports the conclusion of lack of interaction with UDCA.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A similar evaluation was done in the phase 3 study 212620, but not with a focus on interaction, but rather referring to the evaluation of the safety of the compound, and the need to document that there is no negative influence on the overall disease course. The overall course of ALP does indeed not clearly indicate a negative influence of the active treatment, however, in the view of the fact that other biomarkers of disease activity (ALT, AST, GGT and TB) showed increases distinct from placebo it cannot be excluded that some alteration of the underlying disease process takes place. Monitoring of transaminase activities is recommended in the SmPC which is acceptable.

The interaction with fat soluble nutrients (especially fat soluble vitamins) is not investigated by the applicant. As for other IBAT inhibitors a decrease in bioavailability for the fat soluble nutrients is included in the SmPC, the applicant has provided additional analyses. Respective warning has been included in the SmPC.

In conclusion, there is currently no full confirmation of an exclusion of a relevant PD/PK interaction of linerixibat with UDCA based on its bile acid absorption inhibiting properties. A dedicated study on this interaction (based on UDCA PK) has shown a relevant reduction of the conjugated UDCA species. Overall, in the phase 2 studies, a relevant interaction based on the deterioration of markers of disease activity could not be detected and give some reassurance. Still, as some results of the phase 3 study point to a potential elevation of ALP and other related liver biomarkers appropriate warning statements on the monitoring of transaminase activity have been implemented in the SmPC.

The potential interaction with BARBs was tested also in the phase 3 study 212620 evaluating TSBA, autotaxin, IL-31, and other PD biomarkers (C4 and FGF-19) associated with the mechanism of action of linerixibat. There was overall no effect detected, and the differences between the treatment arms was, if any, to the advantage of the active treatment arm.

However, since only a small number of patients was using BARBs as concomitant administration, the results should be viewed with some caution.

## Relationship between plasma concentration and effect and safety

Typical exposure-response analyses were not possible for linerixibat due to the very low oral bioavailability (0.05%) and intermittently measurable, highly variable systemic concentrations (despite a very sensitive bioanalytical assay with an LLQ of 10 pg/mL). Therefore, a K-PD approach was implemented to characterize the dose-response relationship over time for TSBA concentrations and WINRS itch scores. This is agreed in principle, since no other alternative for this approach is feasible.

The applicant has only characterized the dose-response relationship over time regarding efficacy outcome, while impact on safety parameters in particular the impact on gastrointestinal safety was not addressed. This is considered a limitation but is acceptable given the very low bioavailability and limited size of the population.

## With respect to K-PD model for TSBA

The objective according to the applicant was to develop a K-PD model relating linerixibat dose with changes in TSBA concentrations following once and twice daily oral administration over time to:

o Quantify differences in TSBA change over time between treatment and placebo cohorts,

o Quantify distributions of random variability, and

o Identify covariates such as demographics, disease-related attributes, and

biomarkers that may explain variability in response.

Changes in TSBA concentration over time were described with a K-PD modelling approach using an indirect response model. A sine wave function on Kin to describe natural circadian fluctuations of bile acids within 24 hour periods. The effect of food consumption on gall bladder contraction resulting in enterohepatic recirculation of TSBA concentrations was modelled as a stimulatory effect on Kin. The system was modelled to be in a fasted state until a meal is consumed, then switched to a fed state upon meal consumption. The effect of linerixibat was modelled as an Emax model that inhibited Kin.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Baseline TSBA concentrations for healthy participants was predicted to increase with increasing baseline body weight. Baseline TSBA concentrations for PBC patients was predicted to decrease with decreasing ALP concentrations. Model diagnostics indicated reasonable precision in parameter estimates and overall agreement of model predictions with observed data.

While consistent with the known mechanisms driving the physiopathology and anticipated response of TSBA to the drug, the model itself was is mainly based on empirical approaches rather than mechanistic descriptions of gastric emptying, The imputation of drug effects to Kin rather than Kout is mostly based on fitting performances rather than on pathophysiology or pharmacology including enterohepatic recycling, bile secretion, and absorption from the intestinal mucosa. As a result, the model is not as versatile as a mechanistic model and, when applied to other studies, would need to be adapted to be adapted to the meal administration scheme of each study and potentially to other underlying diseases.

It is therefore not established that the proposed K-PD model is sensitive enough to detect clinically relevant covariate effects. The negative correlation between the tested covariates and (decrease) TSBA AUCs should be cautiously interpreted.

With respect to the K-PD model for Worst Itch Numerical Rating Scale Scores and the Dose-response model for Gastrointestinal Symptom Rating Scale Scores, several limitations were identified related to model parameter estimation, predictive performance and covariate implementation: they were acknowledged by the applicant and partly due to the available data that did not permit to allow higher quality models.

Therefore, the proposed models are considered at most supportive evidence.

## Dose justification

The proposed dose of linerixibat (40 mg BID) considered the balance between the benefits (impact on itch) and risks/tolerability across the Phase 1-3 studies as well as K-PD modelling and simulation. This is overall acceptable.

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

The PK of linerixibat was investigated in 2 clinical studies in healthy volunteers and 2 clinical studies in patients. Furthermore, 1 clinical study was conducted in subjects with hepatic impairment and 3 DDI studies. Four additional clinical studies were conducted investigating the PK of linerixibat, but a not sensitive enough analytical method was used and therefore no quantifiable plasma concentrations could be determined, although the substance might have been present.

The PK data in both healthy volunteers and patients is very limited. This is acceptable, because the pharmacological target of linerixibat is in the lumen of the small intestine.

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

The efficacy of linerixibat in cholestatic pruritus in PBC has been evaluated in 4 clinical studies. As the pivotal Phase 3 study, GLISTEN provides primary evidence for the efficacy of linerixibat 40 mg BID. Supportive evidence is derived from GLIMMER and LLSAT studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.1. Dose response - GLIMMER study

GLIMMER was a Phase 2b, 12-week double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group multi-centre dosefinding study to evaluate the efficacy, safety and tolerability of linerixibat (20 mg, 90 mg, 180 mg QD, and 90 mg BID) in participants with PBC and moderate to severe pruritus. The study had a prospectively defined adaptive design. Interim analysis led to addition of a new dose group (40 mg BID) and removal of further recruitment into an existing dose group (20 mg QD).

Adult participants with PBC (demonstrated by having at least 2 protocol-defined criteria relating to ALP levels, anti-mitochondrial titre [or PBC-specific antinuclear antibodies], and liver biopsy results), and at least moderate mean Worst Itch on a 0 to 10-point Numeric Rating Scale (WI-NRS ≥ 4 for at least half the days during the 8 weeks prior to the Screening Visit) entered a 4-week Initial Study Period (a single-blind placebo period). Participants with at least mild itch (WI-NRS ≥3 on at least 5 of 7 days) and acceptable level of compliance (10 from 14 entries in the eDiary) were randomized into the Main Study Period, a 12-week double-blind treatment period. Following completion of the Main Study Period, participants received single-blind placebo in the 4-week Final Study Period (see Figure 1). Patients stratified according to region, and participant's risk of PBC disease progression (based on serum ALP and total bilirubin concentrations at Day 1): ALP &lt;1.67 x ULN and total bilirubin ≤ULN (estimated 60% of participants), or ALP ≥1.67 x ULN and/or total bilirubin &gt;ULN (estimated 40% of participants).

Participants were not eligible for the study if their screening liver and kidney function tests met specified criteria (total bilirubin more than twice the ULN, ALT or AST more than 6 times the ULN, estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;45 mL/min/1.73 m2), or if they had a history of liver decompensation. Current diarrhoea, symptomatic cholelithiasis or inflammatory gall bladder disease, or a QT interval (corrected [QTc]) value &gt;480 msec were also exclusions.

Concomitant treatments for PBC, cholestatic pruritus in PBC, or any other medications that may have affected pruritus were standardized (kept stable) to minimize the risk of data confounding.

Figure 2: GLIMMER 201000 study design schema

<!-- image -->

The primary objective was to investigate the dose response of oral linerixibat on itch in patients with moderate to severe pruritus at Baseline by means of mean change from Baseline at Week 16 (following 12 weeks of randomised double-blind treatment) in the Mean Worst Daily Itch score.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Key secondary objectives were:

- To characterise the effects of linerixibat compared with placebo on impact of symptoms and quality of life in PBC patients with moderate to severe pruritus at baseline, evaluated by means of mean change from Baseline at Week 16 in PBC-40 Scale (all domains).
- To evaluate the effects of linerixibat compared with placebo on markers of disease among participants at high risk of PBC progression (i.e., those with ALP ≥1.67 x the upper limit of normal (ULN) and/or total bilirubin concentrations &gt;ULN at Day 1) by means of measurement of ALP, total bilirubin, liver function tests, etc.

Other secondary endpoints were used to evaluate further efficacy, safety, pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic (PD)/biomarker parameters.

Participants recorded symptoms of itching, as well as the impact on sleep and fatigue, twice daily (morning and evening), at approximately the same time as study drug administration. Itch severity was rated using a numerical rating scale (NRS) ranging from 0 (no itching) to 10 (worst imaginable itching). The morning NRS score reflected itch experienced during the previous night, and the evening score reflected daytime itch. The higher of the two daily scores was defined as the Worst Daily Itch score. The primary endpoint was the Mean Worst Daily Itch score at Week 16, calculated as the 7-day average of the Worst Daily Itch scores, i.e., the highest NRS value reported on each day.

In addition, participants assessed the interference of itch with sleep each morning using the NRS (0-10 scale), and rated fatigue each evening. Overall itch was also captured through a dedicated NRS item.

A sample size of approximately 100 eligible participants was anticipated to allow sufficient precision to provide a minimally detectable effect of at least 2 points on the Mean Worst Daily Itch score (assuming a between-participant standard deviation [SD] of 2 points) based on simulations on an Emax doseresponse model. Approximately 118 participants were planned to be randomised but the number was increased to approximately 140 following interim analysis.

A prospectively-defined interim analysis (IA) was conducted based on week 8 data when approx. 40 participants had reached Visit 5 (Week 12), representing 8 weeks of double-blind treatment in the Main Study Period. The IA was implemented using a firewalled analysis team and reviewed by a prespecified named group of personnel to minimise bias and to preserve blinding of the study team. The IA was conducted to determine whether the initial doses of linerixibat needed to be modified, to allow for discontinuation for futility, elimination of less informative doses, and/or addition of lower or higher doses to more fully describe the dose-response characteristics of linerixibat. The results of the IA could also lead to changes to the randomisation ratio and/or a change in the total number of participants to be randomised in the study. The decision framework followed a step-wise approach; final decisions were based on the totality of the efficacy, safety, and tolerability data:

The primary endpoint was analysed using an appropriate statistical model (ANCOVA) following a series of steps including:

- Preliminary checks using an analysis of covariance model to investigate if the 90 mg BID dose and 180 mg once daily dose could be determined as equivalent (defined as a 95% CI for the difference falling within (-1,1) and including 0).
- Checks of dose-response using a linear model.
- If total daily dose was determined to be statistically significant (at 10% level) in the linear dose-response analysis, then a non-linear dose-response was investigated by fitting a 4parameter Emax model.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The final linear or non-linear model was then selected and presented in terms of the LS Mean change from baseline for each treatment group/total daily dose in the study along with associated 95% CIs. The difference from placebo will also be presented with 95% CIs but as the study is exploratory, a pvalue will not be presented.

## Results

## Conduct

As claimed by the applicant, the study was conducted in accordance with ICH GCP from January 2017 to April 2020 and included 66 centres in 10 countries: 4 centres in Australia, 6 centres in Canada, 5 centres in France, 3 centres in Germany, 5 centres in Italy, 13 centres in Japan, 5 centres in Poland, 5 centres in Spain, 11 centres in the United Kingdom, and 9 centres in the United States.

## Protocol amendments

The protocol was finalised on 26 August 2016. There were 2 subsequent amendments made prior to the prospectively-defined interim analysis (IA) and breaking of the study blind, which mostly aimed improvement in patient recruitment. There were no known implications of the amendments on the objectives of the study or interpretation of the results.

## Interim analysis

The IA was conducted in June 2018 and comprised a total of 78 participants. None of the pre-specified decision criteria were met. The futility criteria were not met. The 180 mg once daily and 90 mg BID were the most promising doses. To better characterise the dose-response model for BID dosing and maximize information on most likely dose to be selected for Phase 3, the dose of 20 mg once daily was removed, 40 mg BID arm was added, and sample size was increased to approximately 140 randomised participants (n=20 for new 40 BID and additional patients in 180 mg QD and 90 mg BID arms).

## Final analysis

## Disposition of participants

There were 239 participants screened of which 194 (81%) entered the 4-week single-blind placebo Initial Study Period. Of these, 147 (76%) participants were randomised to the 12-week Main Study Period to receive double-blind treatment with either placebo or one of the doses of linerixibat (20 mg QD, 90 mg QD, 180 mg QD, 40 mg BID, and 90 mg BID). All randomised were included in the Safety and ITT Populations; 128 completed the Main Study period, 127 were entered into the Final Study Period and completed another 4 weeks of single-blind placebo. There were 138 participants who completed a final Follow-up telephone visit.

For the 47 participants who did not get randomised, the most frequently reported reason was failure to meet criteria for randomisation (43 [22%] participants). Of the 19 participants who did not complete the Main Study Period, the most frequent reason for withdrawal across all treatment groups was AEs (13 participants).

## Demographics and Baseline characteristics

The majority of participants in all treatment groups were female (94%), aged 28 to 64 (76%; median: 57; range 28-78 years) years, and of white/white Caucasian/European heritage (70-74%).

Approximately half of all participants were from Europe/Australia, with the remaining participants from Japan and North America in approximately equal proportions.

The Mean Worst Daily Itch score was lowest for the 20 mg once daily group (4.77), highest for the 90 mg once daily group (6.29), and well-balanced across all other linerixibat treatment groups; median values ranged from 4.36 (20 mg QD) to 6.57 (90 mg BID)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Mean overall itch showed values with lowest reported on 40 mg BID (3.96) and higher on 90 mg BID (4.88); median values ranged from 3.89 (placebo) to 5.36 (90 mg BID); Minimum values as low as 1.1 (40 mg BID) to 1.6 (90 mg BID) were reported.

On PGI-S (severity of itch over past 24 h) 15% (90 mg BID) to 38% (20 mg QD) of subjects at baseline had itch of only mild intensity; moderate to severe itch was reported by the majority of patients (35% in 90 mg QD to 63% in 180 mg QD); severe/very severe itch was reported in 13% (on 40 mg BID) to 30% (in placebo and 90 mg QD). In 40 mg BID arm majority of patients (52%) had moderate itch or mild itch (35%) at the baseline; Of note: this parameter was assessed twice prior to start of treatment in DB phase and intra-subject changes in the assessments were observed.

The mean ALP and total bilirubin values were similar across all treatment groups. There were 51 (35%) participants with high risk of disease progression (ALP); majority of patients did not have elevated total bilirubin

Baseline levels of serum bile acids were low and differed at Baseline across treatment groups (mean range: 5.709 to 22.820 µmol/L), and median Baseline values indicated at least half of all participants across all treatment groups had Baseline values &lt;5 µmol/L.

Baseline values of serum autotaxin showed high inter-subject variability; mean values for placebo, 20 mg QD, 90 mg QD and BID, and 180 QD arms were roughly in the same range (above 700 ug/L) and much higher than the baseline mean values for 40 mg BID treatment arm (below 500 ug/L);

The majority of all participants were taking concomitant UDCA (89% to 100%). Concomitant use of anti-itch therapy was well balanced across treatment groups; the most frequently reported was antihistamines (11% to 19%).

## Efficacy results

A statistically significant improvement from Baseline in PBC-40 Itch Domain was observed at Week 16 for all treatment groups (including placebo) with largest numerical mean difference from placebo of 1 point observed for the 40 mg BID group. Statistically significant improvements from Baseline were also observed for the 40 mg BID group in PBC-40 Social and Emotional Domains (numerically favourable differences from placebo of 2.4 and 0.8, respectively) and for the 20 mg

Mean scores decreased (itch improved) by the end of the Main Study Period (Week 16), for all treatment groups including placebo.

The primary objective of the study was not met, as determined by the protocol-defined primary endpoint analysis for the ITT Population, notably: There were no statistically significant differences between any linerixibat treatment group and placebo in the change from Baseline in Mean Worst Daily Itch score at Week 16 (as measured by a 0-10 point NRS). The greatest mean improvement from baseline in the Weekly Itch Score was observed in the linerixibat 40 mg BID treatment group (LS mean change, 2.86; 95% CI: 3.76, 1.95).

Figure 3: Line Plot of weekly Mean Treatment Group Change from Baseline in Mean Worst Daily Itch against Visit (ITT Population; post hoc analysis)

There was no evidence of a relationship between dose and change in Mean Worst Daily Itch score; total daily dose was not statistically significant (p=0.1106), and dose linearity could not be confirmed. In accordance with the RAP, no further dose-response modelling was performed for the ITT Population.

The primary analysis method selected (ANCOVA) indicated a clinically significant mean change from Baseline greater than 2 points for all linerixibat dose groups at Week 16, which was the pre-defined minimally effective change. However, a mean change from Baseline close to 2 points was also observed for participants in the placebo group. This resulted in a small treatment difference between all linerixibat treatment groups compared with placebo at Week 16, with the largest mean difference from placebo observed for the 40 mg BID treatment group. The probability of a treatment difference ≥2 was low (&lt;10%) for all treatment groups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Investigation of covariates showed no statistical evidence of an effect of Baseline risk group or ALP value at Day 1 on change from Baseline in Mean Worst Daily Itch Score at Week 16 (p=0.7740 and p=0.9160, respectively).

Baseline severity of pruritus was significantly associated with change from Baseline in Mean Worst Daily Itch at Week 16 (p=0.0001), with more severe participants having larger changes.

At least 3 point and at least 4 point reductions in monthly average of worst itch scores on 40 mg BID and placebo showed response rates of 52% vs 31% and 35% vs 19%, respectively in a cumulative post hoc analysis.

Overall itch severity (eDiary entry), PBC-40, PGI-S and PGI-C, sleep interference showed similar trends and changes as primary endpoint.

Mean total serum bile acid values were unexpectedly low and differed at Baseline across treatment groups (mean range: 5.709 to 22.820 µmol/L), and median Baseline values indicated at least half of all participants across all treatment groups had Baseline values &lt;5 µmol/L. Some numerical mean reductions were observed at Week 16 in TSBA and a small mean increase was observed for all treatment groups other than the 90 mg BID group, indicating a return towards Baseline levels following switch to placebo in the patients on active treatment. Variability was high and overall levels low.

New bile acid synthesis was indicated via an increase in mean concentrations of C4. The ANCOVA of change from Baseline in C4 indicated an increase in LS mean C4 values at Week 16 for all treatment groups with the largest increases observed for the 180 mg once daily, and both BID treatment groups. The LS mean and associated 95% CI indicated a statistically significant difference from placebo in favour of linerixibat for all 3 of these treatment groups (LS mean difference: 24.6, 49.4, and 34.4, respectively).

The ANCOVA of mean change from Baseline in FGF-19 and serum autotaxin at Week 16 indicated statistically significant differences in favour of 40 mg BID when compared with placebo, as demonstrated by the LS means and associated 95% CIs. A numerical difference in favour of 40 mg BID was also observed when compared with 90 mg BID.

## 5.3.2. Main instruments for assessment of efficacy and PRO validation

## Worst Itch Numeric Rating Scale (WI-NRS)

Worst Itch NRS was used as a primary efficacy parameter in the Phase II and III clinical studies. Monthly Itch Score was the primary efficacy endpoint for the Phase 3 study GLISTEN following discussion with the FDA and post-hoc examination of the data from GLIMMER. GLIMMER (and LLSAT) used the Mean Worst Daily Itch Score (now referred to as the Weekly Itch Score).

Participants in GLIMMER, GLISTEN, and LLSAT (only Group 2 [participants from GLISTEN]) recorded their worst itch severity twice daily using an eDiary using the WI-NRS, a single patient-reported item with a response scale ranging from 0 ('no itching') to 10 ('worst imaginable itching'). In the morning, participants rated their worst itch experience considering 'between bedtime last night and now.' In the evening, participants rated their itch severity while considering 'between waking this morning and now.'

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

These WI-NRS responses were used to calculate the following scores:

- Worst Daily Itch Score: the worst of the 2 scores recorded that day.
- Weekly Itch Score: the average of the Worst Daily Itch scores in 1 week.
- Monthly Itch Score: the worst Weekly Itch Score for that month.

At least 1 entry on a day was required for the calculation of Worst Daily Itch Score. If there was no entry for a day, then the Worst Daily Itch Score was set to 'missing'.

At least 4 Worst Daily Itch scores were required in a given week to calculate the Weekly Itch Score for that week. If more than 3 Worst Daily Itch Scores were missing in any given week, then the Weekly Itch Score was set to 'missing' for that week.

If 1 or more Weekly Itch Scores was missing, then the worst (highest) of the non-missing Weekly Itch Scores was selected as the Monthly Itch Score. If no Weekly Itch Scores were available for a designated 4-week period, the Monthly Itch Score at that Visit was set to 'missing'.

## WI-NRS: Responder Definitions

Anchor-based analyses, consistent with FDA guidance (2023), were used to define clinically meaningful within-patient change in WI-NRS. The Patient Global Impression of Severity (PGI-S, primary anchor) and Patient Global Impression of Change (PGI-C, supportive anchor with a \"meaningful change\" followup) were employed.

Analyses were conducted utilizing blinded data on the first approximately 100 participants randomized (initial interim sample) and were repeated on the second set of approximately 100 participants (confirmatory interim sample). To enhance robustness of these results and at the request of FDA, the anchor-based analyses were repeated (post hoc) on a pooled sample combining the initial and confirmatory samples. A 1-point improvement on the PGI-S and the 'moderately improved' response category of the PGI-C were considered as the key reference groups for what constitutes meaningful within-patient change on the WI-NRS at Week 24. All 3 waves of analyses established that a 3-point reduction in itch represented a meaningful change for the majority of GLISTEN participants at Week 24. Specifically, a 3.1-point improvement on the WI-NRS corresponded to a 1-point improvement on the PGI-S (primary anchor measure of focus) and a 3.2-point improvement corresponded to 'moderately improved' itch on the PGI-C.

With the primary endpoint in GLISTEN being a MMRM analysis of change in itch over 24 weeks (rather than at Week 24), a mean reduction of 2.5-points on the WI-NRS corresponded to a 1-point improvement on the PGI-S (vs. 3.1-points when examining change at Week 24).

These findings are supported by qualitative interviews with PBC patients with cholestatic itch (n=30), where a 2.7-point WI-NRS reduction was considered meaningful.

## Sleep Interference NRS (eDiary)

Participants in GLIMMER, GLISTEN, and LLSAT (only Group 2 [participants from GLISTEN]) rated their sleep interference due to pruritus each morning in the eDiary using a response scale ranging from 0 ('did not interfere') to 10 ('completely interfered'). The average of the Daily Sleep Scores over a 7-day period was the Weekly Sleep Score (formerly referred to as 'Mean Daily Sleep Score' in the GLIMMER CSR), while the Monthly Sleep Score was the worst Weekly Sleep Score for that month (i.e., worst week score of the 4 weeks).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Post-hoc anchor-based analyses were conducted to explore within-patient change estimates for the Sleep Interference NRS item which assessed pruritus-related sleep interference using a 0-10 scale (0 representing 'did not interfere' and 10 representing 'completely interfered') (m5.3.5.4, NRS PRO Dossier, Section 7).

Anchor-based analyses were conducted utilizing the same pooled sample described above (combining the initial and confirmatory interim samples) and anchors to establish a meaningful within-patient change estimate for the Sleep Interference NRS score at Week 24. Results determined that a 2.8-point reduction in the Sleep Interference NRS score was a meaningful amount of change at Week 24 as this was the amount of change which corresponded to a 1-point improvement on the PGI-S. For average change in sleep interference scores over 24 weeks (rather than at Week 24) 2.3-points was defined as appropriate threshold in the context of the key secondary endpoint of mean change in Monthly Sleep Scores over 24 weeks.

## Patient's Global Impression of Severity (PGI-S) and Patient's Global Impression of Change (PGI-C)

The PGI-S is a single patient-reported item which assesses itch severity using a 5-level response scale (1-absent, 2-mild, 3-moderate, 4-severe, and 5-very severe). In GLIMMER, the PGI-S utilized a 24hour recall period, the version in GLISTEN used a 7-day recall period.

The PGI-C is patient-reported item which assesses participants' views on their change in itch severity since study start. The PGI-C has a 7-level response scale to rate the amount of change perceived by the participant (1-very much improved, 2-moderately improved, 3-minimally improved, 4-no change, 5-minimally worse, 6-moderately worse, and 7-very much worse).

Both scales were administered at site visits.

## Post hoc analyses:

On request of the CHMP, thresholds for meaningful within-patient change for the PGI-S and PGI-C were estimated using PTEA as an anchor for assessment of treatment satisfaction at Week 16 (Week 12 of treatment) in GLIMMER study.

97% of participants in the \"satisfied\" and \"very satisfied\" PTEA groups (n=30/31 and n=34/35, respectively) responded \"yes\" to PGI-C item 2, which directly asked whether participants felt they had experienced a meaningful change in their itch since starting the study.

Based on mean changes in the derived PGI-S score for each of the 2 PTEA reference groups (-0.7 for \"satisfied\" and -1.2 for \"very satisfied\") (Table 10), a meaningful within-patient improvement threshold of ≥1.0-point was proposed for the PGI-S, as a 1-point reduction (improvement) in PGI-S ranged from \"satisfied\" to \"very satisfied\" on the PTEA.

For the PGI-C, participants in the \"satisfied\" group had a mean PGI-C derived score of 1.6 while participants in the \"very satisfied\" group had a mean score of 1.1.

## PBC-40

The PBC-40 is a disease-specific, HRQoL instrument consisting of 6 domains (social, emotional, fatigue, itch, cognitive, and other symptoms) to assess disease impact on patients. Scores for individual PBC40 items range from 1 (or 0 for some items related to itch, social function, and symptoms) to 5, with higher scores indicating worse HRQoL. The cut-off for detecting clinically significant itch has been reported to be a score of ≥7 on the PBC-40 Itch domain [Mells, 2013] which has been shown to correspond to the cut-off used to define the moderate-severe itch population using the WI-NRS (≥4) [Halliday, 2024]. The version of the PBC-40 administered in the linerixibat program utilized a 7-day recall period and had been adapted from the original PBC-40 (4-week recall). The content validation and psychometric evidence for the 7-day PBC-40 is detailed in the m5.3.5.4, PBC-40 PRO Dossier. The PBC-40 was administered at baseline and every 4 weeks in GLIMMER and GLISTEN, and every 3 months to 6 months and at 12 months in LLSAT.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.3. Main study

## 5.3.3.1. GLISTEN

## 5.3.3.1.1. Study title

GLISTEN (Global Linerixibat Itch STudy of Efficacy and Safety iN PBC): A two-part, randomized, placebo controlled, double blind, multicenter, Phase 3 study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of linerixibat for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in participants with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC)

Study code: 212620; EU CT number: 2021-000007-21; NCT number: NCT04950127.

## 5.3.3.1.2. Study design

GLISTEN was a pivotal Phase 3, global, multi-centre, 2-part (Part A and Part B), randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled study in PBC participants with moderate to severe pruritus (WI-NRS ≥ 4) with linerixibat 40 mg BID.

The trial consisted of 3 periods: screening (1 month), intervention (32 weeks) and follow-up (2 weeks).

The Intervention period consisted of two parts: Part A, that tested efficacy and safety of linerixibat 40 mg BID vs. placebo in a 24-week parallel group setting in patients on stable concomitant PBC treatment, and Part B, that assessed the return of itch, improvement in itch, and maintenance of improved itch over 8 weeks on changed (linerixibat-placebo, or placebo-linerixibat), or continued (linerixibat-linerixibat; placebo-placebo) treatment.

Participants were asked to record itch twice daily for 1 month during Screening to obtain a Monthly Itch Score prior to randomization. The study design schema is shown in Figure below.

Participants who completed treatment in Part A and Part B were offered the opportunity to take part in the follow-on LLSAT study. Participants who did not enter the follow-on study or were discontinued from study treatment but remained in GLISTEN completed a follow-up telephone call approximately 7 to 14 days after the last dose of linerixibat.

<!-- image -->

*The follow-up period will only apply to participants who do not enrol into a follow-on study Two types of sites were involved: Metasites (with fully remote data collection due to COVID-19 pandemic) and Brick and mortar sites (allowance to collect some data remotely).

Figure 5: GLISTEN 212620 study design schema

<!-- image -->

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Treatment

Linerixibat tablets 40 mg or matching placebo was administered 30 min prior to food intake twice daily and Fseparated by a 4 h break from intake of bile acid binding resin such as cholestyramine.

## Randomisation

All eligible participants were centrally assigned to randomized study intervention using an IVRS/IWRS. They were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio to receive one of the following treatments:

- linerixibat 40 mg BID in Part A and Part B
- linerixibat 40 mg BID in Part A and placebo in Part B
- placebo in Part A and Part B
- placebo in Part A and linerixibat 40 mg BID in Part B.

Randomization was stratified by the severity of pruritus (moderate: ≥4 to &lt;7; \"severe\": ≥7) and concomitant cholestatic pruritus treatment (bile acid binding resin (BABR)-containing regimen; regimen that does not contain BABR; no cholestatic pruritus treatment). Region/type of site (United States [US] Brick and Mortar, fully decentralized US site [\"Metasite\"], and Rest of world Brick and Mortar) was an administrative stratification factor and was therefore not used in the analysis.

## Blinding

This was a double-blinded study. After the primary analysis when all randomized participants completed Part A intervention period (up to Week 24/Visit 9) on 28 October 2024, the study was partially unblinded (core sponsor team unblinded, site remained blinded) on 14 November 2024 (database lock). GSK personnel involved in direct communication with the study sites/investigators continuing on Part B of GLISTEN remained blinded to the randomized treatment assignment of individual participants in the study until the database lock date of 31 January 2025.

## Patient population

Key inclusion criteria:

Participants aged ≥18 years with PBC defined as those with at least 2 out of 3 of the following criteria:

- Documented history of sustained increased ALP levels &gt;ULN first recognized at least 6 months prior to Screening
- Documented positive AMA titer (&gt;1:40 titer on immunofluorescence or M2 positive by ELISA or by an alternative validated assay) or PBC-specific antinuclear antibodies (antinuclear dot and/or nuclear rim positive)
- Liver biopsy (documented at any time in the past) consistent with PBC

PBC-related pruritus of ≥4 points on WI-NRS with 40/56 entries available in the Screening period and with at least 4 days daily itch scores each week, during the 4-week period immediately preceding Randomization at Day 1. Additionally, participants who had a Monthly Itch Score of ≥4 (i.e., at least 1 of the 4 Weekly Itch Scores was ≥4) and had no Weekly Itch Score that was &lt;3 for any other week.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Key exclusion criteria:

Liver-related conditions: Relevantly elevated TB (TB &gt;2 x ULN using the average of 2 baseline measures), ALT &gt;6 x ULN, ALT &gt;5 x ULN using the average of two baseline measures, Abnormal liver biochemistry (ALT, AST and ALP, or TB) during the Screening period (at Visit 1 or Visit 2) and the variance between these two samples for the abnormal parameter was &gt;40%, History or presence of hepatic decompensation, or presence of Hepatitis B, or C, primary sclerosing cholangitis, alcoholic liver disease and/or confirmed hepatocellular carcinoma or biliary cancer, symptomatic cholelithiasis or cholecystitis or current malignancies or moderate (or greater) alcohol consumption.

Kidney function: eGFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73m² at Screening (based on Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology Collaboration [CKD-EPI] equation).

Potential confounders: conditions associated with itch (e.g., contact dermatitis), patients with changes in prior/concomitant treatment of the disease or itch.

Other relevant conditions: e.g., HIV infection, any current uncontrolled psychiatric condition, QTc &gt;480 msec.

## 5.3.3.1.3. Objectives and estimands

## Primary objective

The primary objective was to investigate the superiority of 24 weeks of treatment with oral linerixibat compared with placebo on itch in PBC patients with cholestatic pruritus. The primary endpoint was Change from Baseline in MIS over 24 weeks (Weeks 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, and 24) using a 0-10 NRS. The primary hypothesis was that linerixibat reduces itch score over 24 weeks compared to placebo. A negative change from baseline (or reduction) represents an improvement in itch.

The null hypothesis (H0) assumes that there is no difference in the reduction of itch score over 24 weeks between linerixibat and placebo groups. The alternate hypothesis (H1) assumes that there is a difference in the reduction of itch score over 24 weeks between linerixibat and placebo groups.

## Estimands for the primary objective

| disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic                                                                                                                 |                  |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------|
| ICE 2: Permanent treatment discontinuation, disruptions in treatment, or treatment delays related to the Coronavirus disease (COVID-19) pandemic | Hypothetical     |
