The primary estimand targets the difference in mean Change from Baseline in Monthly Itch Scores averaged over 24 weeks between Linerixibat + background itch therapy versus Placebo + background itch therapy, regardless of treatment discontinuations/interruptions/delay unrelated to COVID-19 pandemic, regardless of changes in background itch therapy/rescue medication usage and had treatment discontinuations/interruptions/delay due to COVID-19 pandemic did not happen.

A supplementary estimand for the primary objective was defined to assess the hypothetical treatment effect of linerixibat compared to placebo in the absence of intercurrent events including treatment discontinuation, disruptions in treatment or treatment delays, changes in itch therapy or use of rescue. In this case, all Daily Itch Score data impacted by the intercurrent events were discarded.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on primary estimands

All efficacy analyses were performed using the intent to treat (ITT) population (defined as all randomized participants who were classified according to the treatment randomized), or subsets of the ITT population where specified. Part B analysis was restricted to all randomized patients not withdrawing during part A.

## Primary Endpoint Analyses

The primary endpoint was change from baseline in Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks. The Monthly Itch Score was determined using the Worst Itch Numerical Rating Scale (WI-NRS). The Monthly Itch Score, will be determined from the worst weekly average available for the month.

The primary analysis was a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) of change from baseline in Monthly Itch Score over the 24-week treatment period. The model included main effects for treatment, visit (Weeks 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, and 24), and concomitant use of itch medications at baseline as categorical variables, baseline Monthly Itch Score as a continuous variable, as well as treatment by visit interaction and baseline Monthly Itch Score by visit interaction. An unstructured covariance structure will be assumed; if this does not converge then compound symmetry (CS) will be used, followed by an autoregressive [AR(1)] covariance structure if CS does not converge. Use of an unstructured covariance structure did not lead to convergence so, per the above, a compound symmetry (CS) covariance structure was used. LS means over the 24 weeks (Visit 4/Week 4 to Visit 9/Week 24) were calculated using equal weighting for each timepoint. The overall significance level for the primary endpoint analysis was set at the two-sided 0.05 level.

In line with the targeted estimand, data following intercurrent events unrelated to COVID-19 pandemic (treatment policy strategy) were included as available. For intercurrent events related to the COVID-19 pandemic (hypothetical strategy), outcomes impacted by COVID-19 were discarded. This assumed that unobserved outcomes of participants who withdrew from the study or who experienced a COVID-19 related intercurrent event were MAR.

A supplementary estimand for the primary endpoint was also defined, handing all intercurrent events with a hypothetical strategy. For this data collected post ICE were discarded.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The main analytical approach was repeated using the non-BABR ITT population, which excluded all participants taking BABR as the background itch therapy at baseline and using the non-Metasite ITT population which excluded participants enrolled at the Metasite (i.e., the fully decentralized study site where all study visits occurred remotely).

A sensitivity analysis using a tipping point approach was conducted to evaluate the impact of the MAR assumption on the conclusions of the primary analysis. For the tipping point analysis in a first step missing MIS for Week 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, and 24, including data discarded due to COVID 19 related intercurrent events, will be multiply imputed under the MAR assumption. A shift parameter (ranging from -3 to 3 by 0.5 increments) will then be added to the imputed data. Since the MIS NRS has a 0-10 range, any values that are less than 0 will be set to 0, and any values greater than 10 will be set to 10. Then multiply imputed data sets will be analysed with the primary MMRM analysis and results for the overall treatment effect will be combined with Rubin's rules. The shift parameters will vary independently for each arm, will only be applied to the imputed monotone missing values, and a positive/negative shift parameter corresponds to a worsening/improvement of the itch.

The use of eDiary data collection for many of the study endpoints permitted the use of different approaches to study visits (operating models): via traditional in-person clinics ('Brick and Mortar'), fully decentralized ('Metasite') or a combination ('Flexible'). Supplementary analyses were conducted to evaluate any impact for each treatment group and between treatment groups for the Metasite vs. Flexible vs. Brick and Mortar operating models.

## Secondary objectives

Key secondary endpoints of change from baseline in WIS at Week 2, change from baseline in MSS over 24 weeks, as well as responder endpoints defined as achieving ≥2, ≥3, and ≥4-point reduction from Baseline in the MIS at Week 24 were controlled for multiplicity (see 3.3.1.3.4.). Additional supportive secondary endpoints include change from baseline in PBC-40 domain scores at Week 24, change from baseline in PGI-S over 24 weeks, PGI-C over 24 weeks, change from baseline in ALP at Week 24, and change from baseline in bilirubin at Week 24.

## Estimands for the secondary objectives

For key secondary objectives the targeted estimand applies the same intercurrent event handling as for the primary one.

For secondary objectives the targeted estimand applies a treatment policy strategy for all three listed intercurrent events.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on the secondary estimands

All key secondary endpoints and supportive secondary endpoints were analyzed using a MMRM model similar to the primary endpoint except for the responder endpoints. The summary measure for the responder endpoints was the difference in responder rates (proportion of responders) between linerixibat and placebo groups, analyzed using a stratified analysis with Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) weights, adjusted for baseline itch severity (\"moderate\": ≥4 to &lt;7; \"severe\": ≥7) and concomitant use of itch medications. Multiple imputation based on the MAR assumption was performed to impute missing Monthly Itch Scores prior to deriving the responder definitions. The imputed data sets were then analyzed using CMH method and subsequently combined.

A step-down/hierarchical approach was prospectively defined to test the following key secondary endpoints if statistical significance was achieved for the primary endpoint:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Change from baseline in Weekly Itch Score at Week 2
- Change from baseline in Monthly Sleep Score over 24 weeks
- Responders defined as the following reduction from baseline in Monthly Itch Score at Week 24
- o ≥2-point reduction;
- o ≥3-point reduction;
- o ≥4-point reduction.

These tests were conducted sequentially at a 5% two-sided significance level.

Additional supportive secondary and exploratory endpoints were not adjusted for multiplicity and twosided p-values &lt;0.05 were considered nominally significant.

## 5.3.3.1.4. Results

## Participating sites and countries:

This study was conducted at 115 centres (centres with at least 1 participant screened) with participants in 19 countries (Argentina, Belgium, Brazil, Bulgaria, Canada, China, Czech republic, France, Germany, Greece, Israel, Italy, Japan, Mexico, Poland, Russian Federation, Spain, United Kingdom, and United States.

Study initiation date (first participant first visit): 27 August 2021

Completion date for Part A (last participant Week 24/Visit 9): 28 October 2024

Study completion date (last participant last visit): 20 December 2024

Database lock date Part A: 14 November 2024; Database lock date (Final): 31 January 2025

## Conduct

After the primary analysis when all randomized participants completed Part A intervention period (up to Week 24/Visit 9) on 28 October 2024, the study was partially unblinded (core sponsor team unblinded, site remained blinded) on 14 November 2024 (database lock). GSK personnel involved in direct communication with the study sites/investigators continuing on Part B of GLISTEN remained blinded to the randomized treatment assignment of individual participants in the study until the database lock date of 31 January 2025.

For population K-PD model development, individual participant-level, de-identified (not associated with participant number), and unblinded exploratory biomarker (TSBA) and tolerability data (GSRS scores) were analysed prior to unblinding the study. Independent sponsor programmers and clinical pharmacology modelling and simulation analysts had access to actual randomization and dosing information.

## Participant flow and numbers analysed

A total of 376 unique participants were screened, of which 238 (63%) participants met the eligibility criteria and were enrolled in the study. Screen failure was observed in 138 (37%) participants. The most frequently reported reason for screen failure was inclusion/exclusion criteria not met (117 [31%] participants) followed by withdrawal by subject (19 [5%] participants). A total of 238 participants were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio.

## Part B

Overall, 211 (88.7% of the randomised population) participants entered Part B of the study. However, 5 subjects (2%) in the lin-lin treatment group (4 based on participant's decision and one based on an investigator's decision) withdrew the study prior to start of treatment in Part B.

## Deviations from study plan

In total 6 versions of the study protocol were created, including one specific for Israel. All amendments apart from the Amendment 02 were considered substantial based on the criteria set forth in Article 10(a) of Directive 2001/20/EC of the European Parliament and the Council of the European Union. Multiple modifications concerning inclusion/exclusion criteria, wording of PROs (e.g., PBC-40), withdrawal criteria, etc., were introduced with the amendments 04, 03, and 01.

Compliance to study procedures ranged from 82% to 92% in both treatment arms.

## Baseline data

The demographic characteristics were balanced between the linerixibat and placebo groups. Participants were predominantly female (95%), white (62%), and Not Hispanic or Latino (76%).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Majority of the participants were White/White Caucasian/European heritage (58%), followed by AsianEast Asian heritage (16%) and Asian-Japanese heritage (14%). Overall, median age of the participants was 55.0 years (range: 30 years to 80 years; 25% of the patients was 65 years or older and 4% - 75 years and older). Height and weight were well balanced across treatment arms.

In the Part B Lin-Pla group had a higher proportion of participants who were 'Hispanic or Latino' (43%) compared with the other groups (16% to 21%).

## Baseline disease characteristics

Important baseline disease characteristics were generally balanced between the linerixibat and placebo groups: The mean (SD) Monthly Itch Score was 7.34 (1.541); All participants had moderate to severe itch on WI-NRS. More than half (58%) of the participants had severe baseline itch defined as severe itch (≥7) on the WI-NRS; The majority of participants had controlled PBC as assessed using the ALP biomarker: 24% had ALP ≤1 x ULN and 76% had ALP increased; 29% had ALP &gt;1 to &lt;1.67 x ULN. Half of the participants (48%) had PBC that was not adequately controlled (ALP ≥1.67 x ULN); The majority of the participants had TB ≤1 x ULN (87%).

The mean (SD) duration of PBC since diagnosis was 75.5 (68.13) months while mean (SD) duration of pruritus since diagnosis was 92.8 (87.93) months. The majority of the participants (88%) did not have cirrhosis.

Approximately half of the participants (50% in the placebo group and 56% in the linerixibat group) were not receiving cholestatic pruritus therapy. The use of concomitant cholestatic pruritus therapy regimen that did not contain BABR was higher in the placebo group compared with linerixibat group (42% vs. 35%). The use of cholestyramine was similar between the linerixibat and placebo groups (6% and 8%, respectively). The use of concomitant fibrates was higher in placebo group when compared with linerixibat group (25% vs. 19%).

The mean TB concentration was lower in the linerixibat group when compared with the placebo group.

The study was not stratified by baseline biomarker concentrations nor are any of the biomarkers utilized for diagnosis of cholestatic pruritus or PBC. However, mean baseline concentrations of the biomarkers related to pruritus pathogenesis (i.e. TSBA, autotaxin, and IL-31) and pharmacodynamics of linerixibat (i.e. C4 and FGF-19) were largely similar between treatment groups (Table 19).

The most frequently reported current medical history findings other than PBC were hyperlipidemia (103 [43%] participants), hypertension (76 [32%]), hypothyroidism and hyperlipidaemia (35 [15%], each), and gastrooesophageal reflux disease (33 [14%]).

Most participants (up to 97%) were receiving UDCA during Part A and Part B. The proportion of participants receiving UDCA was similar between linerixibat group (96%) and placebo group (97%) in Part A and across treatment groups in Part B (range: 80% to 96%).

In Part A, the use of concomitant itch medication was slightly higher in placebo group when compared with linerixibat group (61 [51%] participants in the placebo group and 53 [45%] in the linerixibat group).

The most frequently reported concomitant itch medications (≥5% in either treatment group) were bezafibrate, cholestyramine, hydroxyzine, gabapentin, sertraline and fenofibrate

## Outcomes and estimation

## Primary endpoint

The primary objective of the study was met. Statistically significant improvement in the Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks was observed in the linerixibat group when compared with the placebo group (least-squares [LS] mean change from baseline, -2.86 vs. -2.15; LS mean difference vs. placebo, - 0.72; 95% confidence interval [CI]: -1.15 to -0.28; p=0.001).

In the population that excluded participants enrolled at Metasite, there was a statistically significant difference between linerixibat and placebo groups in Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks (LS mean difference vs. placebo in change from baseline: -0.68 [95% CI: -1.14, -0.23; nominal p=0.003])

Table 18: Analysis of Change from Baseline in Monthly Itch Score Over 24 Weeks - Part A (Study 212620 GLISTEN Primary Endpoint; non-Metasite ITT Population)

SD = standard deviation; LS = least squares; CI = confidence interval

a Baseline determined from all placebo patients, n=110

b Based on least squares mean from a mixed-effect model for repeated measures (MMRM) for change from baseline in Monthly Itch Score over the 24-week period with terms for treatment, visit, treatment by visit interaction, baseline itch, baseline itch by visit interaction, and use of baseline concomitant itch medication.

- Supplementary estimand for the primary objective

In the hypothetical situation with no intercurrent events, there was a statistically significant difference between linerixibat and placebo groups in Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks: (LS mean difference vs. placebo in change from baseline: -0.86 [95% CI: -1.29, -0.42; nominal p&lt;0.001]).

The number of participants experiencing intercurrent events was similar between the linerixibat and placebo groups, except for the intercurrent event of permanent treatment discontinuation unrelated to the COVID pandemic, which was higher in linerixibat group (23%) compared with placebo group (11%).

- Supplementary, sensitivity analyses, and subgroup analyses

There was no significant interaction between operating model (Brick and Mortar vs. Flexible vs. Metasite) and treatment group (p=0.304). Missing data analysis (tipping point) suggests that it would unlikely impact the primary efficacy results.

Subgroup analyses (age, sex, ethnicity, baseline values, concomitant medications, etc.) were generally consistent with the primary results. No statistically significant treatment-by-subgroup interactions were found, although some numerical differences were observed. However, some subgroups were very small (e.g., baseline concomitant BABR).

Subgroup analysis evaluating the impact of region on change from baseline in Monthly Itch Score showed no difference between linerixibat and placebo in European sites (LS mean difference vs. placebo: 0.01 [95% CI: -0.76, 0.79]).

## Key secondary endpoints and responder analyses

The first 2 key secondary objectives of the 5 key secondary objectives tested in the stepdown/hierarchical approach were met.

- Weekly Itch Score: Statistically significant improvement in the Weekly Itch Score from baseline was observed in the linerixibat group when compared with the placebo group as early as Week 2 (LS mean difference vs. placebo, -0.71; 95% CI: -1.07, -0.34; p&lt;0.001).

- Monthly Sleep Score: Linerixibat group showed a significant improvement in pruritus-related sleep interference compared with the placebo group over 24 weeks (LS mean change from baseline, -2.77 vs. -2.24; LS mean difference vs. placebo, -0.53; 95% CI: -0.98, -0.07; p=0.024).
- Supplementary analysis evaluating the non-BABR ITT population was consistent with the ITT population. There was a statistically significant difference in Monthly Sleep Score between groups over 24 weeks: (LS mean difference vs. placebo in change from baseline: -0.60 [95% CI: -1.08, -0.12; nominal p=0.014]).
- Responder Analysis (Monthly Itch Score): Linerixibat showed a higher proportion of participants achieving itch improvement at Week 24 across responder definitions: ≥2-point (68% vs 64%), ≥3-point (56% vs 43%), and ≥4-point (41% vs 29%). Statistical significance wasn't met for most definitions. Nominal significance was observed for ≥3-point (ITT) and ≥4point (non-Metasite) improvements (p=0.043).
- The results of the supplementary analysis evaluating the proportion of responders in the nonBABR ITT population were consistent with the analysis of the ITT population for all prespecified responder definitions (≥2-points [nominal p=0.359], ≥3-points [nominal p=0.020], and ≥4-points [nominal p=0.027]) at Week 24.
- Supplementary analysis of empirical cumulative distribution function showed small separation between the curves representing various responder definition for itch (see the figure below).

## Supportive Secondary Endpoints (PGI-S, PGI-C, PBC 40)

## PGI-S and PGI-C

Part A

Linerixibat demonstrated greater efficacy than placebo in improving Patient Global Impression of Itch - Severity (PGI-S) and PGI-C scores over 24 weeks (p&lt;0.001 for both).

Participants in the linerixibat group reported numerically better improvement in the derived PGI-S scores from baseline compared to the placebo group over 24 weeks (LS mean difference of -0.37 [95% CI: -0.55, -0.20). PGI-C scores lower scores (greater itch improvement) on linerixibat over 24 weeks (LS mean difference of -0.49 [95% CI: -0.76, -0.21).

Categorical analysis of PGI-S showed that the rates of participants with \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch decreased and remained consistently lower on linerixibat compared to placebo (range: 7% to 21% on linerixibat compared to 48% at the baseline; Range: 13% to 24% on placebo from the baseline value of 55%). The rates of participants with \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch increased and remained higher on linerixibat compared to placebo over the whole 24-weeks treatment period (range: 54% to 68% on linerixibat from the baseline value of 10%; range: 28% to 51% from the baseline value of 9%).

At Week 24, linerixibat participants reported lower rates of 'moderate' (23%), 'severe' (6%), or 'very severe' (3%) itch compared to placebo (31%, 14%, 5%). A higher proportion of linerixibat participants reported 'absent' itch (21% vs 9%) and 'very much improved' itch (55% vs 37%) at Week 24. Overall improvement was reported by 93% of linerixibat participants vs 75% of placebo participants (p=0.001). These results were consistent in the non-Metasite population.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Source: 212620 GLISTEN EOS CSR, Table 2.10. Note: Baseline is the last assessment prior to the first dose of randomized treatment for Part A.

Figure 9: PGI-S by Visit for Part A (Study 212620 GLISTEN; ITT Population)

The recall period for the PGI-C was \"since you first started this study\" so its initial assessment was at Week 4 in GLISTEN rather than at baseline. Starting at Week 4 there was a clear difference between groups with notably more participants reporting 'very much improved' itch in the linerixibat group (40%) compared to placebo (11%) and fewer linerixibat participants reporting 'no change' vs. placebo

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(12% and 29%, respectively). This trend was sustained through end of Part A, including at Week 24 where 55% of linerixibat participants reported 'very much improved' compared to 37% of placebo participants. Only 3% of linerixibat participants reported 'no change' at Week 24, whereas 24% of placebo participants reported 'no change' in their itch.

## PBC-40

PBC-40 analysis, which assesses change from baseline in HRQoL as a secondary endpoint (not adjusted for multiplicity), showed no significant differences between groups for change from baseline in PBC-40 domain scores at Week 24. A larger change from baseline in the PBC-40 Itch and Social domain scores at Week 24 was observed for the linerixibat group compared with placebo without reaching significance for the difference between groups (Itch: LS mean change from baseline, 3.47 points vs. 2.89 points; LS mean difference -0.58; 95% CI: 1.34, 0.18; p=0.132; Social: LS mean change from baseline, -2.57 vs. 2.42 points; LS mean difference -0.15; 95% CI: -1.57, 1.27; p=0.836) (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN EOS CSR, Table 2.36).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pruritogens and PD biomarkers

## Part A

Among the pruritogenic biomarkers the largest changes compared to the baseline and against placebo over 24-week treatment period was observed for IL-31. Ratio for change vs. baseline compared to placebo and over 24 weeks reached nominal significance for all PD and pruritogen parameters.

TSBA decreased from baseline in the linerixibat group (mean range across visits: -13.665 to -6.043 μmol/L), while concentrations remained relatively stable in the placebo group (mean range: -5.425 to 3.130 μmol/L).

Over 24 weeks, TSBA was reduced in the linerixibat group compared with the placebo group with the ratio in the change reaching nominal significance (0.75, 95% CI: 0.66 to 0.85; nominal p&lt;0.001]).

A mean decrease in autotaxin concentration from baseline was observed across all visits in the linerixibat group (mean range: -236.34521 to -116.81741 μg/L), while concentrations also decreased in the placebo group (mean range: -153.19279 to 8.76658 μg/L), but to lesser extent. Over 24 weeks, ratio in the change compared with the placebo group was 0.88 (95% CI: 0.82, 0.95; nominal p&lt;0.001]).

IL-31 was collected only in portion of population and the changes in absolute values on linerixibat were small compared to placebo. Over 24 weeks, ratio compared to baseline was 0.5 (95% CI: 0.43, 0.59) and ratio in the change compared to the placebo group was 0.53 (95% CI: 0.44, 0.65; nominal p&lt;0.001]), suggesting relevant difference.

C4 levels increased across visits for the linerixibat group (mean range: 24.6814 to 34.4401 μg/L), remaining relatively stable in the placebo group (mean range: -1.9278 to 2.4534 μg/L). Ratio for change vs. baseline compared to placebo and over 24 weeks reached nominal significance (2.55, 95% CI: 2.22 to 2.93; nominal p&lt;0.001).

FGF-19 concentration decreased across all visits in linerixibat group (mean range: -75.1070 to - 54.1210 ng/L), while concentrations remained relatively stable in the placebo group (mean range: - 17.5008 to 21.6931ng/L). Ratio for change vs. baseline compared to placebo and over 24 weeks reached nominal significance (0.58, 95% CI: 0.51 to 0.65; nominal p&lt;0.001).

Increase in C4 and decrease in FGF-19 suggest increase in bile acid synthesis by Week 4.

The change from baseline in TB observed in the linerixibat group when compared with the placebo group was statistically significant (mean difference vs. placebo, 2.08; 95% CI: 0.89 to 3.28). The mean TB concentrations were lower at baseline in the linerixibat group compared with the placebo group (10.899 μmol/L vs.12.527 μmol/L), whereas they were similar between the 2 groups at Week 24 (12.863 μmol/L vs.12.204 μmol/L). Majority of the participants in the 2 groups had TB within normal range at baseline and post-baseline.

There were no clinically significant differences between linerixibat and placebo groups in change from baseline in ALP at week 24.

## Part B data analyses (including post hoc analyses)

## Itch

## Mean changes in pruritus (Part B)

There was no evidence of rebound of itch (worsening relative to Part A baseline) in participants who switched from linerixibat in Part A to placebo in Part B (mean change in Weekly Itch Score from Part A baseline to Week 32: - 3.09 [±SD 2.733]). Itch scores increased following blinded withdrawal of linerixibat, but mean Weekly Itch Scores did not exceed or even return to near baseline values over the 8-week period of Part B. Mean change in Weekly Itch Score from Part B baseline to Week 32 was 0.89 (SD 2.397; Median: 0.27; Min: -2.5 and Max: 7.8; Table 2.54 in CSR).

Participants switching from placebo to linerixibat in Part B experienced small itch improvement, mean change (additional improvement) in Monthly Itch Score was -1.07 (±SD 2.056).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Source: Figure 2.3

Note: Error bars represent the standard error of the mean.

which starts after Week 24 (Visit 9).

Figure 11: Observed mean change from Part A baseline in weekly itch score-Part A and B, for participants assigned placebo in Part B (ITT Population)

In addition to the prospectively defined description of Monthly Itch Score during Part B, a post-hoc analysis of the data has been conducted. In participants who took linerixibat in Part A and who switched to placebo in Part B itch returned during the 8 weeks of Part B: the point estimate for the increase in itch during Part B being 0.79 with a 95% confidence interval that did not span 0 (0.28, 1.30). Those who had taken placebo during Part A and continued on placebo in part B experienced essentially no change in itch severity during Part B (0.02, 95% CI -0.46, 0.50).

## Updated (post hoc) summaries for achieving &lt;4 on Weekly Itch Score (missing data accounted for)

When assessing the proportion of participants achieving an absolute pruritus level consistent with absent to mild itch (Itch Score &lt;4) (after imputing missing data following the last visit for participants who discontinued treatment or withdrew from the study), a higher proportion of linerixibat-treated participants consistently achieved Weekly Itch Scores &lt;4 compared with placebo across Part A (Figures below). Patterns were consistent with received treatment for participants who switched treatment in Part B. Starting at Week 26 (2 weeks post treatment switch), a larger proportion of participants who switched to linerixibat achieved Weekly Itch Scores &lt;4 compared to those who switched to placebo (Figure 13).

Post-hoc MMRM analyses evaluating changes in the Monthly Sleep Score in Part B

Post-hoc MMRM analyses evaluating changes in the Monthly Sleep Score in Part B are included in m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Table 67.120003 and Table 67.120004.

- Participants who crossed over from placebo in Part A to linerixibat in Part B experienced a significant improvement in pruritus-related sleep interference over Part B (LS mean change -0.53; 95% CI: -1.00, -0.05) (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Table 67.120003).
- In contrast, participants who switched from linerixibat in Part A to placebo in Part B experienced a significant worsening of sleep interference (LS mean change 0.93; 95% CI: 0.42, 1.45) (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Table 67.120003).
- Participants who did not switch treatment maintained a relatively stable mean Monthly Sleep Score over Part B (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Table 67.120003).

In a complementary post-hoc analysis comparing the group of participants who remained on linerixibat throughout Part A and Part B of GLISTEN to the group who switched from linerixibat to placebo in Part B, there was a significant worsening in sleep interference among those who switched to placebo compared to participants who remained on linerixibat the entire study duration (LS mean difference 0.80; 95% CI: 0.07, 1.52; p=0.031) (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN, post-hoc Table 67.120004).

PGI-S and PGI-C (Part A and B)

## PGI-S and PGI-C categorical summaries with missing data accounted for

Updated analyses were provided with missing data imputed (i.e., for participants who discontinued treatment or withdrew from the study, missing responder data based on PGI-S after participants' last visit were imputed as non-responders reverting to their baseline PGI-S response in Part A; Missing PGI-C data following participants' last visit were imputed as \"no change\" response on PGI-C).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Part A

After imputing missing data as described above, differences in PGI response rates favouring linerixibat remained apparent over time. For example, at Week 4, 17% more participants in the linerixibat group than placebo achieved a ≥2-point reduction on PGI-S (5-point scale) (34% vs 17%), and this pattern was observed through Week 24 (39% vs 28%). Similarly, at Week 4, approximately 28% more participants receiving linerixibat than placebo reported a \"very much improved\" PGI-C response (39% vs 11%), with consistent separation through Week 24 (47% vs 34%).

For example, at Week 4, 12% of linerixibat-treated participants reported \"absent\" itch vs. 2% on placebo, and at Week 24, 20% vs. 10% reported \"absent\" itch (Figure 16).

## Figure 16: Pie Chart of PGI-S Categorical Responses at Baseline, Week 4 and Week 24 - Part A (212620 GLISTEN; ITT Population)

Similarly, when examining categorical PGI-C responses after imputing missing data as described above, a greater proportion of linerixibat-treated participants continued to report \"very much improved\" itch across Part A visits compared to placebo, including at the first and last assessments in Part A (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Figure 67.120007). At Week 4, 39% of linerixibat-treated participants reported \"very much improved\" vs. 11% on placebo, and at Week 24, 47% vs. 34% reported \"very much improved\" (Figure 17).

## Figure 17: Pie Chart of PGI-C Categorical Responses at Week 4 and Week 24 - Part A (212620 GLISTEN; ITT Population)

All revised PGI-S and PGI-C pie chart summaries for both Parts A and B are provided in m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Figure 67.120005, post-hoc Figure 67.120006, post-hoc Figure 67.120007, and post-hoc Figure 67.120008.

## Part B

Part B patterns (week 32 vs. week 24) for PGI-S and PGI-C (figures below) showed changes consistent with the received treatment/changes in the received treatment indicating that removal of active treatment caused worsening of participants' condition, while start of treatment with linerixibat led to further improvement (on top of the pronounced placebo effect):

- PGI-S
- Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to clinically relevant return/increase of pruritus: the number of cases with \"absent\" and \"mild\" pruritus decreased (from 17% to 4% for \"absent\" and 54% to 34% for \"mild\"; cumulatively from 71% to 38%; difference: -33%), while the number of cases with \"very severe\", \"severe\", and \"moderate\" itch increased considerably (cumulatively from 28% to 62%, i.e., difference: 34%; from 2% to 15% for \"very severe itch\" and 2% to 11% for \"severe itch\").
- Conversely, switch from placebo to linerixibat led to improvement of pruritus: proportion of

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

participants reporting \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch increased from 48% to 65% (difference: 17%; 4% to 15% for \"absent\" and 44% to 50% for \"mild\"), while cumulative number of \"very severe\", \"severe\" and \"moderate\" itch decreased relevantly from 52% to 35% (difference: - 17%; including relevant decrease in \"very severe\" itch from 6% to 0%, and in \"severe\" itch from 19% to 8%).

##  PGI-C

- Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to decrease in the rate of positive assessments of the received treatment: proportion of subjects reporting \"very much improved\" category decreased from 57% to 41% (difference: -16%) and cumulative proportion of subjects with \"very much improved\" togather with \"moderately mproved\" decreased from 90% on linerixibat to 69% (difference: - 21%), while the proportion of subjects reporting no benefit, or worsening in their condition (categories \"very much worse\", \"moderately worse\", \"minimally worse\", and \"no change\") increased from 4% to 18% (difference: 14%).
- Conversly, start of treatment with linerixibat in subjects on placebo led to increase in \"positive\" assessments (\"very much improved\" from 31% to 52%; difference: 21%; cumulatively with \"moderately improved\" from 60% to 77%; difference 17%), while number of patients reporting \"no change\" in their condition on placebo decreased from 29% to 4% (difference: -25%).

Note: bold formatting and shading have been introduced by the Rapporteur to indicate more relevant/significant changes.

Correlation analyses: Part A: Baseline correlations between IL-31 or autotaxin and Weekly Itch Score, and repeated-measures correlations between change from baseline in IL-31 or autotaxin and change from baseline in Weekly Itch Score in the linerixibat cohort of Phase A showed that baseline autotaxin and IL-31 concentrations significantly correlated with Weekly Itch Score. However, the Pearson's correlation coefficients were small (0.13 and 0.33 for autotaxin and IL-31, respectively) suggesting weak predictive relevance for Weekly WI-NRS score. The correlations between change from baseline in autotaxin and IL-31 and change from baseline in Weekly Itch Scores showed a similar trend of significant but weak correlation.

Part B: A similar trend was observed when assessing the correlation between biomarkers and Monthly Itch and Sleep Scores (see m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN, RPS-CLIN-195948, RPS-CLIN-195949, RPSCLIN-198241). At the final timepoint in Part B, Week 32, TSBA, autotaxin and IL-3 each weakly correlated with Monthly Sleep Score (correlation coefficients of 0.23, 0.19 and 0.31, respectively).

## Further Post hoc analyses (Parts A and B)

Key secondary sleep endpoint: analyses by baseline itch severity

A subgroup analysis was conducted to evaluate the Monthly Sleep Score by baseline itch severity category (moderate/severe) (Table 25) based on an MMRM model over 24 weeks that showed treatment by baseline interaction (p=0.0070) with larger effect seen on linerixibat compared to placebo in sleep improvement.

Subgroup analyses (itch and sleep) by baseline itch severity (severe)

A post-hoc analyses of the primary endpoint and the key secondary Week 2 endpoint (change from baseline in Weekly Itch Score at Week 2) including baseline itch severity as a categorical factor (moderate/severe) and assessing a treatment by baseline severity interaction showed no statistically significant interaction (p=0.2191 and p=0.4540). However, the estimated effect size was numerically more pronounced in participants with severe baseline itch than in the ITT population across other endpoints (Table 23). The larger effect in the severe subgroup is also illustrated by the longitudinal plot of change from baseline in Weekly Itch Score over Part A (Figure 20).

Sleep responder analyses with missing data accounted for

Analyses with the imputed data (for participants who discontinued treatment or withdrew from the study, missing data after their last visit were imputed as non-responders) sleep responder analyses were conducted among participants with moderate to severe sleep impairment at baseline using the clinically meaningful threshold for improvement (≥2.5-point reduction from baseline).

Across Part A timepoints, a higher proportion of linerixibat-treated participants than placebo consistently achieved a meaningful reduction in Monthly Sleep Score (Figure 21). In a post-hoc stratified CMH analysis using multiple imputation, the proportion of sleep responders in the linerixibat group was statistically greater (p&lt;0.05) than placebo from Week 8 onwards except for Week 20 (m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN Post-hoc Table 67.133003).

## Reduction in Monthly Sleep Score from Baseline &gt;= 2.5

<!-- image -->

Source: m5.3.5.1, 212620 GLISTEN Post-hoc Figure 67.120001.

Figure 22: Bar Chart of Monthly Sleep Score Responders (≥2.5-point reduction definition) Among Participants with Moderate to Severe Monthly Sleep Score at Baseline - Part A (212620 GLISTEN; ITT Population)

For participants who discontinued treatment or withdrew from the study in Part A, missing sleep responder data after participants' last visit were imputed as non-responders.

Moderate to severe sleep impairment is defined as a Monthly Sleep Score ≥4, supported by its alignment with the PBC-40 sleep disturbance item. A \"sometimes\" response on the PBC-40 item corresponded to a mean Sleep Interference NRS score of approximately 4, supporting this threshold (m5.3.5.1; 212620 GLISTEN post-hoc Table 67.127001).

Larger differences between groups were observed using more stringent sleep responder definitions (e.g., ≥4-point reduction from baseline in Monthly Sleep Score).

For participants that discontinued treatment or withdrew from the study in Part A, missing sleep responder data after participants' last visit were imputed as non-responders.

Moderate to severe sleep impairment is defined as Monthly Sleep Score ≥4, supported by its alignment with the PBC-40 sleep disturbance item. A sensitivity analysis was conducted in which a conservative imputation approach was taken whereby any participant with missing data at Week 24 for any reason (e.g., treatment discontinuation, study withdrawal, insufficient data for Monthly Sleep Score calculation) was imputed as a non-responder. The Cummulative Distribution Function (CDF) curve showed a clear separation between treatment groups across responder thresholds at the end of Part A (Figure 24).

## 5.3.6. Supportive study

## LLSAT: Long-term Safety and Tolerability Study of linerixibat for the Treatment of Cholestatic Pruritus in Participants with Primary Biliary Cholangitis.

LLSAT is an ongoing Phase 3, open-label, multi-centre study evaluating the long-term safety and efficacy of linerixibat in 223 participants with cholestatic pruritus, specifically those previously enrolled in earlier linerixibat studies (BAT117213, GLIMMER, or GLISTEN).

Study Design &amp; Participants: Participants were divided into two groups. Group 1 initially received 90mg BID of linerixibat, transitioning to 40mg BID after results from the GLIMMER study were available. Group 2 remained on a consistent 40mg BID dose throughout the study.

Key Objectives: The primary purpose is to assess the long-term safety of linerixibat. For participants previously enrolled in the GLISTEN study (Group 2), the study also evaluates the maintenance of itch response over time.

Enrolment &amp; Status: The study, initiated on 14 July 2020 and conducted across 86 centers in 17 countries, aims to provide data on the long-term use of linerixibat for managing cholestatic pruritus. As of the October 28, 2024, data cut-off, 215 participants had received treatment, with 165 (77%) remaining actively enrolled and 50 (23%) having withdrawn, primarily due to adverse events or withdrawal of consent. 105 subjects (50%) had completed 52 weeks of LLSAT treatment.

Only limited efficacy data for the Group 2 (150 patients from GLISTEN treated with 40 mg linerixibat BID) are being presented in the CSR.

Study population was predominantly female (95%) with a mean age of 56 years and primarily of White/Caucasian heritage (61%). Most participants were non-smokers (60%) and abstainers from alcohol (78%).

Efficacy findings:

The mean (SD) baseline Monthly Itch Score was 5.32 (2.718). Participants who responded to treatment at Week 24 generally maintained that response at Week 52, with responder rates of 70.6% (≥2-point reduction), 77.8% (≥3-point reduction), and 78.3% (≥4-point reduction) in itch score. Continuous treatment with linerixibat led to sustained improvements in sleep interference and fatigue scores over 52 weeks.

Improvement in sleep interference due to pruritus was noted early on continuous treatment and improvements were maintained over 52 weeks. An improvement from baseline in Monthly Fatigue Score was observed over 52 weeks of continuous treatment.

The mean ALP and total bilirubin values at baseline were 209.62 IU/L and 10.499 μmol/L, respectively.Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and meta-analysis)

Liver fibrosis markers (post hoc analysis):

Liver fibrosis markers (transient elastography (Fibroscan), where available, and ELF) were plotted from the start of GLISTEN until the 12-month visit in LLSAT, a timeframe which encompasses all those who have been on continuous linerixibat treatment for at least 52 weeks, albeit with different start times.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

This approach was taken because the liver fibrosis markers were only assessed at GLISTEN Baseline (visit 3) and week 24 and LLSAT Baseline visit (equivalent to GLISTEN Week 32) and every 12 months thereafter. The data were plotted by 4 cohorts, based upon GLISTEN randomization assignment. Intragroup variability was high, as evidenced by large error bars (SE), but data remained overall steady over this time period.
