The biomarkers ALP and TB, as indicators of risk of PBC progression, were plotted over time where month 0 is equal to the start of continuous linerixibat treatment in either GLISTEN or LLSAT, and all other time points represent planned study visits in either GLISTEN or LLSAT until 11 or 12 months have elapsed. Month 11 represents either LLSAT visit month 3 or month 9 for those who began continuous linerixibat dosing at the start of GLISTEN Part A or Part B, respectively.

Change from baseline for mean ALP and TB remained overall steady throughout the continuous dosing period, with variability in the 10-20% range (SE) relative to representative mean ALP and TB values from GLISTEN and LLSAT baselines (GLISTEN mean baseline ALP = 222 IU/L; TB = 12.6 umol/L; LLSAT baseline ALP = 209 IU/L; TB = 10.5 umol/L).

## 5.3.7. Observational data/RWD

Not applicable.

## 5.3.8. Patient experience data (PED)

The CHMP invited the patient organisation - Nederlandse Leverpatiënten Vereniging (Dutch Liver Patients Association)- to provide perspectives on the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adults with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC) during the assessment of linerixibat, as part of the early patient engagement. Specifically, it requested input on current treatments, unmet medical needs, quality of life, expectations for new medicines, acceptable side effects, pregnancy considerations, and any other aspects that are insufficiently understood or considered. Additionally, the patient community's views on linerixibat and the expected benefits of the medicine. The following feedback was received on 30 July 2025. The patient organisation emphasised that PBC is a severe, rare, immune-mediated liver disease with a major impact on quality of life. Although ursodeoxycholic acid is the standard treatment, there is no cure, and many patients continue to experience severe symptoms, particularly fatigue, pruritus (itch), abdominal pain, depression and anxiety. Many patients reduce or stop working, withdraw from social activities, and face a risk of cirrhosis. Patients with an inadequate response to current therapy have a significant unmet need for new treatments.

The patient community considers linerixibat to be an effective and important treatment for itch in PBC and hopes it becomes available in the near future. It highlighted that:

- Itch affects around 60% of PBC patients.
- Existing treatment options often have limited effectiveness and may carry a substantial side-effect burden.
- Severe, intractable itch can lead to liver transplantation or suicide.
- Itch severity is not related to age, sex, duration of disease, liver biochemistry or response to diseasemodifying treatment.
- Although placebo responses are high in itch trials, the study demonstrates a positive impact of

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

linerixibat on patients with itch.

· Reported stool changes were described mainly as looser stools rather than true diarrhoea.

The organisation concluded by expressing hope that these perspectives would support a positive discussion on approval of the medicine.

See also section 1.8.

## 5.3.9. Healthcare professional engagement

A healthcare professionals' organisation (European Association for the Study of the Liver (EASL) has commended this application and stated that

\"Common symptoms, including pruritus, have a strong impact in the quality of life of people with PBC. Pruritus occurs in 20-70% of patients and it is associated with sleep deprivation, depression, social isolation, and worse fatigue. Symptom management remains a critical, yet often insufficient, component of care since no treatment has yet been approved specifically for cholestatic pruritus in PBC.\"

Available treatments for pruritus include cholestiramine, rifaximine, and PPAR agonists, however they are limited by side effects and low tolerability.

IBAT inhibitors have emerged as a promising and safe new class of agents, approved for the treatment of chronic pruritus in paediatric cholestatic disorders.\"

## 5.3.10. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.10.1. Discussion

## Clinical package for efficacy

The target indication proposed is treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC). To support this indication 4 Phase 2 and 3 studies have been conducted in patients with PBC, including 184 in phase 2, and 236 in phase 3 development (65 patients who had previously been enrolled in the phase 2 GLIMMER study). Key evidence on efficacy has been generated in the pivotal phase 3 GLISTEN study. Supportive evidence particularly on dose selection was provided from the Phase 2b GLIMMER study. Data presented from the long-term extension study to GLIMMER and GLISTEN LLSAT are very limited.

Given the rarity of the disease, the limited size of the population is acceptable.

## Dose-finding

Dose finding was done in an exploratory dose-finding GLIMMER study. The chosen study design is overall acceptable for a Phase IIb exploratory dose finding study.

The study evaluated a broad range of dosing regimens (20 mg, 90 mg and 180 mg as QD, and 40 mg and 90 mg as BID doses) were tested in 147 patients with PBC over 12 weeks treatment period against placebo. Some imbalances across treatment arms (e.g., baseline level of itch and of autotaxin, which were lower on 40 mg BID) are noted, which might have impacted study outcomes.

There were no statistically significant differences between any of the linerixibat treatment groups and placebo in the change from Baseline in Mean Worst Daily Itch score at Week 16 (as measured by a 0-10 point NRS).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

However, patients with moderate to severe itch showed mean changes from Baseline in excess of 3 points for the patients treated with the 180 mg once daily, or 40 mg BID or 90 mg BID treatment groups.

The most frequently reported AEs were diarrhoea and abdominal pain, which followed a doseresponse pattern with highest incidence in the 180 mg once daily group.

The number of patients with AEs leading to discontinuation was mainly due to diarrhoea and abdominal pain; the discontinuation incidence due to diarrhoea was lowest in the 90 mg once daily and 40 mg BID groups (4% each) and most frequently reported for the 180 mg once daily and 90 mg BID treatment groups (19% and 14%, respectively). Thus, dose-finding and dose justification is burdened with multiple uncertainties and data supporting dose selection are not convincing. However, the 40 mg BID dose (and method of administration: 30 min prior to the intake of any food or beverage and at least 4 h before or after intake of a bile acid binding resin) was used in the pivotal (GLISTEN) study and is recommended in the PI, and efficacy and safety results support that this dose is acceptable.

## Key evidence of efficacy

The applicant has submitted 1 double-blind, randomised, placebo-controlled phase 3 study (Study 212620, GLISTEN) that aimed to provide the pivotal placebo-controlled efficacy and safety information for 40 mg BID linerixibat in patients with PBC-related pruritus.

This application is considered an application with only one pivotal trial. Therefore, compelling and straightforward efficacy and safety data are required (see Points to consider on application with 1, meta-analysis; 2. One pivotal trial).

## Study population

Patients were adults with PBC. Exclusion of paediatric/adolescent populations is appropriate. Confounding by prior or concomitant treatment was minimised. A key inclusion criterion was \"worst itch\" (WI). As itch often fluctuates within 24 h and across days, a threshold of 3 points on WI-NRS used in GLIMMER was likely too low. The higher threshold in GLISTEN (≥4) is considered more appropriate.

The patient population includes all stages of the condition except for the most progressed patients (e.g., decompensated liver). This is acceptable.

## Endpoints and estimands

The choice of WI as primary endpoint is acceptable, and limitations of this parameter are acknowledged as this parameter captures only the most severe pruritus episode and may overestimate burden in patients with fluctuating symptoms. The Applicant defined thresholds for meaningful within-patient change and validated response criteria in PBC to address uncertainties related with unclear clinical relevance of WI. Additional tools (PGI-S, PGI-C) were used in the pivotal study, consistent with EMA guidance, to also capture average patient-reported itch.

For weekly analyses, only one daily entry (the highest WI score) was retained to calculate the weekly mean, and for monthly analyses, the worst weekly value was carried forward to represent the month. This approach resulted in the exclusion of a large proportion of collected data: 50% of daily entries for weekly averages and 75% of weekly entries for monthly averages.

Aside from itch, quality-of-life and disease burden were assessed with PBC-40 and sleep interference NRS, with the latter as key secondary endpoint. Pruritogens and PD markers were collected. Further evaluations of mood, ALP and bilirubin (as markers of the disease progression/prognosis), liver fibrosis were collected. These are all agreed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pivotal (GLISTEN) study

## Design and analysis methodology

This was a double-blind, randomised, placebo-controlled Phase 3 study in PBC patients with moderate to severe itch (WI-NRS ≥4), with two-arm parallel treatment period of 24-weeks (Part A of the study) comparing linerixibat 40 mg BID vs placebo and an 8-week randomised withdrawal phase (Part B), when patients continued or switched treatment to primarily assess rebound/worsening of itch after withdrawal of linerixibat. Main efficacy evidence comes from Part A. Overall, design, comparator and study duration is accepted. The part A of GLISTEN study is considered the pivotal part of this application.

Patients were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio to four treatment groups ((1) linerixibat in part A and part B, (2) linerixibat in part A and placebo in part B, (3) placebo in part A and B as well as (4) placebo in part A and linerixibat in part B) at start of the treatment. Hence, at study start treatment for both parts of the study is decided. Considering that some patients discontinue the study prior to part B in a potentially treatment dependent fashion and that part B analysis is restricted to those patients not withdrawing in part A, between group comparisons in part B should be considered with caution. Specifically, for comparisons between groups that received different treatment in part A, it cannot be ensured that benefits of randomization are maintained (comparison may be biased). Considering the exploratory character of part B this may be less of a concern but should be kept in mind when discussing the results.

To address the concern on data integrity during remote data collection, subgroup analyses excluding data from \"Metasites\", and analyses considering the remotely collected data in the \"brick and mortar\" sites was done. This is agreed.

An interim analysis with partial unblinding was conducted after Part A. Measures appear adequate to protect Part B integrity.

Six protocol versions were implemented. More relevant protocol amendments 3 (incorporated new liver function test monitoring and investigational drug stopping criteria based upon serum ALP and GGT increases from baseline) and 4 (modification of the exclusion criterion on current itch-related skin conditions) likely had no relevant impact on the outcomes of the main trial, as overall number of the concerned participants at the time of their introduction was low (7 in total; 6 with itch-related skin condition) and as these participants were evenly distributed across treatment arms.

## Endpoints, Estimands and statistical methodology

The primary estimand targets the effect regardless of changes in background treatment/rescue medication usage and treatment discontinuations/interruptions/delays unrelated to COVID-19 pandemic (treatment policy strategy) and had treatment discontinuations/interruptions/delays due to COVID-19 pandemic not happened (hypothetical strategy). This estimand is overall agreed and considered reasonable.

Considering that background treatment is part of the targeted treatment strategy it is straightforward to allow changes of this and handle it with a treatment policy strategy. It might be argued whether rescue usage is best handled with a treatment policy strategy. However, no patient used rescue medication, and this discussion is not necessary.

In part A, overall 47 (%) and 62 (%) of patients experienced an ICE in the placebo and linerixibat arm. The majority of ICEs were treatment interruptions and delays (placebo; 33; linerixibat: 37).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

They did not result in study discontinuation. At least some itch score data were recorded for all these patients and in the majority of cases no data were missing. The same holds true for the ICE \"change in background/rescue medication\" (10 per treatment group). Permanent treatment discontinuations happened more frequently in the linerixibat arm compared to the placebo arm (27 vs 13). 6/8 (pbo/lin) patients discontinued the study at the same time, while for the others some or all monthly itch score data were recorded. Less than half the patients had the month 6 itch score recorded. Targeting an estimand based on the hypothetical strategy for all intercurrent events as a

supplementary estimand is supported but considered less relevant from a regulatory perspective.

The primary analysis is based on an MMRM model. In line with the targeted estimand data collected following changes in background treatment/rescue medication and following treatment discontinuation/interruption/delay unrelated to COVID-19 pandemic will be included in the analysis, while data collected following COVID-19 pandemic related treatment

discontinuations/interruptions/delays are excluded from the analysis. For the latter missing data (following COVID-19 related ICEs) a MAR assumption may be appropriate. However, for the other ICEs the MAR assumption is questionable as e.g. treatment discontinuation likely results in a loss of efficacy, and the acceptability of the MMRM analysis depends on the actual amount of missing data following the ICSs. If all or almost all data following ICEs handled with a treatment policy strategy is collected, the MMRM analysis is acceptable. Otherwise, alternative MNAR-based approaches (reference based multiple imputation) may be better aligned to the targeted estimand and should in any case be provided as further sensitivity analysis. Reference-based multiple imputation approaches (Jump to Reference) were provided and are consistent with the primary MMRM analysis supporting robustness of results.

Furthermore, robustness of results with respect to deviations from the MAR assumption for COVID19 related ICEs was appropriately assessed by means of the prespecified tipping point analyses, supporting that the extent of missing data is unlikely to impact the conclusions of the primary analysis. For the analysis to lose statistical significance, monthly itch score values for placebo patients would have to be increased by a considerable amount while values for linerixibat patients would have to have simultaneously worsened considerably compared to MAR based values.

Multiplicity control strategy (hierarchical testing) adequately controls the type 1 error in the strong sense across primary and key secondary endpoints. However, based on the results (see below), statistical significance could only be claimed for the primary and first two key secondary endpoints. For none of the key secondary responder evaluations statistical significance can be claimed.

In this study, planned futility analysis was conducted and IDMC recommended continuation of the study as planned. There is no objection against implementing an unblinded futility analysis through the IDMC.

## Validation of responder criteria

Validation for NRS scales (Worth Itch and Sleep Interference) and PBC-40 overall covers relevant steps as outlined in respective FDA guidance: amongst others content/construct validity, test-retestreliability, convergent/discriminant validity, ability to detect changes over time, and known-groups validity are covered. Exploration of MICDs for responder definitions primarily used anchor-based methods applying relevant anchors (PGI-S and PGI-C) but also considered distribution-based methods. Hence, overall the validation approach seems acceptable.

Still, especially considering that the NRS scales are used as the primary/key secondary endpoints in phase 3 (GLISTEN), their validation should have been finalized prior to conducting GLISTEN to ensure independence of validation and their appropriateness for confirmatory assessment. To the most part this principle was followed. One component where blinded GLISTEN data was used is the evaluation of the meaningful change threshold (MCT) via an anchor-based approach. However, prior to GLISTEN meaningful change threshold was already evaluated based on anchor-based analyses using phase 2b GLIMMER data as well as the observational validation study and qualitative insights from PBC patients. anchor-based MCT estimates for different data sources and shows that similar results are obtained for all data sources. This supports that the pre-GLISTEN data were the fundament of defining the meaningful change threshold and that GLISTEN data are in line with this. Furthermore, the anchor-based analyses using data from the Phase 3 GLISTEN study were conducted at the request of the US FDA to confirm the MCT for the WI-NRS.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Clinical relevance of a 1-point PGI-S decrease and \"moderate\" PGI-C as markers of improvement was quested by the CHMP, as well as the relevant categories (\"moderately improved\" and \"very much improved\") on the PGI-C in terms of their meaningfulness for patients. In GLIMMER, the Participant Treatment Experience Assessment (PTEA) was utilised with the assessment of patients' satisfaction (\"satisfied\"/\"very satisfied\"). Evaluation of the threshold for clinically relevant within patient change in PGI-S and as per category on PGI-C vs. PTEA (categories \"satisfied\"+\"very satisfied\", and \"very satisfied\") based on the data in GLISTEN study was requested. For the PGI-S scale, the Applicant's opinion that 1-point reduction represents meaningful change is not fully supported. The median, Q1 and Q3 show that the majority of the patients with the responses \"very satisfied\", \"satisfied\" and \"somewhat satisfied\" had experienced the change in pruritus severity by 1 point on PGI-S, it is assumed that the PGI-S as a scale is insufficiently sensitive to differentiate between responders and non-responders, or to define which change in pruritus was clinically relevant for patients based on this scale. Regarding the proposal of use of ≥2-point reduction in PGIS as a responder criterion, information on such change is too limited in the GLIMMER population to draw any conclusions. Thus, the post hoc responder analysis based on the PGI-S scale is regarded less reliable.

For PGI-C, mean derived scores in the \"satisfied\" and \"very satisfied\" PTEA groups corresponded most closely to the categorical responses \"moderately improved\" (score \"2\") and \"very much improved\" (score \"1\"), respectively. The Applicant considered these two PGI-C response categories to represent clinically meaningful change and were used to define responder thresholds. This is agreed only partly. Judging from the median and Q1/Q3 values, it seems that the category of \"very much improved\" on the PGI-C scale (score \"1\") represents patients with who were very satisfied with treatment and majority of patients, which were satisfied with treatment. The score of \"2\" on the PGI-C (\"moderately improved\") has clear overlap with the category of \"somewhat satisfied\", that is not considered highly clinically meaningful. Consequently, use of the score \"1\" (\"very much improved\") on PGI-C is accepted as a clinically relevant definition for response, whereas score \"2\" on the same likely reflects less pronounced efficacy and seems less robust for evaluation of clinically relevant response.

## Study conduct and results

The study was conducted under GCP, with quality assurance in place. No critical audit findings were reported.

The trial enrolled 376 patients at 115 centres across 19 countries; 238 were randomised (118 placebo, 119 linerixibat). Compliance was high (82-92%).

Dropout was higher with linerixibat (23% vs 11%). Part B had further imbalances due to additional withdrawals.

The population was mostly female, white and middle-aged, with PBC-related pruritus (assessed with WI-NRS) of moderate to severe intensity. The population seems broadly representative of the target group. However, large portion of population had compensated PBC (as judged based on ALP), and was at relatively early stage of PBC (judging from the results of histology), also patients with liver decompensation were excluded from the study. Treatment arms appear well balanced. The population is considered largely representative. Absence of data in those with hepatic decompensation and with Child-Pugh B and C has been reflected as limitation in 4.4 of the SmPC, including recommendation that linerixibat should not be used in patients with Child-Pugh C and liver decompensation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The relevance of the low baseline levels of biomarkers for cholestasis and pruritogenic biomarkers was evaluated by means of correlation analyses (post hoc). The Applicant has presented baseline correlations between IL-31 or autotaxin and Weekly Itch Score, and repeated-measures correlations between change from baseline in IL-31 or autotaxin and change from baseline in Weekly Itch Score in the linerixibat cohort of Phase A. The correlations were partly nominally significant but weak, with IL-31 showing stronger association than autotaxin. The Applicant has explained that PBC-related pruritus is multifactorial (related with TSBA, IL-31, and lysophosphatidic acid (LPA), that is estimated by surrogate marker autotaxin) and capturing of the link between pruritogens (at baseline and changes) and the symptom of pruritus is difficult. Overall, the question of the relevance of the relatively low levels of pruritogenic biomarkers at the baseline in regards to the strength of the effect remains somewhat uncertain and weak correlations provide limited objective support for a direct biomarker-efficacy relationship.

Efficacy results - Part A

Reduction in itch was observed on linerixibat, but placebo effect was also pronounced. The placeboadjusted difference (Least square mean of 0.68 or 0.72 points on WI-NRS in favour of linerixibat, non-Metasite and ITT analyses, respectively) was small. Similar effect was observed across all parameters related to itch. The clinical relevance of the difference between treatment groups was therefore questioned (see further below). Additionally, a concern of possible partial unblinding due to the multiple cases of diarrhoea was raised. To demonstrate that the observed effect over placebo was due to the intervention and not the result of an unintentional unblinding due to the presence of diarrhea, multiple sensitivity analyses (Analyses incorporating diarrhea as a covariate including interaction testing, covariate adjusted and time-dependent MMRM models, subgroup comparisons, responder analyses, and graphical assessments, and MMRM analyses of primary endpoint restricted to the latter portion of the study), showed no statistically significant main effect of diarrhoea and no evidence of a treatment-by-diarrhoea interaction. Furthermore, treatment effects remained directionally consistent in participants with and without diarrhoea, and sensitivity analyses including diarrhoea as a covariate, including as a time-dependent covariate, did not alter the overall conclusion in favour of linerixibat. Differences in the change in itch severity between linerixibat and placebo treatments (mean values, responders, at different time points, with and without \"diarrhoea\" as covariate) remain in the same range as the difference observed in the primary analysis. In spite residual uncertainty data are considered reassuring.

Supplementary and subgroup analyses did not show any major effects of the way how data were collected, missing data, age, sex, race, ethnicity, baseline TSBA, baseline concomitant fibrate use, baseline cholestyramine use, baseline concomitant cholestatic pruritus therapy, baseline and concomitant BABR use, and baseline UDCA. However, some of the subgroups were very small (e.g., baseline concomitant BABR) and the respective results are insufficiently robust. In per region analysis no difference to placebo was observed in Europe. No clear explanation for this fact could be found.

Categorical analyses of the PGI-S and PGI-C scores are generally in line with the primary efficacy assessments showing high placebo effect. However, differences across treatment arms are more pronounced and can be considered partly clinically relevant in contrast to the mean values of Wi- NRS, where the differences were small.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

PGI-C: Rates of the participants reporting \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch was much higher on linerixibat treatment (54% to 68%) compared to placebo (28% to 51%) and did not overlap between the two groups. In spite of considerable decrease in the rate of participants with \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch on linerixibat difference to placebo remained small (range: 7% to 21% on linerixibat compared to 48% at the baseline; Range: 13% to 24% on placebo from the baseline value of 55%), suggesting that some patients with severe/very severe itch remain resistant to treatment.

Notably, during the part B of the study, the rates of participants with \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch in those switched to placebo considerably increased (from 4% on linerixibat to 29% and 26% after 4 and 8 weeks after switching to placebo). Reverse effects were observed in the PLA-LIN cohort, where rates of \"severe\"/\"very severe\" itch cases relevantly decreased (from 25% on placebo to 6% and 8% after 4 and 8 weeks on linerixibat). Rates of the participants with \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch increased after switch from placebo to linerixibat (from 48% on placebo to 76% and 65% after 4 and 8 weeks on linerixibat) but decreased after switch from linerixibat to placebo (from 71% on linerixibat to 48% and 39% after 4 and 8 weeks on placebo).

PCI-C: The recall period for the PGI-C was \"since you first started this study\" so its initial assessment was at Week 4 in GLISTEN rather than at baseline. Starting at Week 4 there was a clear difference between groups with notably more participants reporting 'very much improved' itch in the linerixibat group (40%) compared to placebo (11%) and fewer linerixibat participants reporting 'no change' vs. placebo (12% and 29%, respectively). This trend was sustained through end of Part A, including at Week 24 where 55% of linerixibat participants reported 'very much improved' compared to 37% of placebo participants. Only 3% of linerixibat participants reported 'no change' at Week 24, whereas 24% of placebo participants reported 'no change' in their itch.

Assessments of PGI-S and PGI-C categorical data over Part A were considered suggestive of clinically relevant differences across the categories reflecting the categories informative for assessment of response (e.g., \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch, and \"absent\" and \"mild\" itch on PGIS; \"not changed\" and \"very much improved\" on PGI-C). However, missing patients were not considered in the analyses. The Applicant has provided the updated categorical analyses of PGI-S and PGI-C, where study or treatment discontinuation were treated as non-response (PGI-S data after treatment discontinuation or withdrawal as non-response by assigning baseline PGI-S status) or as \"no change\" (for PGI-C).

Data remained similar to the analyses provided with original submission. Specifically, a greater proportion of participants on linerixibat than placebo reported improvement on PGI-S and PGI-C in Part A (Week 24). Concretely, proportion of subjects with absent or mild itch (PGI-S) increased form 10% to 66% on linerixibat and from 9% to 51% on placebo (20% vs. 10% with itch absent on linerixibat and placebo, respectively). Proportion of subjects with the remaining categories showed roughly similar changes on both treatment arms. E.g., \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch decreased from 47% to 11% (from 11% to 4% for \"very severe\") on linerixibat and from 55% to 20% (from 12% to 5% for \"very severe\") on placebo.

For PGI-C in part A (week 24) proportion of participant with highly clinically relevant response - \"very much improved\" - that is considered to reflect patients' satisfaction with received treatment most adequately (see above), was reported in almost half of the linerixibat-treated population and was higher than on placebo (47% vs. 34%). Combined frequency of \"very much improved\" and \"moderately improved\" cases also showed higher benefit on linerixibat (69% vs. 55%, respectively), while higher proportion of subjects had no change on placebo compared to linerixibat (30% vs. 17%, respectively).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

While these results show smaller differences across treatment arms, these are considered reassuring and supportive of added clinical benefit compared to the pure placebo effect. Percentage of days per week without itch, with itch severity of ≤2, and with a ≥3-point reduction from baseline in itch, monthly WI-NRS &lt;4 points and weekly WI-NRS &lt;4 (part A) was evaluated and showed consistent results with other itch-related parameters. Data are difficult to interpret, as observed data were used and no imputation strategies were applied. Rates of the days without itch were low in both treatment groups (8.6% vs 6.4%). Larger percentage of the days where itch was absent or very mild (the worst Daily Itch Score was ≤2) was reported over 24 weeks, with median value suggesting better efficacy on linerixibat (mean: 32.0% vs. 22.4%; median: 24.2% vs. 5.0%). The Applicant states that at baseline patients had daily itch of at least 4 point intensity and considers these changes relevant in this light. As an alternative, %age of the patients with &lt;4 point weekly worst itch intensity (mild intensity) was presented over 24 weeks of treatment, that shows consistently higher rates on linerixibat compared to placebo. The proportion of days with a ≥3-point reduction from baseline compared to placebo did not show clinically relevant difference (mean: 39.6% vs 34.2%; median: 42.8% vs. 32.7%). Notably, dropouts/permanent treatment discontinuations and other missing data (e.g., missing entries) were not accounted for in these analyses and might have impacted the outcomes. Generally, it is noted that the week 24 differences in itch (and other efficacy parameters) to placebo are smaller compared to those at earlier visits/weeks. The mechanism of action of linerixibat and the study data indicate that the full effects of linerixibat is likely developed at week 8. Within this context smaller difference to placebo at week 24 is suggestive of some type of bias. Consequently, efficacy data (and changes in biomarkers) evaluated over 24 weeks and using imputation strategies are regarded more reliable. However, this approach was not consistently used throughout various analyses presented and, in fact, rather small number of data are based on this approach. The Applicant presented additional summaries of participants achieving Monthly Itch Score &lt;4 and Weekly Itch Score &lt;4, with missing post-discontinuation or post-withdrawal values imputed as nonresponse. The absolute threshold of &lt;4 is clinically interpretable and aligns with \"tolerable\" itch severity. The analyses are supportive and directionally consistent with the primary endpoint and other efficacy parameters, showing large placebo effect, modest differentiation from placebo, but consistently better effects achieved on linerixibat. The Applicant argues that baseline itch severity is an important determinant of response and was a stratification factor. Subgroup analyses were presented for the ITT population and severe baseline itch subgroup. For Monthly Sleep Score, the Applicant has provided MMRM subgroup analyses by baseline itch severity and repeated them in participants with moderate to severe sleep impairment at baseline. Part B sleep analyses were also summarised. Missing values (e.g., due to dropouts) were replaced with \"non-response\" in various responder analyses based on itch severity and sleep impairment. These data indicate that the treatment effect (reduction in pruritus, improvement in sleep) is numerically larger in participants with severe baseline itch and/or greater baseline sleep impairment. The difference varies but is consistent across various analyses and definitions. This is plausible, as patients with greater baseline burden have more room for improvement. Moderate baseline itch subgroup showed no (convincing) treatment benefit. Thus, evidence for benefits in moderate baseline itch remains weak. The evidence of efficacy in the subgroup with higher baseline burden of itch is considered supportive. Although, various limitations (post hoc analysis, partly not validated

definitions of response, etc.) are acknowledged.

Pruritogens and biomarkers Mean changes in TSBA, autotaxin, and IL-31 were small and not clearly supportive of efficacy. Many patients had baseline levels within normal ranges, limiting scope for improvement and questioning the adequacy of the selected population. The Applicant has provided multiple subgroup analyses evaluating the possible link between linerixibat and the changes in the pruritogenic biomarkers, as well as estimating the relevance of changes in terms of response. Additionally, sensitivity analyses with pruritogenic and PD biomarkers in part B of the study containing data imputation and correlation analyses of pruritogens (IL-31 and autotaxin as surrogate) vs. itch severity/change in itch severity were conducted. Correlation analyses showed weak levels of correlation between IL-31 and autotaxin and itch severity/change in itch severity. Somewhat more pronounced correlation was observed with IL-31. However, the number of patients with IL-31 and the strength of evidence is limited. The Applicant justifies the lack of strong correlation with multifactorial nature of pruritus in PBC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

It has been noted that autotaxin decreased also on placebo. This was explained with natural fluctuations by the Applicant.

C4 and FGF-19 changes suggest enhanced bile acid production, consistent with IBAT inhibition, supporting the mechanism of action of linerixibat. Increase in TSBA towards end of the 24-weeks treatment with linerixibat raised the question whether this may reflect compensatory elevation in bile aced synthesis, with the increased risk of worsening of cholestasis. However, this change does not coincide with the elevations in C4, which took place at the start with linerixibat treatment and remained stable over the 24-weeks treatment period, supporting the lack of causal relationship.

Sensitivity analyses of pruritogens and PD biomarkers in Part B suggest changes (regardless of their effect size) consistent with the received treatment which can be considered supportive of the mechanism of action.

## Efficacy results - Part B

This study phase is considered exploratory. No rebound effects were observed. Patients switched from linerixibat to placebo reported only slight worsening in mean weekly WI-NRS by less than 1 point on 0-10 WI-NRS by the end of the 8-week placebo-treatment period. Multiple additional post hoc analyses support the original analyses, suggesting that the additional effect (compared to placebo) was less than 1 point at the group level (around mean 0.7 points on WI-NRS). Values did not reach baseline levels before study start and median value showed that the itch severity did not worsen in at least half of the population (median change: 0.27 on placebo compared to linerixibat). These data raised concerns about the efficacy of linerixibat, as they appeared to indicate smaller worsening of itch after the treatment discontinuation than it would be anticipated and lack of full recurrence of itch after switch from linerixibat to placebo suggesting that a substantial portion of the anti-pruritic (itch-reducing) effect attributed to linerixibat may likely be due to placebo effect.

The data from PGI-S and PGI-C are regarded clinically relevant as these are easily interpretable and can reflect patients' satisfaction with the received treatment. However, provided data were not interpretable without the adequate \"baseline\" value (at week 24 in the subgroups treated) and due to inappropriate handling of missings. An updated analyses of PGI-S and PGI-C was provided where missing data of the subjects discontinuing treatment/study were replaced with baseline values (PGIS) or \"no change\" category (PGI-C), and utilising suitable baseline (i.e., week 24).

Part B patterns (week 32 vs. week 24) for PGI-S and PGI-C showed changes consistent with the received treatment/changes in the received treatment indicating that removal of active treatment caused worsening of participants' condition, while start of treatment with linerixibat led to further improvement on top of the pronounced placebo effect:

- PGI-S

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to clinically relevant return/increase of pruritus: the number of cases with \"absent\" and \"mild\" pruritus decreased (from 17% to 4% for \"absent\" and 54% to 34% for \"mild\"; cumulatively from 71% to 38%; difference: -33%), while the number of cases with \"very severe\", \"severe\", and \"moderate\" itch increased considerably (cumulatively from 28% to 62%, i.e., difference: 34%; from 2% to 15% for \"very severe itch\" and 2% to 11% for \"severe itch\").
- Conversly, switch from placebo to linerixibat led to improvement of pruritus: proportion of participants reporting \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch increased from 48% to 65% (difference: 17%; 4% to 15% for \"absent\" and 44% to 50% for \"mild\"), while cumulative number of \"very severe\", \"severe\" and \"moderate\" itch decreased relevantly from 52% to 35% (difference: - 17%; including relevant decrease in \"very severe\" itch from 6% to 0%, and in \"severe\" itch from 19% to 8%).
- PGI-C
- Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to decrease in the rate of positive assessments of the received treatment: proportion of subjects reporting \"very much improved\" category decreased from 57% to 41% (difference: -16%) and cumulative proportion of subjects with \"very much improved\" togather with \"moderately mproved\" decreased from 90% on linerixibat to 69% (difference: - 21%), while the proportion of subjects reporting no benefit, or worsening in their condition (categories \"very much worse\", \"moderately worse\", \"minimally worse\", and \"no change\") increased from 4% to 18% (difference: 14%).
- Conversly, start of treatment with linerixibat in subjects on placebo led to increase in \"positive\" assessments (\"very much improved\" from 31% to 52%; difference: 21%; cumulatively with \"moderately improved\" from 60% to 77%; difference 17%), while number of patients reporting \"no change\" in their condition on placebo decreased from 29% to 4% (difference: -25%).

Although, these data are exploratory in their nature, these can be considered clinically relevant and supportive of efficacy.

To summarise, the efficacy package for linerixibat demonstrates modest but consistent treatment effect in patients with cholestatic pruritus associated with PBC. Across Part A of the pivotal study, statistically favourable effects versus placebo were observed for the primary endpoint and multiple secondary efficacy measures related to itch severity. A pronounced placebo response resulted in a small placebo-adjusted treatment effect on the primary WI-NRS endpoint (approximately 0.7 points), raising uncertainty regarding the clinical relevance of the mean treatment difference.

However, despite the modest effect size on continuous itch scales, categorical patient-reported outcomes (PGI-S and PGI-C) demonstrated more pronounced differentiation from placebo. A higher proportion of linerixibat-treated participants reported absent or mild itch, clinically meaningful improvement, and lower rates of unchanged symptoms compared with placebo. These findings were generally maintained after conservative handling of missing data and are considered supportive of a clinically relevant treatment benefit.

The exploratory treatment-switch phase (Part B) further supports efficacy. Withdrawal of linerixibat was associated with worsening of pruritus-related outcomes, while initiation of linerixibat after placebo resulted in improvement across multiple patient-reported endpoints. Although the magnitude of these changes was moderate and interpretation is limited by the exploratory nature of the analyses, the directionality of effects was consistent with a treatment-related benefit.

Subgroup analyses suggest that treatment benefit is greatest in patients with severe baseline pruritus and/or substantial sleep impairment. In contrast, evidence of efficacy in patients with moderate baseline itch remains limited.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The robustness of the efficacy findings is supported by multiple sensitivity analyses addressing potential unblinding due to diarrhoea. These analyses did not identify a significant effect of diarrhoea on treatment outcomes and did not materially alter the efficacy conclusions. Nevertheless, residual uncertainty remains because of the high placebo response and the overall small placebo-adjusted treatment effect.

Mechanistic support from pruritogenic biomarkers was limited. Changes in TSBA, autotaxin and IL31 were generally small and showed weak correlations with clinical outcomes. In contrast, pharmacodynamic markers (C4 and FGF-19) demonstrated changes consistent with IBAT inhibition and support the proposed mechanism of action.

## LLSAT study - maintenance of effects

An interim analysis has been submitted. Efficacy data are limited. The Applicant claims maintenance of effects, but evidence is weakened by pronounced drop in patient numbers over time. Liver fibrosis and disease progression markers (bilirubin and ALP) were analysed together with the data from GLISTEN and showed no apparent changes suggesting relatively stable course of the background disease.

## 5.3.10.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

Overall, the totality of evidence indicates a modest but consistent efficacy for linerixibat across clinically relevant endpoints including pruritus severity, patient global assessments and sleep-related outcomes. The evidence shows clinically more meaningful benefit in patients with severe baseline pruritus. Efficacy can be considered sufficiently substantiated.

## 5.4. Clinical safety

Please refer to the table of studies in section 5.1.2

For the purpose of this document, the following definitions apply:

'Adverse event - AE' means any untoward medical occurrence in a subject to whom a medicinal product is administered, and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment.

'Serious adverse event - SAE' means any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose requires inpatient hospitalisation or prolongation of existing hospitalisation, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, results in a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is life-threatening, or results in death. The definition (in line with ICH E2A) includes important medical events that may not be immediately life-threatening or result in death or hospitalisation but may jeopardise the patient or may require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes listed in the definition above.

'Adverse drug reaction - ADR' means any untoward and unintended response to a medicinal product related to any dose administered, for which, after a thorough assessment, a causal relationship between the medicinal product and the adverse event is at least a reasonable possibility, based for example, on their comparative incidence in clinical trials, or findings from epidemiological studies and/or on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports.

## 5.4.1. Safety data collection

The safety data for linerixibat was collected from two Phase 3 studies, the placebo-controlled GLISTEN study and the open label LLSAT, 2 Phase 2 studies in cholestatic pruritus in PBC, GLIMMER and BAT117213, two Phase 2 studies in T2DM, 200185 and 201351, and eight Phase 1 studies including 1 study in participants with hepatic impairment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the pooled safety analysis, data were derived from Part A of the pivotal Phase 3 GLISTEN study and from the Phase 2 GLIMMER study. Part A of GLISTEN evaluated linerixibat 40 mg BID over a treatment duration of 24 weeks, in GLIMMER only subjects treated with linerixibat 40 mg BID were included in the pooled analysis. Treatment duration in GLIMMER was 12 weeks.

The results of the safety analysis are presented in a manner that allows the comparison of the safety profile of linerixibat with placebo across different subgroups. The analysis included exposure-adjusted incidence rates, study-adjusted incidence rate ratios, and 95% CI for selected AEs. Subgroup analyses were performed within the pooled safety dataset by age, race, sex, ethnicity, and disease stage in terms of the presence/absence of cirrhosis at baseline.

The safety data collection and analysis for linerixibat are considered comprehensive. The pooling approach is regarded as appropriate.

It is noted, that due to differences in the data capture of AESIs between the studies, data was not pooled, and analyses of AESIs were therefore based on the data from Part A of GLISTEN only and not of Glimmer. Furthermore, since no SAEs were reported in the linerixibat 40 mg BID or placebo groups in GLIMMER (n = 23), analyses of SAEs are derived from data of Part A of GLISTEN only.

In conclusion, the pooled safety analysis provides a comprehensive overview of the safety profile of linerixibat at the therapeutic dose. Nevertheless, the shorter treatment duration and limited sample size in GLIMMER should be taken into account when interpreting the long-term safety data.

## 5.4.2. Patient exposure

The clinical safety information submitted for linerixibat includes data from 596 subjects treated with at least one dose of the medication in clinical studies. By development phase, these are

- Phase 1: 257 subjects
- Phase 2: 184 subjects

- Phase 3: 236 subjects, including 65 patients transferring from the phase 2 GLIMMER study

150 subjects who participated in the open-label phase 3 LLSAT study had previously been enrolled in the phase 3 GLISTEN study.

In blinded placebo-controlled studies, 282 patients were exposed to linerixibat, 194 patients were exposed to the proposed dose range. Of these, 103 subjects were treated for 5.5 months and 45 for 7.4 months.

Including exposure time from antecedent studies, 107 patients have long-term safety data with exposure of 12 months or more.

The clinical safety data for linerixibat comply with the EMA's requirements for long-term exposure. The requirement for at least 100 patients to be exposed for 12 months or more is met regarding orphan designation, with 107 patients treated with dosage levels intended for clinical use.

The demographic and baseline characteristics of the study population from the GLISTEN, GLIMMER, and LLSAT studies are generally comparable across treatment groups. The majority of participants were female (95%), with a mean age of 55.7 years. The age distribution across the studies shows that the proportion of patients older than 65 years is relatively consistent, ranging from 22% in GLIMMER to 27% in LLSAT, with 25% in GLISTEN. Patients older than 75 years are only reported in the GLISTEN study with a total of 9 (3.8 %).

Most of the participants were of White-White/Caucasian/European heritage (60%), followed by participants of Asian-East Asian heritage and Asian- Japanese heritage (14% each). 2 black or African American were included (&lt;1%), both in the treatment group.

In the pooled safety dataset, disease characteristics were balanced between treatment groups. 23/142 (16 %) patients in the treatment groups and 14/128 (11 %) in the placebo group were reported with cirrhosis at baseline. However, histologically confirmed stage 4 cirrhosis was reported for 7 (5%) participants in the linerixibat group and none in the placebo group.

The most frequently reported current medical conditions other than PBC varied across the studies but included hyperlipidaemia, hypertension, and hypothyroidism. Concomitant medications were frequent, with most participants receiving UDCA (ursodeoxycholic acid) and some receiving background pruritus therapy, including antihistamines, opioid receptor antagonists, and bile acid binding resins. In GLISTEN, a notable imbalance was observed in concomitant use of loperamide: 18 (15%) participants in the linerixibat group versus only 1 (&lt;1%) participants in the placebo group.

## 5.4.3. Adverse events

The description and reporting of AEs and the distinction between events that meet the AE definition and those that do not as outlined in Appendix 3 of the study protocols aligns with ICH E2A Clinical Safety Data Management including the definition and reporting of SAE and CV events.

All integrated safety analyses of AEs were performed based on a \"while on treatment\" approach (i.e., safety measures which occur post discontinuation of study intervention which are not classified as intervention emergent were excluded).

Exposure and study adjusted incidence rate ratios and 95% CI reported for selected AEs have been derived from Poisson regression model with study and treatment as explanatory variables.

Most TEAEs were Grade 1 or 2 in severity in all treatment groups.

In Study 214656 (food effect study), the proportion of participants reporting AEs was numerically higher in the fed state compared with the fasted state, driven by a greater number of gastrointestinal AEs in the fed state. Since instructions in the Phase 2 and 3 studies were to administer linerixibat at least 30 minutes before food or beverage (other than water) for both the morning and evening doses, more information is not available.

## 5.4.3.1. Adverse drug reactions

Table 28: Summary of ADRs proposed for inclusion by the applicant in the SmPC

| Gastrointestinal disorders                 | Gastrointestinal disorders   |
|--------------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| Diarrhoea                                  | Very common                  |
| Abdominal pain                             | Very common                  |

In the pooled safety dataset, 91 participants (64%; 374.17/100 PY) in the linerixibat groups and 30 participants (23%; 68.45/100 PY in the placebo groups, experienced at least 1 ADR. In the OL Study LLSAT, 47% participants experienced at least 1 ADR.

## Gastrointestinal disorders

Diarrhoea is a known class effect of IBAT inhibitors and has been observed in association with linerixibat as a very common ADR. Abdominal pain has also been reported in association with other IBAT inhibitors and was identified as a very common ADR in association with linerixibat

Gastrointestinal AEs, in particular diarrhoea and abdominal pain, represent the most frequent and consistently observed ADRs with linerixibat 40 mg BID, occurring at clearly higher incidence rates compared with placebo and across both controlled and long-term open-label studies.

Accordingly, the most frequently reported ADR (incidence ≥10%) in the linerixibat group were diarrhoea and abdominal pain. Exposure adjusted incidence of treatment-related diarrhoea and abdominal pain were higher in the linerixibat group compared with placebo (diarrhoea: 70 participants [49%; 243.22 per 100 PY] vs 17 [13%; 34.98 per 100 PY]; abdominal pain: 17 participants [12%; 34.38 per 100 PY] vs 2 participants [2%; 3.76 per 100 PY)].

In LLSAT, the most commonly reported ADRs were diarrhoea (75 [35%; 46.79 per 100 PY]) and abdominal pain (16 [7%; 6.06 per 100 PY]).

## Investigations

Increases in ALT and AST have been reported in association with other IBAT inhibitors. These laboratory changes were disproportionately reported as AEs in participants receiving linerixibat compared with placebo in the pooled safety dataset.

In the pooled safety data set, in the treatment group 8/67 patients with elevated ALT at baseline showed an increase in ALT of &gt;1.5 x baseline compared with 2/62 in the placebo group. In the treatment group 6/72 patients with elevated AST at baseline showed an increase in AST of &gt;1.5 x baseline compared with 0/62 in the placebo group.

## Severe ADRs

In the pooled safety analysis, a total of 15 participants (11%) in the linerixibat group and 6 participants (5%) in the placebo group experienced severe ADRs. Severe AEs of abdominal pain and diarrhoea were reported in 4 (3%) and 3 (2%) participants, respectively, in the linerixibat 40 mg BID treatment group compared with none in the placebo group.

In the long-term open label treatment, 23 participants (11%) experienced severe TEAEs. Severe AEs of diarrhoea and abdominal pain were reported each in 2 (&lt;1%) participants.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.4. Adverse events of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

## 5.4.4.1. Adverse events of special interest

Linerixibat (GSK2330672) is a selective small molecule that reversibly inhibits the IBAT. Interruption of the entero-hepatic circulation of bile acids by inhibition of the principal bile acid transporter, IBAT, should lead to increased bile acid excretion in faeces and reduced levels in circulation. The most common adverse events reported across the trials included were gastrointestinal, mainly diarrhoea and abdominal pain, which is likely due to the mechanism of action of IBAT blockers. Bile acids are diverted to the large intestine, which may lead to the aforementioned ADRs. Therefore, diarrhoea has been included as an AESI in the key safety studies GLISTEN, GLIMMER, and LLSAT.

IBAT inhibitors (e.g., Odevixibat, Maralixibat) have also been associated with increased ALT. Elevated ALT reported as a Liver Event was also included as an AESI due to initial observations of elevations of ALT in the early clinical development program. In the phase 2 Study GLIMMER, an elevated ALT value that met stopping criteria was considered an AESI. In the phase 3 studies GLISTEN and LLSAT, an elevated ALT value reported as a 'Liver Event' (i.e., met Liver Monitoring or Stopping criteria) was considered an AESI.

The following other important events were identified and included in the analyses for the Phase 3 studies (GLISTEN and LLSAT) and the pooled safety dataset:

- Drug related hepatic disorders (MedDRA SMQ)
- Diarrhoea (excl. infective) (MedDRA HLT)
- Cholecystitis and Cholelithiasis (MedDRA HLT)
- Gastrointestinal and Abdominal pain (excl. oral and throat) (MedDRA HLT)
- Abdominal discomfort (MedDRA PT)

The following FMQs were also included in the analyses for the pooled safety dataset: 'Diarrhoea', 'Cholecystitis', 'Abdominal pain', and 'Hepatic injury''.

The most common AEs across the trials included were gastrointestinal, mainly diarrhoea and abdominal pain.

## Diarrhoea

In GLISTEN and LLSAT, investigators were asked to provide additional information for the purpose of characterizing the AESIs. This included investigator determination of severity by NCI CTCAE grade [National Cancer Institute, 2017] and the use of antidiarrheal agents in the management of diarrhoea and the outcomes of these approaches. Since the definition of diarrhoea AESI was inconsistent between GLIMMER and GLISTEN in the pooled safety database, the HLT of 'Diarrhoea (excluding infective)' was used to capture and analyse the data.

In the pooled safety data set, far more participants experienced AEs within the HLT of 'Diarrhoea (excluding infective)' in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group than in the placebo group, (84/142 [59%] vs 23/128 [18%]; adjusted rate ratio [95% CI]: 6.80 (4.27, 10.83). Most events started with the first week of treatment and most of them were mild to moderate; 3 of them were of severe intensity (Table 29). In the pivotal study GLISTEN, there were 5 patients reported who experienced diarrhoea of CTCAE Grade 3 (Table 31) vs 1 in the placebo group. 7 % of affected patients had three or more recurrences. No cases were classified as SAEs. 6 of the 84 patients reporting diarrhoea (7 %) discontinued study treatment in the linerixibat group and 1/23 (&lt;1 %) in the placebo group.

In Glisten, subjects with the event diarrhoea, were more frequently treated with anti-diarrhoeal medication in the treatment group (31/70 [44%]) compared with placebo (5/22 [23%]). Resolution with anti-diarrhoeal therapy was achieved in 68% of these patients (21/31) in the treatment-group and 80% (4/5) in the placebo-group.

In LLSAT, a total of 91 (42%) participants experienced diarrhoea with an exposure-adjusted incidence rate of 214.46/100 PY in the linerixibat 90 mg BID group and 36.92/100 PY in the 40 mg BID group. Most events were of mild to moderate severity. Severe events were reported in 2/91 affected participants (2 %), and CTCAE Grade 3 diarrhoea in 3 of them (3%). No CTCAE Grade 4 or 5 events were reported, and none were classified as serious. In the linerixibat 40 mg BID group, 5 % of the events led to treatment discontinuation. The time to event analysis indicated the first reports of diarrhoea in participants occurred within the first week of treatment.

Duration of diarrhoea was derived from each individual Case Report Form's AE Log start and end dates (as per investigator judgement, subject recall, and AE reporting guidelines provided in protocol). The median duration of all instances of AE of diarrhoea was 10 days with a range of 1 to 230 days in the pooled linerixibat 40 mg BID group. In GLISTEN, 7 % of the affected patients had ≥ 3 recurrences. This rate is not reported for GLIMMER and LLSAT.

A retrospective analysis of the phase 2 study GLIMMER showed that 17/23 patients receiving the intended dose of 40 mg BID used ant-diarrheal treatment (74 %) vs. 1/36 in the placebo group. In the phase 3 Study GLISTEN, anti-diarrheal treatment (loperamide) was recommended for participants if diarrhoea impacted their daily life. In Part A of the study, among subjects experiencing diarrhoea anti-diarrheal therapy was administered more frequently in the treatment group (31/70 [44%]) compared with placebo (5/22 [23%]). Resolution with anti-diarrhoeal therapy was achieved in 68% of these patients (21/31).

In the open label study LLSAT 51/91 participants (56 %) who experienced diarrhoea initiated antidiarrheal treatment, resulting in resolution in 30 participants. Resolution rates are only reported for those receiving anti-diarrhoeal therapy.

The PRO Gastrointestinal Symptom Rating Scale (GSRS) was used in GLISTEN and GLIMMER. In GLISTEN, the first increase (worsening) in the mean Diarrhoea Syndrome score was observed at Week 1 for the linerixibat group (+ 0.98) compared to a decrease (improvement) observed in the placebo group (- 0.19). For the linerixibat group, mean Diarrhoea Syndrome scores remained elevated (worsened) compared to baseline through Week 24 with a peak at Week 3 (mean score, 2.96; mean change from baseline + 1.34). At Week 24, a mean increase (worsening) from baseline was observed for the linerixibat group (+ 0.58) compared with a mean decrease (improvement) of - 0.19 for placebo.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In GLIMMER, worsening from baseline in the mean Diarrhoea Syndrome score was observed at the end of study (Week 16) across all active treatment groups (range: + 0.81 [20 mg QD] to + 1.32 [180 mg QD]) compared with no change in the placebo group (-0.02). the maximal mean score was observed at Week 8 (V4) for the intended dose group (linerixibat 40 mg BID) (mean score, 4.00; mean change from baseline, + 1.71).

In the supporting three phase 2 studies and seven phase 1 studies, the most common AEs were diarrhoea and abdominal pain. Most of them were mild or moderate in intensity.

## Liver related AEs

## Elevated Transaminases, ALP, and TB

In the pooled safety data set, shifts from normal baseline ALT and total bilirubin values to above the ULN at worst post-baseline were observed more frequently in the linerixibat group than in the placebo group (see following table).

In the treatment group 8/67 patients (11.9 %) with elevated ALT at baseline showed an increase in ALT of &gt;1.5 x baseline and 6/72 patients (8.3 %) with elevated AST at baseline showed an increase in AST of &gt;1.5 x baseline compared with 2/62 (3.2 %) and 0/62 in the placebo group.

Of the patients with ALP and TB &lt; 2 x ULN at baseline, 18 participants (12.6%) showed an increase in ALP to ≥ 2 ULN in the treatment group compared to 3.9% (6 participants) in the placebo group. 1.4% (2 participants) showed an increase in TB to ≥ 2 ULN compared to 0 in the placebo group. In patients with elevated ALP and/or TB levels ≥ 2 x ULN at baseline, there was no difference between the two groups in terms of the number of patients with further increase of these parameters (see Table 36).

An increase in both parameters ALP and TB to ≥ 2 ULN occurred only in patients with elevated ALP at baseline: 1 in the treatment group and 6 in the placebo group.

An overall increase in mean change from baseline for ALT, ALP, AST and Bilirubin, was observed in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group compared with the placebo group. There was a higher number of participants in the linerixibat 40 mg BID treatment group with increase in liver laboratory parameters to above defined thresholds based on multiple of ULN (where baseline values were within normal range) and multiple of baseline (where baseline were above ULN) (see Table 34) compared with the placebo group for ALT, AST, ALP and TB. This was more pronounced for participants with abnormal liver laboratory parameters at baseline particularly for ALT and AST.

In the open label LLSAT, 10 (5%) participants had post-baseline maximum ALT ≥3x ULN and TB ≥2x ULN and 11 (5%) participants had post-baseline maximum AST ≥3x ULN and TB ≥2x ULN (see eDISH plots above). All 10 participants with post-baseline maximum ALT ≥3x ULN and TB ≥2x ULN also had post-baseline maximum AST ≥3x ULN. Of the 11 participants with ALT and/or AST ≥3x ULN and TB ≥2x ULN, 9 participants (4 %) met the stopping or monitoring criteria. The remaining 2 participants had elevated liver laboratory parameters at baseline (see following table)

## Participants meeting liver monitoring or stopping criteria

In the pivotal study GLISTEN, elevated ALT that met liver monitoring or stopping criteria occurred in a higher proportion of participants in the linerixibat group compared with placebo (9% [11/119] vs 3% [3/118]). 6 of these 11 cases were adjudicated as related to study treatment versus 0 in the placebo group. All events were of mild to moderate intensity. No severe or serious events were reported. Discontinuations due to liver events occurred in 4/11 (36%) of the affected participants in the linerixibat group vs 0/3 in placebo. One event in the treatment group was not resolved by the end of the study. No clear temporal pattern was observed in time-to-event analysis of ALT elevations reported as a Liver Event.

In GLIMMER, one participant in the linerixibat 90 mg BID treatment group had an AESI of ALT elevation that met stopping criteria. The event was considered by the investigator to be related to study treatment; however, the participant's concomitant medication may have contributed to the abnormal ALT. Further information could not be found.

In LLSAT, 9/209 (4%) participants had liver laboratory elevations that met the liver stopping criteria. 5 of these cases resolved during follow-up. All cases which met stopping criteria were assessed by the GSK HSP for adjudication of causality. 2 of the 9 events were adjudicated possibly related to linerixibat.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the pooled safety dataset, AEs within the MedRA SMQs 'Liver related signs and symptoms' were more frequent in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group compared with placebo, primarily increases in ALT and AST. AEs within the MedDRA SMQ 'Drug-related hepatic disorders' were reported in 22 (15%) in the linerixibat group vs 10 (8%) on placebo. All AEs in the linerixibat group were mild or moderate; 2 severe events occurred in the placebo group. Treatment was permanently discontinued for 5/22 in the linerixibat group and 3/10 participants on placebo. One SAE of Drug related Hepatic Disorder was reported in the Linerixibat group.

Even though no cases of Drug induced liver injury were reported in the summary of safety for the pooled safety data set, patient narratives include events adjudicated by the HSP as possible DILI.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In LLSAT, in the linerixibat 90 mg BID group (N=55) no cases of drug-related hepatic disorders, severe events, hepatic failure, fibrosis, and cirrhosis, or other liver damage-related conditions were reported, unlike in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group (N=209), which had several reported cases across these categories. There is one case of DILI reported in the LLSAT study. One patient received fluconazole 150 mg daily for vaginal candidiasis from Study Day 78 to Study Day 85. On Study Day 88, a slight increase in ALT was noted. On Study Day 178, the protocol liver chemistry stopping criteria was met for increase in ALT (=5 x ULN). Treatment with linerixibat was stopped on Study Day 181. HSP Panel concluded that the event is possibly related to linerixibat based on the following: firstly, LFT changes cannot be separated temporally (in a convincing manner) from fluconazole start/stop and linerixibat start/stop and secondly, there were no other alternative causes at the time of the liver event other than a conclusion of possible DILI.

## Gallbladder polyp

In LLSAT, one participant experienced a serious adverse event of gallbladder polyp, which was considered by the investigator to be related to the study intervention. No additional details are provided.

## Cholecystitis and symptomatic cholelithiasis

In the pooled safety dataset, the number of participants who experienced events of cholecystitis and symptomatic cholelithiasis was similar between treatment groups. In the pooled safety dataset, the incidence of the AE Cholecystitis was 2 (1%) in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group and 0 in the placebo group. 1 of the 2 events was serious.

In LLSAT, 3 (1%) participants reported AEs within the MedDRA HLTs of Cholecystitis and Cholelithiasis, exposure adjusted incidence rate was 1.07 per 100 PY. All observed events of cholecystitis or cholelithiasis were of mild to moderate intensity. In all 3 cases, dose was not changed. One participant experienced a SAE that was not considered by the investigator to be related to treatment with linerixibat.

## Abdominal pain

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the pooled safety data set, abdominal pain was commonly reported as an AE in participants receiving linerixibat. In the pooled safety dataset, abdominal pain was observed in 23 % participants in the linerixibat treatment group and 9 % in the placebo group. Most events of abdominal pain were experienced within 5 days of initiating treatment. Most abdominal pain events were mild in intensity, and none were serious.

In LLSAT, 44 (20%) participants experienced AEs of abdominal pain (MedDRA HLT Gastrointestinal and Abdominal Pain [excl oral and throat]). The majority of events were mild or moderate with 3/44 (7%) participants reporting AEs considered severe. There were no serious AEs of abdominal pain.

## Gastrointestinal haemorrhage

FOBTs were included in the clinical development program for linerixibat, based on early nonclinical findings of minimal GI haemorrhage. In GLISTEN Part A, shift tables for Week 24 showed comparable rates of FOBT positivity between treatment (8%) and placebo (9%), with similar conversion rates from positive to negative and negative to positive across groups.

In LLSAT, a shift table is also provided. At Month 12, findings were consistent with GLISTEN, with no apparent treatment-related effect on FOBT results. The number of participants decreased markedly over time (82 at Month 12 to only 6 at Month 48).

3 non-serious AEs relating to GI haemorrhage were reported, two in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group (melaena and haematochezia), both considered related to study intervention by the investigator, and one in the placebo group (haematochezia), which occurred in a participant receiving concomitant mesalazine for colitis and was not considered related to study intervention.

## Absorption of fat-soluble vitamins

There is a theoretical risk that interfering with bile acid circulation might impair the absorption of fatsoluble vitamins (FSVs).

Plasma levels of the FSVs A, D, E, and K were measured during the GLISTEN, GLIMMER, and LLSAT studies. No differences were found in LS mean change from baseline in GLISTEN Part A in 25Hydroxyvitamin D, Alpha Tocopherol, Beta+Gamma Tocopherol, Vitamin A, and Vitamin E.

## Vitamin A

In GLISTEN Part A, post-baseline shift analyses for vitamin A showed that, at end of study, 14% of participants in the treatment group and 10% in the placebo group shifted from normal baseline values to below the reference range. When considering the worst post-baseline value, the proportions were comparable between groups (treatment: 18%; placebo: 17%).

## Vitamin D

In GLISTEN Part A, post-baseline shifts in vitamin D showed that none of the patients had vitamin D decrease from normal to below the reference range at the end of study.

In GLISTEN, no differences in the incidence of fractures were observed between treatment groups. A total of 2 participants in linerixibat group (2% [2/119]) and 3 participants in the placebo group reported AEs of fractures (3% [3/118]). Fractures were reported between day 25 and day 185, with no discernible pattern observed.

In LLSAT at day 120, 12/241 participants receiving linerixibat reported fractures (~39/1,000 PY) between Study Day 197 and 1284, almost all in participants with pre-existing bone disease. Events showed no temporal pattern suggestive of a treatment effect. The observed incidence aligns with published and real-world data in PBC and does not exceed expected background rates. Overall, no short- to mid-term fracture risk signal was identified, although long-term risk cannot be fully excluded.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In both studies, considerable proportion of participants had a vitamin D deficiency at baseline. More than one third of the participants in GLISTEN continued to receive supplementation in the course of the studies (doses not provided).

## Vitamin E

In GLISTEN Part A, mean and median vitamin E concentrations did not show any notable change from baseline in either treatment group. However, shift tables were not provided. It cannot be excluded that individual participants experienced reductions despite stable statistic values at group-level.

## Vitamin K

Post-baseline shift analyses for vitamin K showed that, based on the worst, 22% of participants in the linerixibat group compared with 8% in the placebo group shifted from normal baseline levels to below the reference range.

In GLISTEN Part A, a post-hoc ANCOVA analysis of vitamin K change from baseline in FSV levels at Week 24, adjusted for baseline values, showed a small LS mean difference of -0.08 [95% CI: -0.25, 0.09] (baseline 0.871 nmol/L, SD 0.78), which was not statistically significant.

At worst case post baseline, 26 (22 %) participants in the linerixibat group and 9 (8 %) in the placebo group had Vitamin K that decreased from normal at baseline to below normal reference range in GLISTEN. At end of study, the proportions were lower in both groups and the difference in percentage was smaller however still visible (linerixibat: 8%; placebo: 3%).

Accordingly, during the LLSAT study, at worst case post baseline, 23 (11%) participants had a Vitamin K measurement that decreased to below the normal reference range.

A total of 3 participants experienced treatment-related AEs of fat-soluble vitamin deficiency (Vitamin A decreased, Vitamin D decreased, Vitamin K deficiency).

## 5.4.4.2. Serious adverse events

SAEs were reported in a higher number of participants in the linerixibat 40 mg BID groups compared with placebo groups (10 % vs. 2 %). In the LLSAT study, SAEs were reported by 13% (10.50 per 100 PY) of the participants. There were no MedDRA PTs in which a SAE was reported in more than 1 participant, and no pattern of PTs was observed for the reported SAEs.

## 5.4.4.3. Deaths

During the clinical development program, 2 participants died.

1 participant died during the LLSAT, 523 days after the first dose of linerixibat study. Cause of death was reported as aspiration pneumonia, probably secondary to hepatic encephalopathy. Amongst others, the participant's medical history included hepatic cirrhosis, and she was on multiple medications. The diagnosis of hepatic encephalopathy is only found on the death certificate; an autopsy was not performed. The clinical examinations conducted during the study do not indicate any deterioration in hepatic function.

1 participant died during the LLSAT screening period after completion of her participation in the GLISTEN study during which she had received placebo (Part A) and linerixibat 40 mg BID (Part B). Underlying cause was reported as hypertension and atrial fibrillation. The investigator did not consider the event to be related to study participation or study intervention.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No fatal SAEs have been reported in either the GLISTEN or GLIMMER studies.

## 5.4.5. Discontinuation due to adverse events

In the pooled safety dataset, the incidence of any AE that led to permanent discontinuation of study treatment was higher in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group (13 %) compared with placebo group (5 %). These differences were mainly driven by diarrhoea, abdominal pain, AST increased, and ALT increased.

In LLSAT, 27 (13%) participants had at least 1 AE that led to treatment discontinuation. The most common AEs leading to discontinuation of treatment was diarrhoea. The exposure adjusted incidence rate of permanent discontinuation of study treatment due to diarrhoea was higher while receiving linerixibat 90 mg BID (11.89 per 100 PY) compared with linerixibat 40 mg BID (4.05 per 100 PY).

## 5.4.6. Safety in special populations

## Age

31 patients ≥ 65 years of age were treated in the pooled safety data set and 58 in the open-label study. Patients older than 75 years are only reported in the GLISTEN study with 3 patients in the treatment group. No specific safety concerns were identified in participants aged &gt;65 years.

No fatalities and no clustering of events were observed in the &gt; 65-year subgroup.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Hepatic impairment

A phase 1 PK-Study 214899 was conducted in patients with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B; any aetiology, no PBC; n = 8) and matched healthy controls (n = 8) receiving a single dose of 40 mg linerixibat (please refer to 2.2). Systemic exposure was markedly increased in moderate hepatic impairment, with Cmax, AUC₀-₂₄, and AUC₀-t increased 9.4-, 12.5-, and 13.1-fold, respectively, compared with matched healthy controls. The mean fraction excreted unchanged in urine (fe%) increased approximately tenfold (0.15% vs 0.014% in controls), reflecting higher systemic exposure. In this study, no AEs were reported in participants with moderate hepatic impairment following single dose administration of linerixibat 40 mg.

Cirrhosis was reported at baseline in 23/142 (16 %) patients in the treatment groups, all classified as Child Pugh A, and 14/128 (11 %) in the placebo groups. Patients with hepatic impairment Child Pugh C were excluded from the studies.

The number of AEs was higher in patients treated with linerixibat and SAEs only occurred in the treatment group (see following table).
