As in the overall population on linerixibat, in the population with cirrhosis, GI adverse events (78% [18/23]) were the most frequently reported AEs in the linerixibat group; with Diarrhoea being reported in 61% (14/23) participants.

The safety profile, particularly systemic adverse events, at supratherapeutic doses (2.25-fold higher than the recommended therapeutic dose) was evaluated in the dose finding study GLIMMER. In this study 180 mg QD and 90 mg BID were administered for 12 weeks which resulted in clearly higher mean Cmax values compared with those observed in the above-mentioned PK study in patients with hepatic impairment.

## Renal impairment

Patients with estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;45 mL/min/1.73 m2 were excluded from the study. Therefore, no safety data is available for patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal disease.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.7. Immunological events

Hypersensitivity reactions are not reported in the summary of safety.

In the CSR of GLISTEN and GLIMMER, there are no cases of \"drug hypersensitivity\" in the SOT Immune system disorders reported in the treatment groups vs. 1 case each in the placebo groups.

Drug hypersensitivity is reported in 6/245 (3 %) participants in LLSAT.

## 5.4.8. Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions (3 %) of patients in the linerixibat group

## OCA

Two interaction studies with obeticholic acid (OCA), a synthetic bile acid, showed that concomitant administration of linerixibat with OCA can decrease plasma concentrations of OCA conjugates (taurine and glycine). This interaction could potentially decrease the efficacy of OCA as the conjugates are equipotent with OCA. Since, however, the compound OCA has been taken from the market in 2024, the relevance of the studies is considered to be rather low, though it could serve as evidence that linerixibat reduces the absorption of synthetic bile acids.

## Bile acid binding resins

In the clinical studies, 4.2% of the patients were taking cholestyramine, and 5.2% bile acid binding resins (BABRs). The Applicant explored the binding of linerixibat to BABRs, such as cholestyramine, colestipol, or colesevelam, and a high binding rate was detected. These resins have the potential to inhibit the effect of linerixibat on its target IBAT. There are no specific safety data available for patients who took BABRs in addition to linerixibat (at an interval of 4 hours). Nevertheless, in GLISTEN, administration of bile acid binding resins was required to be at least 4 hrs before or after linerixibat and was shown not to impact efficacy.

## UDCA

In the pivotal study, 96% of patients in the linerixibat group and 98% in the placebo group received concomitant UDCA. Participants who were taking UDCA had to be on stable doses of UDCA for &gt;8 weeks at time of screening, with no changes or discontinuation permitted until completion of the Main Study Period.

As part of the Phase 2a study BAT117213, linerixibat 90 mg BID was co-dosed with UDCA for 2 weeks. Linerixibat had no significant effect on plasma concentrations of UDCA, it did however reduce exposure to the main conjugates glyco-UDCA and tauro-UDCA (Cmax and AUC).

## 5.4.9. Vital signs and laboratory findings

No clinically relevant findings in vital signs, physical findings, ECG or other observations related to safety were reported for the key safety studies.

## 5.4.10. Post-marketing experience

Not applicable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.11. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

Not applicable.

## 5.4.12. Exploratory and background data

## Study 224293

This genetic study was conducted to investigate the association between loss-of-function (LOF) and reduction-of-function (ROF) variants in the ileal bile acid transporter (SLC10A2) with cholelithiasis and colon cancer. LOF and ROF genetic variants in Solute Carrier Family 10 Member 2 (SLC10A2) have been experimentally validated to affect IBAT activity. These functionally relevant variants in SLC10A2 were evaluated to assess their potential associations with cholelithiasis and colon cancer to inform potential effects of IBAT inhibition.

The results suggest that variants in the SLC10A2 gene leading to either ROF or LOF in IBAT are associated with an increased risk of gallbladder disease but not of colon cancer.

Additional supportive data are presented from 3 US claims-based studies:

- Study 220783: Background Rate of Gallstones in a Prevalent PBC Population: a US Claims Database Analysis
- Study 218576: Longitudinal Description of Liver Chemistry among PBC Patients in the US
- Study 222317: Background Rate of Liver Events in a Prevalent PBC population: a US Claims Database Analysis

## Study 220783:

An US claims-based study was conducted to estimate the background rate of cholelithiasis and cholecystitis among patients with PBC treated in real-world settings using the 2016-2019 MarketScan Commercial and Medicare Supplemental database.

According to Study report 22.95% of the PBC population had cirrhosis reported as comorbidity, and 1.78 % of the PBC patients were also diagnosed with Primary sclerosing cholangitis. 76.89 % received Ursodoxycholic acid as concomitant therapy.

In this cohort of 3,713 patients with PBC in the US, median age was 59 years (IQR: 52-64) and 88.02% were female. The overall background rate (number of patients observed to experience an event of interest during follow-up) of cholelithiasis and/or cholecystitis ranged from 18.96 events per 1,000 PY (when restricted to those with no evidence of cholelithiasis/cholecystitis in the prior year, i.e., \"incident\" gallstone disease) to 37.91 per 1,000 PY (overall unrestricted analysis, i.e., \"prevalent\" gallstone disease). The percentage of patients with PBC experiencing incident cholelithiasis and/or cholecystitis within 12 months of follow-up ranged from 1.94% to 3.80% among those with prevalent gallstone disease.

## Study 218576

An US EMR-based study was conducted to understand how liver biochemistry changes over time among patients with PBC treated in real-world settings using the 2016 - 2020 Optum EMR database. This data source was chosen due to the availability of laboratory. An overall cohort of 3,510 patients with PBC in the US was identified (median age: 64 years [IQR: 55-72], 82.7% female, 86.0% White) of which a subset of 1,376 patients met similar inclusion/exclusion criteria to PBC patients from the GLISTEN study (median age: 64 [IQR: 55-72], 90.8% female, 86.5% White).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

According to table 8 of the study report, 100 % of patients had cirrhosis at baseline, of the patients with ALP ≥1.67xULN, 7.5 % had also Primary sclerosing cholangitis as comorbidity. 8.5 % had also alcoholic liver disease at baseline and 9.9 % autoimmune hepatitis, 2.3 % had liver transplant and 2.6 % Hepatocellular carcinoma

At baseline in overall PBC cohort, 38.6% of patients had ALT &gt; ULN, 47.3% had AST &gt; ULN, 69.0% had ALP &gt; ULN, and 33.1% had ALP ≥ 1.67x ULN. In the trial-like cohort, 38.1% of patients had ALT &gt; ULN, 40.0% had AST &gt; ULN, 66.4% had ALP &gt; ULN, and 29.7% had ALP ≥ 1.67x ULN. In the overall cohort at 6 months of follow-up, 5.0% met ALT increased monitoring criteria and/or stopping criteria and 6.3% met AST increased monitoring criteria and/or stopping criteria. In the trial-like cohort at 6 months of follow-up, while there was a general trend of decreasing lab values, 1.9% met ALT increased monitoring criteria and/or stopping criteria and 1.5% met AST increased monitoring criteria and/or stopping criteria, a proportion of patients in both cohorts experienced liver enzyme elevations that met the GLISTEN per protocol increased monitoring and stopping criteria.

## Study 222317

An US claims-based study was conducted to estimate the background rate of liver events and related complications among patients with PBC treated in real-world settings using the 2016-2023 MarketScan Commercial and Medicare Supplemental database. An overall cohort of 7,160 patients with PBC in the US was identified (median age: 58 years [IQR: 51-64], 86.7% female) of which a subset of 2,796 patients met similar inclusion/exclusions criteria to PBC patients from the GLISTEN study (median age: 58 years [IQR: 51-64], 86.7% female). This data was previously used for Study 220783. It built on Study 220783 by expanding both the study period and the outcomes of interest.

According to Table 4 of the study report, 100 % of patients had cirrhosis at baseline, 2.3 % had also alcoholic liver disease at baseline and 10.8 % autoimmune hepatitis, 3.41 % were also diagnosed with PSC, 1.2 % had liver transplant and 1.1 % Hepatocellular carcinoma. 70.87 % reported UDCA as concomitant therapy.

Of the 13 liver events and related complications assessed which were defined based on relevant ICD10 codes, the most common were cirrhosis, ascites, oesophageal varices, portal hypertension, and fracture. The incidence rate over the follow-up period for cirrhosis, ascites, oesophageal varices, portal hypertension, and fracture ranged from 41.22 events per 1,000 PY (ascites) to 200.48 per 1,000 PY (cirrhosis) in the overall cohort and 10.36 (ascites) - 116.26 event per 1,000 PY (cirrhosis) in the triallike cohort. The percentage of patients with PBC experiencing cirrhosis, ascites, oesophageal varices, portal hypertension and fracture within 12 months of follow-up was 25.82%, 6.45%, 7.96%, 6.86%, and 5.80% in the overall cohort and 15.05%, 1.23%, 2.94%, 2.17%, and 4.08% in the trial-like cohort.

## 5.4.13. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.13.1. Discussion

Safety assessment is based on 282 PBC patients treated with linerixibat, of whom 194 received the proposed dose of 40 mg BID. 103 patients were treated for 5.5 months and 45 patients for 7.4 months in double-blind, placebo-controlled trials. 107 patients were treated for ≥ 12 months in the extension open label study. All these 107 patients had previously participated in one of the double-blind studies. Clinical safety data for linerixibat comply with the EMA's requirements for long-term exposure, i.e. more than 100 patients treated for ≥ 1 year.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the demographic and baseline characteristics of the study population are well-documented, and the consistency in age distribution and disease characteristics across the studies supports the pooling of data for safety analyses.

In the pooled safety data set, disease characteristics were balanced between treatment groups, with the exception of a slight imbalance in reported cirrhosis at baseline of 16 % (23 participants) in the treatment group vs. 11 % (14 participants) on placebo). It should be noted that the seriousness of the experienced itch is not related to the progression/severeness of the underlying PBC.

In the pooled safety set, 127 patients (89%) in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group and 94 (73%) patients in the placebo group experienced at least 1 AE. Most TEAEs were Grade 1 or 2 in severity in all treatment groups. The most frequently reported AEs in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group were diarrhoea and abdominal pain.

In the long-term extension study LLSAT, the most commonly reported AEs were GI disorders (diarrhoea, abdominal pain upper, abdominal pain) and COVID-19. 23 participants (11%) experienced severe events.

## SAEs

SAEs were reported in a higher number of participants in the linerixibat 40 mg BID groups compared with placebo groups (10 % vs. 2 %). In the LLSAT study, SAEs were reported in 13% (10.50 per 100 PY) of the participants. There were no MedDRA PTs in which a SAE was reported in more than 1 participant, and no pattern of PTs was observed for the reported SAEs. In the limited number of patients with cirrhosis, SAEs were reported only in the treatment group. However, these events appear largely confounded by the patients' advanced underlying disease and comorbidity burden.

## Death

Two cases of death were reported in the open label LLSAT study.

One of them, a female participant, died 523 days after the first dose of linerixibat. Liver cirrhosis and extensive concomitant medication was documented at baseline. Cause of death was reported as aspiration pneumonia, probably secondary to hepatic encephalopathy. An autopsy was not performed. The clinical examinations conducted during the study do not indicate any deterioration in hepatic function. The second patient died due to coronary arteriosclerosis. Both cases are not considered related to study intervention.

## Diarrhoea

Diarrhoea was the most common adverse event associated with linerixibat treatment, typically starting within the first week of therapy. In the pooled safety data set, diarrhoea was reported in 59 % of the treatment group versus 18 % in the placebo group. 2 % of the cases in the linerixibat group and none in the placebo group were assessed as severe. In the placebo-controlled studies, 4 % of the patients treated with linerixibat 40 mg BID discontinued study treatment due to diarrhoea vs &lt;1 % on placebo, in the long-term LLSAT, the rate was very similar in the 40 mg BID group: 5% discontinued study treatment due to diarrhoea.

In the pooled safety data set as well as in LLSAT, the time to event analysis indicated that the first reports of diarrhoea in participants receiving linerixibat occurred within the first week of treatment. Patient-reported outcomes were assessed using the Gastrointestinal Symptom Rating Scale (GSRS) in the phase 3 study GLISTEN and the phase 2 study GLIMMER. Although, the GSRS does not capture the duration of individual diarrhoea episodes, the data indicate persistent symptomatology in a subset of patients as reflected by elevated mean Diarrhoea Syndrome scores at the end of both studies. In the phase 3 study GLISTEN, antidiarrheal treatment (loperamide) was recommended for participants if diarrhoea impacted their daily life. In the linerixibat group, 31 of 70 patients (44% of patients with the AE diarrhoea) started antidiarrheal treatment compared with 5/22 (23%) in the placebo group. The episode resolved in 21 out of these 31 (68%) participants in the treatment group and 4/5 (80%) participants in the placebo group. In LLSAT 51/91 participants who experienced diarrhoea initiated antidiarrheal treatment, which led to resolution of diarrhoea in 30 participants. Diarrhoea resolution rates are only reported for those receiving anti-diarrhoeal therapy. The median duration of all instances of AE of diarrhoea was 10 days with a range of 1 to 230 days in the pooled linerixibat 40 mg BID group. In GLISTEN, 7 % of the affected patients had ≥ 3 recurrences.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Taking both AESI and PRO data into account, some diarrhoea events persisted until the end of treatment, probably chronically recurrent in some cases. This possible persistence of symptoms is clinically relevant for patient management. Interpretation of the course of diarrhoea is additionally limited by the high rate of concomitant antidiarrheal treatment which did not result in resolution in all cases.

The SmPC adequately reflects that diarrhoea is a very common adverse reaction when taking linerixibat and may require symptomatic management as well as considerations towards interrupting or discontinuing linerixibat treatment if diarrhoea persists.

Despite the frequency of diarrhoea and the need for symptomatic treatment in some patients, severe complications typically associated with fluid loss or electrolyte disturbances were not observed within the controlled study setting, even in elderly participants. Treating physicians are alerted in the SmPC to advise patients to monitor signs and symptoms of dehydration, which is acceptable.

## Liver related AEs including elevated liver function tests

Higher increases from baseline were observed in the linerixibat groups compared with placebo for ALT, ALP, AST and bilirubin. Considerable variability in the course of liver function tests was noted within the treatment group during both studies. In participants that met liver stopping or monitoring criteria, across all examined liver chemistry parameters, the elevations appeared to be transient or rather stable.

There was a higher number of participants in the linerixibat 40 mg BID treatment group with increase in liver laboratory parameters to above defined thresholds based on multiple of ULN (where baseline values were within normal range) and multiple of baseline (where baseline were above ULN) compared with the placebo group for ALT, AST, ALP and TB. This was more pronounced for participants with abnormal liver laboratory parameters at baseline particularly for ALT and AST. Elevated ALT that met liver monitoring or stopping criteria occurred in a higher proportion of participants in the linerixibat groups when compared with the placebo groups. In the pivotal GLISTEN study, 6 (out of 11) of these events in the treatment group were adjudicated as related to study drug vs 1 (out of 3) in the placebo group. Discontinuations due to liver events occurred in 4/11 (36%) participants in the linerixibat group vs 0/3 in placebo. In the extension study LLSAT 2 (out of 9) events were adjudicated possibly related to linerixibat. Therefore, precautionary statements are included in the SmPC recommending baseline transaminase levels before initiating linerixibat and further monitoring aligned with standard clinical practice for PBC patients. If transaminases are elevated more frequent monitoring is suggested and the treating physician may weigh the potential risks against the benefit of continuing treatment in case of persisting transaminase elevations (SmPC 4.4.). It remains unclear whether, particularly in patients with elevated baseline values (i.e. more advanced PBC), the investigational product may potentially contribute to a progressive worsening of liver function therefore hepatotoxicity was included as a potential risk in the RMP.

AEs within the MedDRA SMQ \"Drug-related hepatic disorders\" were reported in 22 (15%) in the linerixibat group and 10 (8%) participants in the placebo group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase 3 study GLISTEN Part A, liver events meeting the predefined liver stopping criteria occurred in 7 participants (6%) in the treatment group and in 2 participants (2%) in the control group. All cases were adjudicated by the Hepatic Safety Panel (HSP). In the treatment group, 3 cases were adjudicated as possibly related to treatment, compared with 1 case in the placebo group. Under continued exposure in the open-label study LLSAT, liver events meeting the predefined liver stopping criteria occurred in additional 9 participants (4.2 %); 2 cases were adjudicated as possibly related to treatment. Hepatoxicity has therefore be considered a safety concern to be monitored in the RMP. Gallbladder disorders In the pooled safety dataset, the number of participants who experienced events of cholecystitis and symptomatic cholelithiasis was similar between treatment groups. The AE cholecystitis was reported in 2 patients of the treatment group and 0 in the placebo group. Genetic studies suggest that variants in the SLC10A2 gene leading to either a reduction or loss of function in IBAT are associated with an increased risk of gallbladder disease. Reference is made to the submitted Study 224293, a \"Genetic study to investigate the association between loss-of function and reduction-of-function mutations in the ileal bile acid transporter with cholelithiasis and colon cancer\". Even though the clinical trial program is unlikely to have sufficient power to quantify the risk of low frequency events such as cholecystitis and symptomatic cholelithiasis, cholecystitis and symptomatic cholelithiasis do not appear to represent a clinically significant risk with linerixibat treatment. In the placebo-controlled GLISTEN study, one participant experienced the SAE Gallbladder polyp which was considered related to the study intervention by the investigator. In the absence of supportive preclinical findings or a pattern across the development programme, the isolated event of gallbladder polyp does not constitute a safety concern, and routine pharmacovigilance is considered sufficient. Gastrointestinal Haemorrhage Early non-clinical studies found some evidence of minimal GI haemorrhage. They were not replicated in subsequent non-clinical studies (rats and dogs). In the clinical trials, no treatment-related effect on Faecal Occult Blood Test (FOBT) results was observed. Three non-serious AEs relating to GI haemorrhage were reported, two in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group and one in the placebo group. The events in the treatment group consisted of melaena and haematochezia; both were assessed by the investigator as related to the study intervention. The single case in the placebo group is not considered related to treatment (patient suffered from IBD). Overall, the events were few, exposure-adjusted incidence rates were low and involved different preferred terms. They showed no consistent pattern across treatment groups, and Vitamin K levels and INR showed no abnormalities. The available data do not indicate a treatment-related increase in the risk of gastrointestinal bleeding. Absorption of fat-soluble Vitamins There is a risk that interference with bile acid circulation might impair the absorption of fat-soluble Vitamins (FSVs). Plasma levels of the FSVs A, D, E, and K were measured during the GLISTEN, GLIMMER, and LLSAT studies. No differences in incidence of FSV related AEs have been observed between linerixibat and placebo. No differences were found in LS mean change from baseline in GLISTEN Part A in 25-Hydroxyvitamin D, Alpha Tocopherol, Beta+Gamma Tocopherol, Vitamin A, and Vitamin E. In LLSAT, at worst post-baseline assessment, 37 participants (18%) had vitamin A levels below the normal reference range. Shift tables were also provided: The absence of post-baseline normal-to-low shifts in Vitamin D is reassuring but must be interpreted cautiously. Given the high rate of baseline deficiency with continued supplementation (~37 %), the data do not fully exclude an effect on vitamin D absorption under non-supplemented conditions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Shift tables for vitamin E were not provided. It cannot be excluded that individual participants experienced reductions despite stable statistic values at group-level.

At worst case post baseline, 26 (22 %) participants in the linerixibat group and 9 (8 %) in the placebo group had Vitamin K that decreased from normal at baseline to below normal reference range in GLISTEN. At end of study, the proportions were lower in both groups and the difference in percentage was smaller however still visible (linerixibat: 8%; placebo: 3%). During the LLSAT study, at worst case post baseline, 23 (11%) participants had a Vitamin K measurement that decreased to below the normal reference range.

A warning is included in section 4.4 of the SmPC that the uptake of fat-soluble vitamins might be decreased accompanied by a recommendation to monitor these vitamins.

## Special population

All subgroup analyses were without significant findings, with a consistent trend across all subgroups indicating a higher incidence of adverse events in the treatment group compared to the placebo group.

The age distribution across the studies shows that the proportion of patients older than 65 years is relatively consistent, ranging from 22% in GLIMMER to 27% in LLSAT, with 25% in GLISTEN. Limited exposure of patients over 65 years is noted. No specific safety concerns were identified in participants aged &gt;65 years. However, an age-related imbalance was observed, with a markedly higher percentage of SAEs in the treatment group compared with placebo in the older age groups. The available data are limited, particularly in the very elderly, as only 3 participants aged &gt;75 years were included in the placebo controlled studies. Consequently, the safety profile in patients aged &gt;65 years cannot be adequately characterised and is considered missing information.

In the pooled safety dataset, disease characteristics were generally balanced between treatment groups. For 23/142 (16 %) patients in the treatment groups and 14/128 (11 %) in the placebo groups, cirrhosis was reported at baseline. In LLSAT, 17 patients were reported with hepatic cirrhosis without specification of stage, and one patient with hepatic failure. No patients with hepatic impairment ChildPugh B were included in the pooled safety population or in LLSAT. Patients with hepatic decompensation (Child-Pugh C) were excluded from all studies. The number of AEs was higher in patients with cirrhosis treated with linerixibat. Similar to the overall population treated with linerixibat, gastrointestinal adverse events were the most frequently reported AEs in this subgroup (78%; 18/23), with diarrhoea reported in 61% (14/23) of participants.

Following single dose administration of 40 mg linerixibat in PK Study 214899, AUC(0-t) and Cmax values were approximately 13-fold and 9.4-fold higher in participants with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B; any aetiology, no PBC) than in matched healthy controls but was clearly below the mean Cmax observed in the Phase 2b study GLIMMER at supratherapeutic doses (for 12 weeks). In patients with a history of liver decompensation or at risk thereof, the available data are insufficient to allow a meaningful safety assessment due to the limited number of such cases in the clinical studies. A warning is included in sections 4.2 and 4.4 of the SmPC that use of linerixibat is not recommended in these patients.

No safety data is available for patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal disease. Renal excretion of linerixibat is negligible, and no dose adjustment is required for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment.

## Background data

Additional data are presented from 3 US claims-based studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The three studies provide context on background rates of gallbladder and liver events in PBC populations. However, direct comparison with the reported clinical trial outcomes is limited due to notable differences in baseline characteristics across studies, e.g. different baseline demographics and disease severity, particularly a high prevalence of patients with cirrhosis and concomitant hepatic injuries different from PBC. For example, in Study 218576, 100 % of patients had cirrhosis at baseline, of the patients with elevated ALP ≥1.67xULN, 7.5 % had also Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis as comorbidity. 8.5 % had also alcoholic liver disease at baseline and 9.9 % autoimmune hepatitis, 2.3 % had liver transplant and 2.6 % Hepatocellular carcinoma. The rate of concomitant UDCA therapy was lower across the studies. With these studies, a qualitative understanding of background rates is supported, but they cannot reliably serve as a quantitative comparator.

## 5.4.13.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

The safety data base is considered limited for robust safety assessment, but acceptable considering that PBC is an orphan condition.

Gastrointestinal adverse events, primarily diarrhoea and abdominal pain, and elevations in transaminases were the most common ADRs with a relevant difference compared with placebo. The higher discontinuation rate in the 40 mg BID group due to diarrhoea, which was mostly mild and moderate in intensity, reflects the clinical impact of persistent gastrointestinal symptoms rather than life-threatening events. Frequent use of anti-diarrhoeal medication supports the need for symptom management in clinical practice and this is appropriately advised in the SmPC.

Linerixibat seems to be associated with a higher risk of liver events. Increase of transaminases occur at a clearly higher frequency in the treatment arm compared with control. 3 cases of hepatoxicity possibly related to study intervention were reported in patients treated with linerixibat in the pooled safety population vs 1 in the placebo group. 2 more were reported under continued exposure in the open label study LLSAT. These were mostly stable or transient. The hepatic toxicity is monitored as potential risk in the Risk Management Plan. Adequate warning statement on the monitoring of transaminases are included in the SmPC.

Patients with hepatic impairment Child Pugh C were excluded from all studies. The safety of linerixibat in PBC patients with advanced PBC and moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B) has not been studied, or data are limited. PK and safety of a single 40 mg oral dose were evaluated in otherwise healthy participants with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B) and matched healthy controls with normal liver function. Systemic exposure was increased in hepatic impairment, but peak concentrations in this study were lower than those observed in the Phase 2b study GLIMMER at supratherapeutic doses administered for 12 weeks. These higher exposures were not associated with a relevant increase in systemic adverse events.

However, in patients with a history of liver decompensation or at risk thereof, the available data are insufficient, and a warning is included in the SmPC regarding these patients that the use of linerixibat is not recommended. If used, close monitoring is advised.

Non-clinical studies indicated low concern for gastrointestinal haemorrhage. Data from the clinical program are sparse, but the available data do not indicate a treatment-related increase in the risk of gastrointestinal haemorrhage.

Based on the provided data, an effect of linerixibat on FSV absorption cannot be excluded in individual cases therefore the SmPC recommends assessment of fat-soluble vitamin levels and INR for all patents prior initiating linerixibat and further monitoring per standard clinical practise.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Limited exposure of patients over 65 years has been noted. While AE and SAE rates were higher in participants &gt;65 years than in those &lt;65 years, events were diverse and did not indicate an organspecific or mechanistically plausible safety signal. Safety in elderly and very elderly patients is followed up as missing information in the RMP.

## 5.4.13.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3.1 above. They are acceptable.

No additional ADRs are proposed for inclusion.

## 5.4.13.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Based on the submitted data Linerixibat is generally well tolerated in patients with PBC and displays an acceptable safety profile. The potential risk on hepatotoxicity is adequately managed with implemented SmPC precautionary statements and will be followed up upon in the RMP. Due to sparse data in elderly patients including consequences of diarrhoea the safety in the elderly is followed up as missing information in the RMP. Diarrhoea as the most common side effect is considered to be manageable in clinical practise with monitoring advice as implemented in the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

The applicant proposed the following summary of safety concerns in the RMP:

Table 44: Summary of safety concerns in the proposed RMP

| Summary of safety concerns   | Summary of safety concerns                                                   |
|------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Important identified risks   | None                                                                         |
| Important potential risks    | Hepatotoxicity                                                               |
| Missing information          | Safety (e.g., consequences of diarrhea) in elderly and very elderly patients |

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

The safety specification is acceptable.

Diarrhoea, led to antidiarrheal treatment in almost half of the patients treated with linerixibat (31/ 70) in the GLISTER study. In the LLSAT study the majority of patients who experienced diarrhoea (51/91) needed antidiarrheal treatment. In both studies, antidiarrheal treatment did not resolve the event in all patients but according to the provided data only in 21 of 31 patients in the GLISTER study and 30 of 51 patients in the LLSAT study. Although mostly of mild or moderate severity, 4 % and 15% of the patients treated with linerixibat discontinued study treatment in the RCTs and in the long-term open extension study, respectively. Diarrhoea can have an impact on the benefit/risk ratio particularly in elderly subjects as it may lead to serious consequences of dehydration like mental disorientation or falls. This might even be pronounced in patients being concomitantly treated with diuretics for hypertension (hypertension was among the most frequent concomitant diseases across studies) and if antidiarrheal medication is not successful. Results from the GLISTER study show, that particularly diarrhoea with a duration of &gt; 14 days is pronounced in the linerixibat group compared to placebo and percentage of affected patients increase by age. Study results also point to a higher percentage of patients with diarrhoea grade 3 compared to placebo treatment with percentages higher in the elderly compared to the younger age groups. However, number of exposed patients in this study is low, particularly in the elderly (n=25 in patients &gt; 65 years of age) and very low in the very elderly (n=3 in patients &gt; 75 years of age). Anticipating a different safety profile in elderly and very elderly patients, \"safety (e.g. consequences of diarrhoea) in elderly and very elderly patients\" has therefore been included as missing information.

The applicant justified transaminases elevation not being important by comparing incidence rates of liver events from US health claims-based studies with the occurrence in the clinical trial programme. However, real world data as an external comparator is considered not as reliable as head-to-head comparison in a clinical trial and incidence rates not as specific as single case evaluation. The range of incidence rates in the RWD study (10.36 to 116.26 events per 1,000 patient-years in the trial-like cohort) is considered a sign of such low reliability. The applicant stated that none of the events that met the protocol-defined liver stopping criteria were adjudicated by the hepatic safety panel as probably or definitely related, however, there were three cases of linerixibat treated patients where a DILI event was considered possibly related by the panel in the GLISTEN trial as well as two cases in the LLSAT trial. A possible causal relation is enough to consider the event an adverse drug reaction, and ALT/AST elevations are labelled in 4.8 the SmPC. The applicant´s proposal to include a warning in the product information recommending monitoring of liver parameters and the advice to reconsider continuing treatment in case the patient develops transaminase elevations is also accepted. Hepatotoxicity/DILI in relation to pruritus treatment is considered to have an impact on the benefit/risk ratio. \"Hepatotoxicity\" is therefore included in the list of safety concerns as important potential risk as further evaluation is required post-authorisation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Therefore, the summary of product characteristics and the package leaflet include a statement that this medicinal product is subject to additional monitoring and will allow quick identification of new safety information. The statement is preceded by an inverted equilateral black triangle.

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, therapeutic indication

Primary biliary cholangitis (PBC) is a rare chronic liver disease, that predominantly affects women. It is characterised by destruction of small intrahepatic bile ducts, resulting in cholestasis, inflammation, and biliary fibrosis. If left untreated, PBC can progress to cirrhosis, liver failure, and premature death.

Every year, at least 100 000 individuals worldwide receive a diagnosis of PBC, with studies suggesting that at least one in 1000 women over the age of 40 years have PBC.

The course of PBC can vary significantly among patients and healthcare providers must evaluate a variety of factors to accurately identify and treat patients with both higher risk of disease progression and worse symptom burden. Pruritus occurs in 20%-70% of patients and negatively impacts quality of life because it is associated with sleep deprivation, depression, social isolation, and worse fatigue. It may occur in the very early stages and may be present even after liver biochemistries have normalized with UDCA treatment. Refractory pruritus might lead to liver transplantation, independent of the stage of disease.

The causal pathway of pruritus in PBC has not been fully elucidated. Potential mediators of the pathophysiology include bile acids, LPA, endogenous opioids and IL-31.

Bile acids have been implicated by multiple lines of evidence and is clear that high levels of TSBA lead to itch (as in PFIC and Alagille syndrome).

LPA is a lipid-signalling molecule that is elevated in many cholestatic patients. LPA injected into mice causes itch in a dose-dependent manner. Consistent with these data, activity of autotaxin, the enzyme that produces LPA, is related to itch in PBC patients with pruritus.

IL-31, a known mediator of pruritus in atopic dermatitis, has recently been shown in PBC patients to be related to pruritus.

Although TSBA concentration as well as LPA (autotaxin) and IL-31 are qualitatively related to itch the quantitative relation, especially in PBC patients, remains to be elucidated.

The indication applied for Linerixibat is for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC).

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

While several second-line therapies targeting disease progression have emerged following positive phase III trials, no treatment has yet been approved specifically for cholestatic pruritus in PBC.

Available treatments for pruritus include cholestyramine, rifaximin, and PPAR agonists, however they are limited by side effects and poor tolerability.

The first-line treatment for pruritus is cholestyramine (4-16 g/day), a non-absorbable anion-exchange resin taken 20 minutes before meals and at least 2-4 hours apart from other medications to avoid absorption interference. However, it has poor palatability and taste limit adherence. Rifampicin (150300 mg twice daily) is an effective second-line therapy but carries a risk of hepatotoxicity and renal dysfunction.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor (PPAR) agonists have shown potential benefit for pruritus in observational studies and as secondary endpoints in recent randomized controlled trials. However, since cholestatic pruritus is not histamine-mediated, antihistamines are not recommended, as they offer no therapeutic benefit and may worsen fatigue and dry mouth due to their sedative and anticholinergic effects.

Recently, the first ileal bile acid transporter (IBAT) inhibitors maralixabat (Livmarli) and odevixibat (Bylvay and Klivfynd) emerged as a new treatment option for cholestatic pruritus and were approved for treatment of cholestatic pruritus in different indications (patients with Alagille syndrome and in treatment of progressive familial intrahepatic cholestasis).

Also, worth mentioning are elafibranor and seladelpar 2 products that were registered for the treatment of PBC.

Symptom management remains critical, yet often an insufficient component of care in part of the PBC population suffering from severe/intractable pruritus.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

For a detailed description of the main clinical studies supporting this application, please refer to section 5.1.2 of this document.

The main evidence of efficacy of linerixibat 40 mg twice daily (BID) in PBC-related pruritus is derived from a single Phase 3 pivotal study GLISTEN. Further, supportive evidence is derived from GLIMMER and LLSAT studies.

GLISTEN was a pivotal Phase 3, global, multi-centre, 2-part (Part A and Part B), randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled study in PBC participants with moderate to severe pruritus (WI-NRS ≥ 4) randomized to receive linerixibat 40 mg BID or placebo. The primary analysis was of Part A, comparing linerixibat with placebo with stable background pruritus and PBC therapy (if applicable) over 24 weeks. Participants were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive linerixibat 40 mg BID or placebo. Randomization was stratified.

Adults with PBC-related pruritus of ≥4 points on WI-NRS with adequate compliance during the screening phase, and without elevated liver function parameters, liver related conditions (Hepatitis B/C, primary sclerosing cholangitis, alcoholic liver disease and/or confirmed hepatocellular carcinoma or biliary-tract cancer, symptomatic cholelithiasis or cholecystitis), or decompensation, other types of pruritus (e.g., contact dermatitis) were selected. Standard concomitant treatments of PBC and pruritus were allowed on a stable regimen.

In total, 238 participants were randomized and 211 (89%) completed Part A (108 [91%] in the placebo group and 103 [87%] in the linerixibat group). Twenty-seven (11%) participants withdrew from the study (11 [9%] participants in the placebo group and 16 [13%] participants in the linerixibat group) during Part A. The most common reason for study withdrawal was withdrawal by the subject (7 [6%] participants in the placebo group and 9 [8%] participants in the linerixibat group).

The study population was predominantly female, white and non-Hispanic or Latino. The majority of the population was aged 30-64 years (74%). Twenty-five per cent of the patients were 65 years or older and 4% were 75 years and older. Treatment arms were well balanced. The majority of participants had controlled PBC as assessed using the ALP biomarker and small portion (13%) of the population had elevated total bilirubin levels at baseline. The majority (about 60%) of patients had severe worst-itch levels (as evaluated with WI-NRS).

The use of previous treatment for PBC was higher in the placebo group than in the linerixibat group

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(18% vs. 9%) and cholestatic pruritus treatment due to PBC was similar (32% in placebo and 28% in the linerixibat group). Roughly half of the participants (55% in the linerixibat and 49% in the placebo arm - Part A) did not receive any concomitant anti-pruritic treatment.

## 9.3. Favourable effects

Primary objective: Statistically significant improvement in the Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks was observed in the linerixibat group when compared with the placebo group (least-squares [LS] mean change from baseline, -2.86 vs. -2.15; LS mean difference vs. placebo, -0.72; 95% confidence interval [CI]: -1.15 to -0.28; p=0.001).

In the population that excluded participants enrolled at metasite(s) (i.e. patients who had no physical contact with the investigator), there was a statistically significant difference between linerixibat and placebo groups in the Monthly Itch Score over 24 weeks (LS mean difference vs. placebo in change from baseline: -0.68 [95% CI: -1.14, -0.23; nominal p=0.003])

Statistical significance for the key secondary objectives was tested in the step-down/hierarchical approach.

Statistically significant improvement in the Weekly Itch Score (1st key secondary endpoint) from baseline was observed in the linerixibat group compared with the placebo group as early as Week 2 (LS mean difference vs. placebo, -0.71; 95% CI: -1.07, -0.34; p&lt;0.001).

The linerixibat group showed a significant improvement in pruritus-related sleep interference (2nd key secondary endpoint) compared with the placebo group over 24 weeks (LS mean change from baseline, -2.77 vs. -2.24; LS mean difference vs. placebo, -0.53; 95% CI: -0.98, -0.07; p=0.024).

Responder Analysis (Monthly Itch Score) (3rd to 5th key secondary endpoints) showed a higher proportion of participants achieving itch improvement at Week 24 across responder definitions: ≥2point (68% vs 64%), ≥3-point (56% vs 43%), and ≥4-point (41% vs 29%). As the ≥2-point difference was not statistically significant testing was stopped.

Post hoc analyses of patients assessments (PGI-S and PGI-C) showed better improvement in itch severity and larger proportion of patients with clinically relevant improvement (\"very much improved\") reported on linerixibat in Part A:

- Proportion of subjects with absent or mild itch (PGI-S) increased from 10% to 66% on linerixibat and from 9% to 51% on placebo (20% vs. 10% with itch absent on linerixibat and placebo, respectively). Proportion of subjects with the remaining categories showed roughly similar changes on both treatment arms. E.g., \"severe\" and \"very severe\" itch decreased from 47% to 11% (from 11% to 4% for \"very severe\") on linerixibat and from 55% to 20% (from 12% to 5% for \"very severe\") on placebo.
- For PGI-C in part A (week 24) proportion of participant with highly clinically relevant response - \"very much improved\" - that is considered to reflect patients' satisfaction with received treatment most adequately (see above), was reported in almost half of the linerixibat-treated population and was higher than on placebo (47% vs. 34%). Combined frequency of \"very much improved\" and \"moderately improved\" cases also showed higher benefit on linerixibat (69% vs. 55%, respectively), while higher proportion of subjects had no change on placebo compared to linerixibat (30% vs. 17%, respectively).

Part B patterns (week 32 vs. week 24) for PGI-S and PGI-C showed changes consistent with the received treatment/changes in the received treatment indicating that removal of active treatment caused worsening of participants' condition, while start of treatment with linerixibat led to further improvement on top of the pronounced placebo effect:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- PGI-S: Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to clinically relevant return/increase of pruritus: the number of cases with \"absent\" and \"mild\" pruritus decreased (from 17% to 4% for \"absent\" and 54% to 34% for \"mild\"; cumulatively from 71% to 38%; difference: -33%), while the number of cases with \"very severe\", \"severe\", and \"moderate\" itch increased considerably (cumulatively from 28% to 62%, i.e., difference: 34%; from 2% to 15% for \"very severe itch\" and 2% to 11% for \"severe itch\").

Conversely, switch from placebo to linerixibat led to improvement of pruritus: proportion of participants reporting \"absent\" or \"mild\" itch increased from 48% to 65% (difference: 17%; 4% to 15% for \"absent\" and 44% to 50% for \"mild\"), while cumulative number of \"very severe\", \"severe\" and \"moderate\" itch decreased relevantly from 52% to 35% (difference: - 17%; including relevant decrease in \"very severe\" itch from 6% to 0%, and in \"severe\" itch from 19% to 8%).

- PGI-C: Withdrawal of linerixibat treatment led to decrease in the rate of positive assessments of the received treatment: proportion of subjects reporting \"very much improved\" category decreased from 57% to 41% (difference: -16%) and cumulative proportion of subjects with \"very much improved\" together with \"moderately improved\" decreased from 90% on linerixibat to 69% (difference: - 21%), while the proportion of subjects reporting no benefit, or worsening in their condition (categories \"very much worse\", \"moderately worse\", \"minimally worse\", and \"no change\") increased from 4% to 18% (difference: 14%).

Conversely, start of treatment with linerixibat in subjects on placebo led to increase in \"positive\" assessments (\"very much improved\" from 31% to 52%; difference: 21%; cumulatively with \"moderately improved\" from 60% to 77%; difference 17%), while number of patients reporting \"no change\" in their condition on placebo decreased from 29% to 4% (difference: -25%).

The effects on the pruritogens and PD-parameters (TSBA, autotaxin, IL-31, C4, and FGF-19) were small and showed small correlation with baseline itch severity and changes in itch, but consistent with the received treatment.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

Generally, the data package is of limited size which is however acceptable considering the rarity of the condition and does not preclude a favourable benefit-risk assessment.

Patients with progressed liver impairment and decompensation (decompensated cirrhosis) were excluded from the pivotal trial. As no data are available linerixibat is not recommended in these patients as adequately reflected in the section 4.4 of the SmPC.

The placebo-adjusted difference in itch (primary endpoint - WI-NRS) and other efficacy parameters (sleep interference, responder rates, quality of life, etc.) as well as changes in pruritogens (TSBA, autotaxin, IL-31) are considered small which raises some uncertainty considering the limited data package. However, clinically relevant patient-reported assessment of change (PGI-C) showed relevantly (by 13%-29%) higher number of subjects reporting their condition as \"very much improved\" on linerixibat and lower (-13% to -17%) number of subjects without change in their condition. Furthermore, the changes in pruritogens remained consistent with the received treatment. This gives sufficient assurance on the favourable effects of linerixibat.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

In the pooled safety dataset, 89 % of participants in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group and 73 % in the placebo group experienced at least one TEAE.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the pooled safety dataset, treatment discontinuations due to adverse events were more frequent in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group (13 %) compared with placebo (5 %). These differences were mainly driven by diarrhoea, abdominal pain, and elevations in AST/ALT.

A higher incidence of SAEs was observed in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group compared with placebo (10% vs 2%). In the OL study LLSAT, SAEs were reported in 13% of participants (10.50 per 100 patient-years). In the limited number of patients with cirrhosis, SAEs were only reported in the treatment group. Although no MedDRA PT was reported as an SAE in more than one participant in the studies and no consistent pattern was identified among the reported SAEs a potential imbalance between the groups warrants consideration.

Across all trials, the most common AEs were gastrointestinal, mainly diarrhoea (in the pooled safety data set 59 % vs 18 %) and abdominal pain (23 % vs. 9 %). The data indicate that a substantial proportion of diarrhoea events were not resolved during the observation period. A high proportion of patients required antidiarrheal medication, and resolution was not achieved in all of these cases.

An overall increase in mean change from baseline for ALT, ALP, AST and bilirubin, was observed in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group compared with the placebo group. Elevated ALT events meeting livermonitoring or stopping criteria occurred more frequently in linerixibat-treated participants than in those receiving placebo. In the pooled safety population, 3 cases of hepatoxicity were adjudicated by the hepatic panel as possibly related in the linerixibat group and 1 in the placebo group. Additionally, 2 cases of hepatoxicity were reported in the open label LLSAT study possibly related to study treatment.

Interference with bile acid circulation might impair the absorption of fat-soluble vitamins (FSVs). Based on the provided data, an effect of linerixibat on FSV absorption cannot be excluded in individual cases.

Subgroup analysis of the safety profile for various intrinsic and extrinsic factors was conducted. From the limited data, no notable differences were observed for any of the subgroups based on age, sex, race, and ethnicity.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The available data on patients ≥ 65 years of age are limited, particularly in the very elderly, as only 3 participants aged &gt;75 years were included in the blinded studies. Consequently, the safety profile in patients aged &gt;65 years cannot be fully characterised and is followed up post authorisation as missing information in the risk management plan. This limitation is also reflected in section 4.2 of the SmPC.

An overall increase in mean change from baseline for ALT, ALP, AST and bilirubin, was observed in the linerixibat 40 mg BID group compared with the placebo group. The observed elevations were mostly transient or stabilised. Due to the higher incidence of these events hepatotoxicity is considered an important potential risk in the RMP. Uncertainties for authorisation will remain due to the overall low number of cases but more data will become available by means of the long-term safety study 212358 as described in the RMP.

No safety data are available for patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C). Whilst no dose adjustment is required for patients with mild to moderate (Child-Pugh A or B) hepatic impairment the patient's hepatic status should be assessed including whether the patient has liver decompensation or has had a decompensation event prior to initiation of treatment with linerixibat. Use of Lynavoy is not recommended in patients with severe hepatic impairment and liver decompensation. This has been reflected in sections 4.2 and 4.4 in the SmPC.

## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The efficacy package for linerixibat demonstrates a consistent but modest treatment effect in patients with cholestatic pruritus associated with PBC. Across Part A of the pivotal study, statistically favourable effects versus placebo were observed for the primary endpoint and multiple secondary efficacy measures related to itch severity. A pronounced placebo response resulted in a small placebo-adjusted treatment effect on the primary WI-NRS endpoint (approximately 0.7 points), raising uncertainty regarding the clinical relevance of the mean treatment difference.

However, despite the modest effect size on continuous itch scales, categorical patient-reported outcomes (PGI-S and PGI-C) demonstrated more pronounced differentiation from placebo. A higher proportion of linerixibat-treated participants reported absent or mild itch, clinically meaningful improvement, and lower rates of unchanged symptoms compared with placebo. These findings were generally maintained after conservative handling of missing data (which considered drop-out due to tolerability/safety issues) and are considered supportive of a clinically relevant treatment benefit.

The exploratory treatment-switch phase (Part B) further supports efficacy. Withdrawal of linerixibat was associated with worsening of pruritus-related outcomes, while initiation of linerixibat after placebo resulted in improvement across multiple patient-reported endpoints. Although the magnitude of these changes was moderate and interpretation is limited by the exploratory nature of the analyses, the directionality of effects was consistent with a treatment-related benefit.

Subgroup analyses suggest that treatment benefit is greatest in patients with severe baseline pruritus and/or substantial sleep impairment. Efficacy in patients with moderate baseline itch seems limited.

The robustness of the efficacy findings is supported by multiple sensitivity analyses addressing potential unblinding due to diarrhoea.

Mechanistic support from pruritogenic biomarkers was limited. Changes in TSBA, autotaxin and IL-31 were generally small and showed weak correlations with clinical outcomes but were consistent with the received treatment/changes in treatment. In contrast, pharmacodynamic markers (C4 and FGF-19) demonstrated changes consistent with IBAT inhibition and support the proposed mechanism of action.

Overall, the totality of the evidence demonstrates a consistent efficacy for linerixibat across multiple clinically relevant endpoints, including pruritus severity, patient global assessments, and sleep-related outcomes. Although the magnitude of the treatment effect was attenuated by a pronounced placebo response, treatment benefits were consistently observed across efficacy measures and were most apparent in patients with severe baseline pruritus, representing the population with the greatest unmet medical need.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Gastro-intestinal effects are a known class effect from IBAT inhibitors. Accordingly, gastrointestinal adverse events, primarily diarrhoea and abdominal pain, and elevations in transaminases were the most common ADRs with a relevant difference compared with placebo. The higher discontinuation rate in the 40 mg BID group due to diarrhoea, which was mostly mild and moderate in intensity, reflects the clinical impact of persistent gastrointestinal symptoms rather than life-threatening events. Frequent use of anti-diarrhoeal medication supports the need for symptom management in clinical practice. This side effect is described in the SmPC and considered clinically manageable.

Linerixibat seems to be associated with a higher risk of liver events. Three cases of DILI possibly related to study intervention were reported in patients treated with linerixibat. Increase of transaminases and ALP occur at a clearly higher frequency in the treatment arm compared with control. This is adequately reflected in the SmPC, and hepatotoxicity is followed up as important potential risk in the RMP.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

Based on the totality of evidence a clinically relevant treatment effect of linerixibat across patientrelevant domains, including pruritus severity, patient-perceived improvement, and sleep disturbance is supported. While the placebo-adjusted effect size on some continuous efficacy measures was modest, the consistency of findings across multiple endpoints, together with supportive results from treatmentswitch analyses and greater benefit observed in patients with severe baseline pruritus, provides sufficient evidence of clinically meaningful benefit.

Considering the favourable safety profile, the lack of significant safety concerns, and the substantial unmet medical need for effective antipruritic therapies in patients with PBC, particularly those with severe pruritus, the benefits of linerixibat are considered to outweigh its risks and the benefit-risk balance is therefore positive.

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

The benefit-risk balance of Lynavoy indicated for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC) is positive.
